The Economic Outlook is an annual publication released on the same 
day as the Annual Budget.
	
	
The 2027 edition is released on 9 October 2026.
	
The data and analysis appearing in the Economic Outlook 2027 are 
compiled by the Ministry of Finance staff at the time of publication. 
Every effort is made to ensure their timeliness, accuracy and 
completeness. When errors are discovered, correction and revisions 
are incorporated into the digital editions available from the Ministry 
of Finance website.

PREFACE
The global economy is entering 2027 amid 
profound change. Geopolitical tensions are 
reshaping trade, investment and energy 
security. Climate risks are becoming more 
immediate, while technological advances 
are transforming industries, jobs and the 
foundations of competitiveness. These 
forces are increasingly interconnected, 
creating both new challenges and new 
opportunities for Malaysia.
The experience of recent years has 
demonstrated the importance of 
resilience and adaptability. The pandemic, 
disruptions to global supply chains, 
heightened geopolitical tensions and rapid 
technological change have altered the 
environment in which economic policy 
is made. Malaysia must respond not by 
seeking a return to old assumptions, but 
by preparing confidently for the world now 
emerging.
The Ekonomi MADANI framework was 
conceived for this purpose: to address 
structural vulnerabilities and place 
Malaysia on a more sustainable and 
inclusive path. Its ambition is clear — to 
build an economy of greater value and 
complexity, founded on good governance, 
environmental sustainability and social 
justice. This is not merely an economic 
undertaking. It is also a commitment to 
restore dignity, widen opportunity and 
ensure that progress is shared.
MINISTER OF FINANCE MALAYSIA
economic outlook   2027
iii

The results thus far have been encouraging. Economic growth exceeded expectations in 
both 2024 and 2025. This momentum continued into 2026, with the economy expanding 
by 5.7% in the first half of the year. Full-year growth is now expected to be at the upper 
end of the projected range of 4.8% to 5.3%.
Yet headline growth cannot be the sole measure of our progress. The greater imperative 
is to translate stronger investment and economic expansion into higher incomes, better 
jobs and wider opportunities for Malaysians, particularly our youth. Investments secured 
today must strengthen productive capacity, deepen domestic capabilities and create high-
value opportunities for the future.
Our approach is grounded in sound economic principles and empirical evidence, but 
policy cannot be shaped by models and statistics alone. It must also respond to the lived 
realities of the rakyat, their aspirations, anxieties and experiences of the economy in 
their daily lives.
Raising the Ceiling of Malaysia’s economic potential must ultimately serve to Raise the 
Floor of the rakyat’s living standards. Both must rest on the firm foundation of good 
governance.
Progress cannot be judged by economic statistics alone if its benefits are not broadly 
shared. Fiscal reforms have enabled the Government to direct resources more effectively 
towards those who need them. Sumbangan Tunai Rahmah, Sumbangan Asas Rahmah and 
BUDI MADANI continue to provide essential support to households, particularly as global 
energy prices and supply disruptions place renewed pressure on the cost of living.
Social protection, however, cannot substitute for productive opportunity. Our 
responsibility is to provide security and assistance where they are needed today, while 
creating the conditions for higher incomes, better employment and greater social 
mobility tomorrow.
Our efforts to Raise the Ceiling are also producing results. Malaysia climbed 19 places 
in two years to rank 15th in the IMD World Competitiveness Ranking 2026, its highest 
position in more than a decade. This progress reflects the strength of our economic 
fundamentals and the growing confidence in Malaysia’s direction.
There remains much more to do. Malaysia must move decisively towards higher-value 
manufacturing, accelerate the energy transition and strengthen its capabilities in artificial 
intelligence, robotics and other emerging technologies. These are among the forces 
reshaping global investment, productivity and competitiveness.
Technological and economic disruption cannot be avoided, but it can and must be 
managed responsibly. The Government’s task is to equip Malaysians with the skills and 
opportunities to benefit from change, assist workers and industries through periods of 
transition, and ensure that technological progress contributes to wider prosperity rather 
than deeper inequality.
economic outlook   2027
iv

These efforts are expected to support economic growth of between 4.2% and 5.2% in 
2027. The significance of this outlook, however, lies not only in the headline number. It 
lies in whether growth delivers stronger productivity, better wages, quality employment 
and a more secure future for Malaysians.
The year ahead will remain challenging. Geopolitical fragmentation, climate risks and 
rapid technological change will continue to reshape the global economic landscape. 
Malaysia must therefore remain agile, strengthen its resilience and pursue reform with 
clarity and purpose.
Our task is not to recreate the economy of the past, but to build one capable of 
succeeding in the world now emerging — an economy that is more productive and 
competitive, yet also more inclusive, sustainable and humane.
Insya-Allah, with sound policies, effective execution and the continued resolve of our 
people, Malaysia will enter 2027 from a position of growing strength and confidence.
I wish to record my sincere appreciation to all those who have contributed, directly and 
indirectly, to the preparation of this Report.
ANWAR IBRAHIM
9 October 2026

chapter 1   economic management and prospects
economic outlook   2027
3
chapter 1
Economic Management and Prospects
Malaysia Madani: 
Reaching for the Skies, 
While Anchored on Our 
Values
Having demonstrated remarkable resilience 
in weathering a series of global shocks over 
the past three years, Malaysia is delivering 
tangible results as shown by the progress of 
various initiatives under the Ekonomi MADANI 
framework. The ongoing Russia-Ukraine conflict 
fuelled energy and food price volatility, while 
uncertainties surrounding the US trade policies, 
particularly the imposition of tariffs, created 
challenges for export-oriented economies. 
The conflict in West Asia has heightened 
geopolitical risks and disrupted global supply 
chains. Despite these pressures, Malaysia 
has endured through prudent economic 
management, strategic reforms, diversified 
trade relationships, strong domestic demand 
and greater adaptability among businesses. 
This resilience reflects the nation’s ability to 
absorb repeated external shocks, maintain 
stability and continue pursuing sustainable 
growth, while safeguarding household 
well-being, strengthening businesses and 
preserving fiscal space to respond to emerging 
challenges.
The Government continues to pursue strategic 
measures under Budget 2026, aimed at 
channelling resources to fortify governance 
and fiscal discipline, drive a high-value resilient 
economy, ensure inclusive and equitable 
growth as well as return economic gains 
directly to the rakyat. The Budget focuses on 
nine core measures, namely, honing excellence 
in governance; serving the rakyat’s priorities; 
championing high-value economy; driving 
‘Made by Malaysia’ products; strengthening 
national resilience; bridging the gap, creating 
opportunities; ensuring continuity of the 
rakyat’s livelihood; empowering services 
for the rakyat; and building self-identity, 
developing human values. By emphasising on 
‘Raising the Ceiling’ and ‘Raising the Floor’, the 
Government will continue to mitigate external 
risks and support sustainable growth through 
targeted policy enhancements and institutional 
governance.
Key Performance 
Indicators of the 
Ekonomi MADANI 
Framework
The Government remains committed in 
advancing the seven key performance 
indicators (KPIs) under the Ekonomi MADANI 
framework, which serves as medium-
term benchmarks for Malaysia’s economic 
transformation. The KPIs cover economic 
strength and competitiveness, human 
capital development, female labour market 
participation, labour income, governance and 
fiscal sustainability. As shown in Figure 1.1, 
Malaysia is making steady gains across the 
Ekonomi MADANI KPIs, reflecting the ongoing 
efforts to strengthen economic resilience while 
advancing inclusive, sustainable and high-value 
growth.

chapter 1   economic management and prospects
economic outlook   2027
4
Malaysia’s economic size is currently ranked 
33rd globally, moving closer towards the 
aspiration of becoming one of the top 30 
largest economies. This progress reflects 
continued growth momentum, supported by 
strong domestic demand and robust external 
trade. Sustained development of high growth 
high value (HGHV) and strategic industries, 
supported by investments in human capital 
development, infrastructures and digital 
technologies, will further strengthen Malaysia’s 
economic capacity.
Similarly, Malaysia’s competitiveness 
strengthened markedly, catapulting eight 
spots to 15th among 70 economies in the 
2026 International Institute for Management 
Development (IMD) World Competitiveness 
Ranking. This is Malaysia’s best rank in recent 
years, bringing the country closer to the 
aspiration of being among the top 12 globally. 
This strong performance reflects broad-based 
improvements in economic performance as 
well as government and business efficiency. 
Continued efforts will be undertaken 
to strengthen infrastructure, streamline 
regulations, enhance digital readiness and 
bolster Malaysia’s competitiveness  
globally.
In efforts to balance the rakyat’s well-being 
and economic development, Malaysia is 
working continuously at improving education, 
health and decent standard of living. In this 
regard, Malaysia aims to raise its Human 
Development Index (HDI) ranking from 67th 
to top 25 globally. The Ekonomi MADANI 
framework also focuses on better quality of 
life for households by encouraging greater 
participation of women in the workforce to 
enhance household incomes and economic 
security. Consequently, the female labour force 
participation improved to 56.6%, remaining on 
track to achieve the target of 60%.
Meanwhile, raising the labour income share 
to GDP remains an important priority under 
the Framework. In 2025, the compensation of 
employees (CE) stood at 33.9%, with ongoing 
efforts focused on strengthening wage growth 
towards achieving the 45% CE target. In 
narrowing this gap, the Government aims 
to enhance productivity, strengthen wage-
setting mechanisms and promote high-paying 
employment.
On governance, Malaysia improved three places 
to 54th position out of 182 countries, with a 
score of 52 in the Corruption Perception Index

5
(CPI) 2026. In advancing towards a top 25 
position, the National Anti-Corruption Strategy 
2024 — 2028 (NACS) prioritises improvements 
in public sector governance, transparency 
and accountability. Ongoing efforts include 
strengthening public procurement practices, 
enhancing oversight mechanisms and 
improving integrity management across 
government institutions. At the same time, 
Malaysia is expected to narrow its fiscal deficit 
to 3.6% of GDP in 2026, moving closer to the 
target of 3% or better. Continued fiscal reforms 
to strengthen revenue mobilisation, optimise 
expenditure and improve subsidy targeting will 
further support economic growth and the well-
being of the rakyat.
Overall, the seven KPIs’ achievements reflect 
Malaysia’s continued progress towards the 
aspirations of the Ekonomi MADANI framework. 
The Government is committed to translate 
ongoing structural reforms into measurable 
economic and social outcomes, supported by 
the implementation of the Thirteenth Malaysia 
Plan, 2026 — 2030 (Thirteenth Plan). 
Economic Outlook
Global Economy 
Global economy is projected to grow 
moderately at 3% in 2026 and 3.4% in 2027 
(IMF, 2026), mainly supported by accelerated 
demand-driven momentum in the global 
technology sector resulting from advances 
in artificial intelligence (AI). The outlook is 
uneven as the conflict in West Asia continues 
to weigh on energy importers and vulnerable 
economies, while AI-driven demand will elevate 
countries that are integrated in the global 
technology value chain. Growth in advanced 
economies is expected to expand modestly 
while emerging markets and developing 
economies (EMDEs) will continue to be the 
major driver of global economic activities. The 
expansion is also supported by easing inflation 
and gradually less restrictive monetary policy, 
providing added impetus to investment and 
trade activities. Global trade continues to face 
increasing fragmentation as inward-looking 
policies especially through higher tariffs weigh 
on cross-border economic activities. As a 
trading nation, it is imperative for Malaysia 
to sustain economic growth by continuing 
to diversify export markets, strengthen 
regional integration and enhance supply chain 
resilience, while preserving macroeconomic and 
financial stability.
Domestic Economy
In 2026, the economy is expected to grow 
between 4.8% and 5.3%, underpinned by 
resilient domestic demand amid a challenging 
external environment. Domestic demand will 
remain the key driver, supported by sustained 
private consumption, favourable labour market 
conditions and continued consumer confidence. 
Meanwhile, investment activity is expected to 
strengthen on the back of strong private sector 
spending and the continued implementation of 
strategic projects. On the supply side, growth 
will be led by the services and manufacturing 
sectors. The services sector will benefit from 
sustained tourism-related activities, steady 
trade flows alongside adoption of digital 
technology, while the manufacturing sector 
will be bolstered by stronger demand for E&E 
products and continued expansion in high-
value activities.
Malaysia’s economy is projected to expand 
between 4.2% and 5.2% in 2027, supported 
by sustained domestic demand, favourable 
labour market conditions, continued income 
growth and firm investment activity. Private 
consumption will remain a key growth driver, 
supported by sustained household purchasing 
power and continued expansion in tourism, 
retail, transportation and other services in 
conjunction with Visit Malaysia 2026 — 2027. 
Investment activity is expected to remain 
robust, underpinned by the realisation of 
approved investments and strategic initiatives 
under the Thirteenth Plan, New Industrial 

chapter 1   economic management and prospects
economic outlook   2027
6
Master Plan 2030 (NIMP 2030), National 
Semiconductor Strategy (NSS) and National 
Energy Transition Roadmap (NETR). The 
services sector will continue to be the primary 
growth driver, while the manufacturing sector 
will benefit from the technology upcycle and 
expansion of high-value activities, particularly 
in semiconductors and E&E industries. 
These developments, together with stronger 
domestic linkages and productivity-enhancing 
investments, are expected to strengthen 
Malaysia’s productive capacity and support 
more balanced, inclusive and sustainable 
growth. The outlook remains subject to 
external risks, including geopolitical tensions, 
shifts in international trade policies and global 
financial market volatility.
Monetary and Financial 
Developments
Monetary policy in 2026 remains supportive of 
sustainable economic growth while maintaining 
price stability amid global uncertainties. The 
Overnight Policy Rate (OPR) was maintained 
at 2.75%, remaining unchanged since July 
2025. The banking system continues to be 
well capitalised, with sound asset quality and 
ample liquidity buffers. The ringgit remained 
broadly stable against currencies of major 
trading partners, supported by Malaysia’s 
strong economic fundamentals and sustained 
growth momentum. Looking ahead, financial 
intermediation will continue to play an 
important role in supporting households and 
businesses, complemented by well-functioning 
markets and continued policy efforts to 
strengthen financial inclusion and sustainable 
financing. The Financial Sector Blueprint 2027 
— 2030 and Capital Market Masterplan 2026 
— 2030 will guide the next phase of financial 
sector development with continued reforms, 
broader investor participation and diverse 
funding sources, further strengthening the 
financial system.
Progress of Budget 2026
Budget 2026 remains anchored on three 
priorities: Strengthening Administration and 
Governance, 'Raising the Ceiling' to accelerate 
economic restructuring, and 'Raising the 
Floor' to enhance socioeconomic well-being. 
In support of these priorities, the Government 
allocated RM419.2 billion under Budget 
2026, which consists of RM338.2 billion for 
Operating Expenditure (OE) and RM81 billion 
for Development Expenditure (DE).
Measure One: Honing 
Excellence in Governance
Implementation of Budget 2026 measures 
continue to strengthen governance and 
institutional capacity, with programmes centred 
on enhancing fiscal responsibility and public 
finance. These measures will further improve 
the delivery of public services and strengthen 
the country’s institutional pillars.
Efforts to strengthen fiscal governance are 
focused on enhancing tax administration, 
regulatory compliance and enforcement 
capacity. The continued implementation of 
e-Invoices represented a key component 
of the Government’s digitalisation of tax 
administration. As at end-August 2026, over 
265,000 taxpayers submitted e-Invoices, 
bringing the cumulative issuance of e-Invoices 
to more than 1.8 billion since its inception. 
Public sector transformation continues to adopt 
digital government services widely to further 
enhance public service delivery. Notably, the 
Special Task Force on Agency Reform (STAR) 
continues to address bureaucratic processes 
and improve service delivery, while MyDigital 
ID and GovTech Malaysia provide the digital 
infrastructure to facilitate seamless access 
to government services. As of September 

chapter 1   economic management and prospects
economic outlook   2027
7
2026, MyDigital ID recorded 14 million 
registered users, while 203 Federal and 
state governments as well as private-sector 
online services, had been integrated with 
the platform. The MyGOV Malaysia platform 
similarly expanded its reach, recording more 
than three million users as of August 2026 
and providing access to 63 services across 
25 government agencies and 91 local  
authorities.
Measures were also undertaken to reinforce 
institutional effectiveness and participatory 
governance, particularly through Parliament 
and cross-party engagement. Budget 2026 
allocated RM20 million to strengthen the 
functions of Parliamentary Special Select 
Committees and for community development 
programmes under the All-Party Parliamentary 
Group Malaysia (APPGM). Parliamentary 
oversight was further strengthened through 
greater visibility of the Special Select 
Committees’ work.
Measure Two: Serving the 
Rakyat’s Priorities
Subsidy rationalisation, especially through 
the BUDI MADANI programme, is necessary 
to build a stronger fiscal foundation and 
ensure continuity of economic activities, while 
safeguarding vulnerable households from the 
rising cost of living. Building on the successful 
rollout of BUDI MADANI RON95 (BUDI95) 
in Peninsular Malaysia, the programme 
was further expanded nationwide with the 
integration of BUDI95 and BUDI MADANI Diesel 
(BUDI Diesel) from 1 July 2026. The enhanced 
mechanism enables eligible MyKad holders 
to purchase RON95 and diesel at subsidised 
prices. As at end-August 2026, more than 
14.8 billion litres of RON95 were purchased 
at a subsidised price amounting to a subsidy 
disbursement of RM18 billion. Meanwhile, over 
130 million litres of diesel were consumed 
amounting to approximately RM295 million in 
subsidies.
The Ikhtiar MADANI Untuk Rakyat is a 
programme to expand community-based 
initiatives and improve socioeconomic 
outcomes at the grassroots level. This 
initiative includes the expansion of Sejahtera 
Komuniti MADANI (SejaTi MADANI), Kampung 
Angkat MADANI, Sekolah Angkat MADANI and 
Solar MADANI, reflecting the Government’s 
continued commitment to community-driven 
development. Under SejaTi MADANI, RM8.5 
million in grants were approved for 89 
communities to undertake income-generating 
activities, including agriculture and small-
scale enterprises, with the initiative expanded 
to the youth. Meanwhile, 145 Kampung 
Angkat MADANI projects and 151 Sekolah 
Angkat MADANI initiatives were approved, 
alongside the nationwide expansion of Solar 
MADANI and the introduction of free Tuisyen 
MADANI. These initiatives are complemented 
by Sejahtera MADANI, which mobilises private 
sector contributions through the Tabung 
Kesejahteraan Rakyat to support community 
development. As at end-August 2026, the 
programme managed to raise nearly RM78 
million from local corporations.
In delivering government services directly 
to rural and remote communities, the 
Government introduced Ikhtiar Perkhidmatan 
Bergerak MADANI as a targeted mobile 
outreach programme spanning financial, 
education, administrative and legal services. 
A total of RM11 million was channelled to the 
Mobile Banking Initiative, deploying 36 units 
across eight states, including 23 in Sabah 
and Sarawak. Additionally, RM2.6 million was 
allocated to expand the National Registration 
Department’s Perkhidmatan Menyemai Kasih 
Rakyat (MEKAR) programme, with two mobile 
bonded trucks serving rural communities, 
senior citizens and persons with disabilities 
(PWDs). 
Complementing these initiatives, RM27 
million was allocated for community 
outreach involving mobile clinics, community 
rehabilitation services and lifelong learning 

chapter 1   economic management and prospects
economic outlook   2027
8
programmes under the Ministry of Higher 
Education. As of July 2026, 49 mobile clinics 
were operated by 10 institutions, benefitting 
more than 5,700 recipients especially in rural 
areas, while 197 lifelong learning courses had 
enrolled over 18,000 participants. In addition, 
mobile infrastructure was also provided 
in the form of Mobile Labour Courts and 
Mobile Children’s Court nationwide to resolve 
workplace disputes and better safeguard the 
privacy and emotional well-being of young 
victims and witnesses.
In accelerating development in Sabah and 
Sarawak, the Government continued to 
support progress in major transport, energy 
and water infrastructure projects. The RM24.9 
billion (Phase 1A – RM10.9 billion, Phase 1B 
– RM14 billion) Pan Borneo Highway Sabah 
continued to advance, with Phase 1A reaching 
89% completion, and Phase 1B at 37.6% as at 
end-August 2026.  Phase 1A is targeted for 
completion in 2028 and Phase 1B in 2030.
Meanwhile, the Pan Borneo Highway Sarawak 
segment which consists of 740km has achieved 
a 99.9% completion as at end-August 2026. 
In addition, the RM8.8 billion Sarawak-
Sabah Link Road (SSLR) linking both states 
without crossing international borders is also 
progressing in phases, with Phase 1 targeted 
for completion in 2027 and Phase 2 in 2030.
Energy infrastructure projects are also 
progressing with RM1.9 billion allocated 
for 18 projects. These projects will enhance 
the capacity and reliability of electricity 
transmission and distribution systems in Sabah 
and Labuan. As of August 2026, two projects 
have been completed, while the rest are still 
work in progress.
Measure Three: 
Championing High-Value 
Economy
Budget 2026 advances Malaysia’s ambition to 
become a high-value economy by attracting 
quality investment, strengthening regional 
linkages, developing local talents and 
expanding the Islamic economy. Under the 
New Incentive Framework introduced on 1 
March 2026, investment policies are now linked 
to job quality, technology transfer, domestic 
supply chains and sustainability. During the 
first half of 2026, approved investments 
increased to RM218.5 billion across more than 
2,700 projects, with the potential to create 
about 99,000 jobs. The manufacturing sector 
received a substantial share of investment, 
particularly in the E&E subsector which 
accounted for RM16.6 billion. Meanwhile, the 
number of approved manufacturing projects 
rose to 88.2%, reflecting the sector’s vibrancy.
Greater emphasis was also given to support 
emerging businesses through the Malaysia 
Co-Investment Fund (MyCIF) by expanding the 
Food Security Scheme to agri-tech startups. 
New initiatives were introduced, namely the 
Venture Capital/Private Equity (VC/PE) Profit-
Sharing Incentive and the Silver Economy 
Scheme, which became operational in March 
2026. From January to July 2026, MyCIF 
recorded RM183 million in co-investments, 
with two upstream agribusiness deals were 
approved under the enhanced Food Security 
Scheme. The VC/PE Profit-Sharing Incentive and 
Silver Economy Scheme are still at the early 
stages of implementation. 
In strengthening the startup ecosystem, the 
Government facilitated the growth of startup 
companies in high-value economy through 
various programmes including Dana Perintis 
and Jelawang Capital Khazanah, as well as 
funds under Cradle Fund programmes such as 
Bengkel Inovasi GLC 2.0 (BIG 2.0). As at end-
August 2026, out of the RM6.9 million allocated 
to BIG 2.0, around 88% has been disbursed to 
approved companies. 
In addition, cross-border competitiveness was 
strengthened through the Johor-Singapore 
Special Economic Zone (JS-SEZ), whereby 
Johor attracted RM55.6 billion in approved 
investments during the first half of 2026. The 
Micron-OCBC JS-SEZ Supplier Event connected 

chapter 1   economic management and prospects
economic outlook   2027
9
30 major global technology companies with 
Malaysian suppliers, supporting qualification, 
localisation and long-term partnerships. 
Investor procedures were also simplified 
through the MIDA Expatriate System (MES), 
which combines expatriate applications, 
approvals and epass endorsements acting as 
the sole submission platform for Employment 
Pass (EP), Professional Visit Pass (PVP) and 
Dependent Pass (DP) for the manufacturing 
and selected services sectors. Furthermore, 
the Investor Pass was enhanced to facilitate 
business travellers through the issuance of 
Multiple Entry Visas (MEVs). As at end-August 
2026, MIDA supported over 180 investors 
across a diverse range of sectors, including 
manufacturing, construction, ICT and  
tourism.
Talent development continued alongside 
investment expansion. By July 2026, Human 
Resource Development Corporation (HRD 
Corp) approved close to 1.5 million training 
slots and more than RM1.3 million in financial 
assistance. GIATMARA enrolled over 3,800 new 
trainees and TVET@PerdaTech involving around 
1,000 students, achieving their annual intake 
targets. More than 500 students completed 
32 TVET Tahfiz micro-credential courses, with 
the highest demand recorded in sewing and 
apparel followed by computer and information 
technology, catering and food services, 
business and entrepreneurship as well  
as E&E.
The 1:3 Internship Policy further links 
expatriate recruitment to local talent 
development. This policy requires participating 
employers to provide up to three paid 
internships for each expatriate hired under 
the National Structured Internship Programme, 
with full implementation starting from June 
2026. Meanwhile, Malaysia also strengthened 
its Islamic-economy ecosystem among others, 
by increasing halal certification. As at end-
August 2026, more than 1,200 certification 
applications were processed through additional 
appointment of 78 contract halal auditors.
Measure Four: Driving ‘Made 
by Malaysia’ Products
Budget 2026 continues to strengthen 
Malaysia’s capacity to develop, commercialise 
and scale up locally created products and 
technologies, while expanding opportunities 
for domestic firms to compete in regional 
and global markets. In driving innovation 
locally, the Government spearheaded initiatives 
focusing on strengthening AI capabilities, 
accelerating research commercialisation and 
developing a steady stream of talents equipped 
with strong science and technology credentials. 
In facilitating this effort, the National AI Office 
(NAIO) was institutionalised as AI Malaysia 
Berhad as at end-July 2026 under the Ministry 
of Digital. It was launched together with the 
National AI Action Plan 2026 — 2030 and 
the Malaysia AI Safety Institute, to further 
strengthen the implementation of the national 
AI agenda and support the country’s transition 
towards a higher value-added digital economy.
While Malaysia is promoting the 'Made 
by Malaysia' products and services by 
focusing more on research, development, 
commercialisation and innovation (R&D&C&I), 
these efforts are also expected to enhance 
product competitiveness, unlock new export 
opportunities and expand access to a wider 
range of international markets. In this effort, 
the Government continues to promote export 
development measures, particularly for micro, 
small and medium enterprises (MSMEs) under 
the Penggalakan Pasaran dan Pembangunan 
Eksport (PPPE) programme, which includes 
Market Development Grant (MDG) and 
Malaysia Go Global (MGG) to facilitate wider 
market access for Malaysian firms. As of June 
2026, the PPPE recorded more than 1,200 
participants, comprising over 930 under MDG 
and 280 under MGG. Meanwhile, during the 
same period, more than 1,100 participants 
attended various exporter training programmes 
organised by Malaysia External Trade 
Development Corporation (MATRADE).

Measure Five: Strengthening 
National Resilience
The Government continues to advance the 
energy transition through the National Energy 
Transition Fund (NETF), implemented by 
Malaysia Debt Ventures Berhad (MDV) and 
Bank Pembangunan Malaysia Berhad (BPMB). 
From 2024 to 2026, a cumulative of RM350 
million was approved, with RM175 million 
channelled to each institution. As at end-
August 2026, over RM270 million in NETF 
funding had unlocked RM1.7 billion from the 
private sector for 30 energy transition projects. 
Under Budget 2026, RM150 million was 
channelled to MDV and BPMB, of which RM77.6 
million has been approved, securing more 
than RM530 million from investors in blended 
financing for eight projects. 
Disaster preparedness and environmental 
resilience continue to be strengthened through 
flood mitigation and river conservation 
projects. Of the RM1.3 billion allocated for 
the Flood Mitigation Plan, RM942 million was 
disbursed for 43 high-priority and 12 new 
projects nationwide. These initiatives were 
complemented by RM189.9 million spent in 
2026 under the broader RM282 million Water 
Sector Transformation 2040 programme 
covering river conservation, infrastructure 
maintenance, water treatment as well as flood 
forecasting and early warning systems.
The Government has launched the Paddy 
Price Subsidy Scheme and Paddy Crop Takaful 
Scheme to strengthen national food security. 
These efforts encompass key initiatives such 
as the Paddy Production Incentive Scheme 
(SIPP) to subsidise ploughing, pest control and 
harvesting as well as the Soil Fertility Recovery 
Programme to optimise soil health and crop 
yields through bio-fertiliser applications. In 
2026, RM688 million and RM20 million was 
allocated to SIPP and Soil Fertility Recovery 
Programme, respectively, benefitting around 
197,000 recipients nationwide. Additionally, 
RM178 million was allocated for the Fishermen 
Subsidies and Incentives Programme enhancing 
fishermen’s livelihood through monthly living 
allowances and catch incentives, while landing 
infrastructure was improved through facility 
upgrades and emergency disaster relief with 
an allocation of RM5.1 million.
Meanwhile, in raising the country’s national 
defence capabilities, RM5.3 billion was 
allocated for the procurement, maintenance 
and repair of military assets to enhance 
the operational readiness of the Malaysian 
Armed Forces. In addition, the Royal Military 
Police procured new assets to strengthen 
intelligence operations, marine patrol and 
maritime surveillance involving an allocation 
of RM1 billion. Furthermore, a total of RM1.2 
billion was allocated to construct and upgrade 
facilities to improve the level of security in 
border areas. 
Measure Six: Bridging the 
Gap, Creating Opportunities
In the effort to reduce the regional 
development divide and ensure equitable 
access to essential utilities, the Government 
committed RM3.3 billion for rural basic 
infrastructure spanning roads, water, electricity 
and bridges across Malaysia. Of this, RM22.8 
million and RM70 million was allocated to 
supply water and electricity, respectively, 
in Sabah and Sarawak. To enhance water 
sector resilience, RM2.5 billion was allocated 
for the Non-Revenue Water (NRW) reduction 
programme under Budget 2026. Of this, RM824 
million was for the management, prevention, 
detection and solution works in Kelantan, 
Labuan, Pahang, Perlis, Sabah and Sarawak. 
Additionally, RM1.7 billion was allocated for 
the replacement of critical pipes nationwide. 
Meanwhile, in Selangor, Kuala Lumpur and 
Putrajaya, Pengurusan Aset Air Berhad 
allocated RM13 billion over the same period 
to upgrade water infrastructure for 1.5 million 
residents. Complementing these efforts, a

11
RM5 million hybrid desalination initiative was 
introduced for ESSZONE security posts and 
remote islands in Sabah, with construction 
tenders awarded for Pulau Banggi, Pulau 
Mabul and Pulau Berhala.
In expanding digital infrastructure and high-
value technical capacity, the Government 
continues accelerating high-speed internet 
penetration in underserved regions. The 
Point of Presence (PoP) Phase 2 project aims 
at expanding fibre optic connectivity near 
industrial parks and rural schools through 
more than 3,000 network access points, has 
achieved a 95% progress rate with expenditure 
exceeding RM497 million as of July 2026.  
The Bumiputera economy continues to be 
strengthened with RM6 billion allocated 
through Majlis Amanah Rakyat (MARA), Yayasan 
Peneraju and Universiti Teknologi Mara (UiTM) 
for Bumiputera students and entrepreneurs, 
alongside RM10 billion in Syarikat Jaminan 
Pembiayaan Perniagaan Berhad (SJPP) credit 
guarantees and RM2.4 billion for G1–G4 
Bumiputera contractors. Under the GEAR-
uP initiative, GLICs and GLCs are expected 
to coordinate further interventions spanning 
entrepreneurship, procurement, growth capital 
and market access to support high-potential 
enterprises in scaling their businesses. In 
building industry leaders among Bumiputera 
companies, the 10 Bumiputera Champions 
Programme has shortlisted nine high-
performing Bumiputera companies towards an 
IPO-readiness target of RM500 million market 
valuation each. 
In addition, the Government, through SME 
Corp. Malaysia’s Tunas Usahawan Belia 
Bumiputera Skala (TUBE-S) programme, is 
actively fostering a new generation of resilient 
youth entrepreneurs with the aim of scaling 
up their businesses and creating sustainable 
employment opportunities. As at end-August 
2026, 486 youth entrepreneurs have completed 
the intensive incubator programme nationwide 
and secured RM7.3 million in matching 
business grants of up to RM15,000 each. 
Additionally, 50 MSMEs received approvals for 
a RM2.7 million in TUNAS-i soft loans capped 
at RM100,000 per business with interest rates 
of up to 2% per annum. To ensure long-term 
business growth and operational sustainability, 
beneficiaries who have completed the 
programme will receive structured coaching, 
guidance and continuous performance 
monitoring for 12 months.
In efforts to strengthen the social mobility of 
vulnerable groups, particularly women and 
children, several initiatives are being advanced 
by the Government. For women MSMEs, RM5 
million in financing facilities was disbursed 
under the Mekar Dinamik programme, an 
initiative under Yayasan Kebajikan Negara 
(YKN) in collaboration with Malaysian Prison 
Department. This initiative provides business 
kits and skills training for formerly incarcerated 
women and female primary breadwinners. 
This programme includes female clients of the 
Pusat Pemulihan Penagihan Narkotik (PUSPEN), 
providing a second chance, particularly for 
clients without family support. From January 
to August 2026, the programme has assisted 
more than 1,680 participants. 
In safeguarding vulnerable communities 
and embodying the commitment to leave 
no one behind, comprehensive interventions 
are directed towards Orang Asli populations 
and PWDs. Orang Asli community 
development continues to be supported 
through an allocation of RM155 million via 
Jabatan Kemajuan Orang Asli (JAKOA) with 
the development of rural roads, TABIKA 
kindergarten upgrades and Program Khas 
Pendidikan Manusiawi (ProKhas) Sekolah 
Terapung in Hulu Perak and Kalabakan, Sabah. 
For PWDs’ welfare, backed by more than 
RM1.2 billion in direct assistance, RM9.6 million 
was allocated to establish Autism Service 
Centres (PPA) in Labuan, Sabah and Sarawak. 
This early intervention facilitates access to 
therapy and support services for 5,500 autistic 
children as at end-August 2026.

Measure Seven: Ensuring 
Continuity of The Rakyat’s 
Livelihood
In cushioning Malaysian households against 
cost-of-living pressures, the Government 
allocated RM15 billion under Sumbangan Tunai 
Rahmah (STR) and Sumbangan Asas Rahmah 
(SARA) in 2026. As an immediate relief ahead 
of festive celebrations in the first quarter of 
2026, a total of RM1.9 billion was allocated 
under the Penghargaan SARA initiative for 
20.2 million Malaysian citizens aged 18 and 
above with a one-off RM100 cashless financial 
credit channelled through MyKad. In efforts 
to strengthen the purchasing power of the 
rakyat, the Government continues to expand 
the Payung Rahmah Programme across urban 
and remote regions. More than RM600 million 
was disbursed for the Jualan Rahmah MADANI 
initiative across 23,040 locations to offer up 
to 30% discounts on daily essential goods. 
For rural communities, more than RM250 
million was utilised for the transportation of 
basic goods distribution across inland Sabah, 
Sarawak and Peninsular Malaysia.
The Government continued to extend social 
protection to the self-employed workers 
including gig workers through contribution 
matching schemes under the Employees 
Provident Fund (EPF) and Social Security 
Organisation (PERKESO). Under the EPF, the 
i-Saraan, i-Saraan Plus and i-Suri schemes 
provide matching incentives for eligible 
contributors, including self-employed workers, 
full-time e-hailing and p-hailing drivers, 
housewives and single mothers. Meanwhile, 
under PERKESO, the Skim Keselamatan Sosial 
Pekerjaan Sendiri (SKSPS) subsidised up to 
70% of contributions for eligible self-employed 
workers. As of August 2026, SKSPS recorded 
over 59,000 registrations, with about RM7.8 
million channelled by the Government.
In strengthening community livelihoods and 
well-being, the Government allocated RM100 
million under Budget 2026 to encourage 
GLICs and GLCs, through their philanthropic 
arms, to collaborate on social impact 
initiatives such as addressing urban poverty 
and strengthening specialised rehabilitation 
services. The grant adopts a 1:1 matching 
arrangement, supporting humanitarian, 
education and community well-being 
initiatives. As of September 2026, 37 high-
impact projects have been awarded grants 
to undertake social programmes. Through 
the GDRN platform, these organisations also 
support the Government in disaster response, 
complemented by an additional RM20 million 
GDRN BANTU Matching Grant to strengthen 
disaster resilience. In addition, development 
planning is progressing for the new 250-bed 
PERKESO Rehabilitation Centre in Penang. The 
centre will provide comprehensive medical 
and physical rehabilitation services for injured 
workers.
In efforts to prepare for an ageing population 
and strengthen standards within the care 
economy, the Government is developing the 
National Care Sector Standards to improve 
care standards, workforce capabilities 
and community-based support. Under the 
Malaysia Technical and Vocational Education 
and Training (MTVET) care worker training 
modules were completed in August 2026, 
benefitting 745 participants. Meanwhile, the 
Ritma Kehidupan Harmoni (RYTHM) initiative 
mobilised youth volunteers to Senior Citizens 
Activity Centres (PAWE) and Community-Based 
Rehabilitation Centres (PDK) nationwide in 
supporting 140 programmes for elderly health 
as well as therapy for PWDs, benefitting more 
than 6,500 beneficiaries.
Measure Eight: Empowering 
Services for The Rakyat
Budget 2026 strengthens essential public 
services through measures covering 
health, education, mobility and housing. 
Implementation has progressed at different 
rates, ranging from infrastructure projects still 
undergoing procurement or construction to 
fully completed assistance programmes.

In healthcare, the Government allocated 
RM46.5 billion under Budget 2026 to 
improve access, facilities and service delivery, 
including RM1.2 billion for the maintenance 
and repair of hospitals and clinics as well as 
RM100 million to upgrade district hospital 
wards. As at end-August 2026, 89 of the 102 
projects received letters of acceptance to 
upgrade wards in district hospitals, while the 
remaining projects are still at the preliminary 
procurement stages. In addition, RM3.9 million 
was spent on primary healthcare reform, 
implemented through health departments 
nationwide. Furthermore, in supporting a more 
transparent healthcare costs and stronger 
integration of public and private services, 
the Ministry of Health Malaysia continues to 
encourage the sharing of clinical and financial 
cost information through the Price, Admission 
and Clinical Electronic Data Hub (PACED) 
system. As at end-August 2026, over 90 private 
hospitals submitted more than 480,000 clinical 
records and around 380,000 billing records, 
while 50 clinical coding-training sessions 
benefitted more than 2,700 participants. 
Under the education pillar, initiatives continue 
to focus on enhancing facilities and access 
as well as expanding student welfare. At the 
tertiary level, RM20 million was allocated for 
the expansion of MySISWA Job on Campus 
initiative to all public universities. As of August 
2026, more than 13,000 students have been 
appointed across 850 types of job placements 
on campus, exceeding the original target of 
10,000. Additionally, the Dapur Siswa MADANI 
initiative was also expanded from 75 to 143 
operating premises, supporting over 200,000 
students from lower-income and vulnerable 
groups as at end-July 2026.
In easing the mobility of the rakyat, measures 
were put in place to improve public transport 
access, rural connectivity and road safety. 
The expansion of the Stage Bus Service 
Transformation (SBST) programme has 
increased the number of routes and buses 
nationwide, benefitting more than 18 million 
passengers. Meanwhile, subsidised Rural Air 
Services in Sabah and Sarawak eased the 
mobility of more than 800,000 people. The 
complimentary MyRailLife pass, covering 
Keretapi Tanah Melayu (KTM) Komuter and rail 
shuttle services across Peninsular Malaysia, 
benefitted more than 350,000 users, namely 
PWDs, primary and secondary schoolchildren 
as well as children below six years old. In 
terms of expediting the process of road 
maintenance, the MYJalan platform plays a 
pivotal role in ensuring timely response to 
complaints on road defects and infrastructure 
issues at the district level. As at end-July 
2026, 73.1% of the RM30 million allocation for 
district road works was disbursed.
Progress in expanding access to affordable 
housing was sustained through integrated 
measures encompassing new housing 
construction, repairs, financing support and 
maintenance works. Budget 2026 allocated 
RM672 million for the Program Residensi 
Rakyat (PRR) and Rumah Mesra Rakyat (RMR) 
projects, benefitting an estimated 33,000 
residents. As at end-August 2026, 60.5% of 
the total allocation, equivalent to more than 
RM400 million had been disbursed to finance 
the PRR and RMR projects comprising of 
various implementation stages, which are 
planning (8,122 units), under construction 
(4,051 units) and completed (5,344 units).
Meanwhile, the Government continues 
to register strong progress in supporting 
homeownership, particularly among borrowers 
who require financing guarantees to secure 
housing loans. Syarikat Jaminan Kredit 
Perumahan Berhad (SJKP) is on track to achieve 
its 2026 target of around RM8 billion total 
guarantees in home financing, reinforcing its 
role in promoting greater financial inclusion. 
As at end-August 2026, more than 20,000 
applications were approved, with total 
financing amounting to RM5.5 billion.

Measure Nine: Building Self-
Identity, Developing Human 
Values
Building self-identity and developing strong 
human values are essential to creating a 
united, progressive and resilient nation. These 
efforts contribute to strengthening social 
cohesion, good governance and shared values 
towards building an inclusive and harmonious 
society.
In upholding Islamic principles, RM35 million 
was provided to promote the comprehensive 
practice of Islam. Key community leaders 
that play an essential role in advancing 
Islamic education, fostering good character 
and promoting values that strengthen social 
harmony and nation-building are appreciated 
through the special contribution involving 
70,000 imam, bilal, siak/noja/marbut and 
takmir teachers. Furthermore, the upgrading 
of infrastructure at 27 TVET tahfiz schools are 
ongoing with RM2 million disbursed as at end-
August 2026.
Malaysia’s strength lies in its multicultural 
society. In leveraging this advantage, efforts 
were undertaken to enhance the rakyat’s unity, 
strengthen national identity and promote 
multi-racial family-oriented activities. In line 
with this, Budget 2026 allocated RM55 million 
for national unity and identity programmes 
as well as RM60 million for Rukun Tetangga 
MADANI. As at end-August 2026, progress of 
expenditures for both programmes stood at 
65.9% and 90.5%, respectively.
Investment in sports facilities, talent 
development and community participation 
are vital in encouraging healthy lifestyles at 
the same time uniting Malaysians through 
shared achievements and national pride. 
Hence, Budget 2026 further intensified the 
development of national sports through the 
Podium Programme with an allocation of 
RM70 million, of which 52.5% was spent as at 
end-July 2026. More than 1,000 athletes and 
coaches have benefitted from this initiative 
through training programmes as well as 
participation in domestic and international 
competitions. As football plays a significant 
role in fostering unity, RM68 million was 
allocated for football development programmes 
which encompass the Program Pembangunan 
Bola Sepak Negara (PPBN), succession planning 
and talent development, with a disbursement 
of 56% as at end-July 2026. 
Promoting arts and creativity strengthens 
cultural appreciation, preserves heritage 
and inspires innovation among future 
generations. The Film in Malaysia Incentive 
(FIMI) remains a strategic driver of investment 
in Malaysia’s screen industry, attracting both 
international and domestic productions while 
generating wider economic benefits through 
local spending, employment and industry 
development. As of September 2026, FIMI-
supported productions have generated 
approximately RM550 million in investment and 
created more than 5,500 jobs for Malaysians 
nationwide. Under Budget 2026, RM100 million 
was allocated to FIMI, of which approximately 
RM40 million has been channelled to the 
National Film Development Corporation 
Malaysia (FINAS) for the first and second 
quarter rebate payments. These outcomes 
reflect FIMI’s strong role as an investment-
enabling mechanism that translates public 
funding into tangible economic value, industry 
growth and employment opportunities for 
Malaysia. 
Furthermore, RM20 million was allocated 
under Budget 2026 for the Digital Creative 
Ecosystem (DICE) initiative, in an effort to 
position Malaysia as a leading regional 
digital creative hub. This programme has 
generated more than 1,600 jobs and trained 
over 300 participants. It has also supported 
20 digital creative startups and 10 digital 
creative companies. Through its market 
access initiatives, the programme further 
facilitated more than RM350 million in export 
opportunities for the digital creative  
industry.

Economic Management
Malaysia continues to progress towards the 
aspirations of the Ekonomi MADANI framework 
and the Thirteenth Plan, underpinned by 
sustained economic growth and improvements 
in competitiveness. In sustaining resiliency 
amid the challenging and uncertain global 
landscape, the Government remains committed 
to prudent and balanced fiscal and economic 
management as the nation transitions towards 
a higher-value economy. Building on these 
efforts, ongoing initiatives seek to strengthen 
productivity, facilitate investments, scale up 
MSMEs, accelerate technology and digital 
adoption, advance the sustainability agenda as 
well as ensure food security. The Government 
remains committed to raising living standards 
and strengthening socioeconomic resilience 
amid persistent cost-of-living pressures, 
regional disparity, modest wage progression 
and demographic shifts. 
Raising the Ceiling
MSME empowerment
Malaysia’s MSMEs play an important role in 
the economy, accounting for 39.7% of GDP in 
2025. Being among the frontrunners in the 
ASEAN SME Policy Index 2024, which includes 
micro enterprises, Malaysia is recognised for 
its well-developed institutional framework, 
robust financing ecosystem and extensive 
support programmes for MSME digitalisation, 
innovation and market expansion. 
Nevertheless, the index highlights further 
progress is needed in programme evaluation 
and MSME sustainability initiatives. In light 
of this, the Thirteenth Plan aims to increase 
MSME contribution to 50% of GDP by 2030, 
through the development of more productive 
and competitive domestic enterprises.
Limited participation in global value chains, 
financing constraints and uneven digital 
adoption continue to hinder the firms’ ability 
to scale up and compete internationally. 
Gaps in technology, talent, certification 
and market linkages may further challenge 
productivity improvements and access to 
regional and global markets. Strengthening 
these capabilities will be critical to enabling 
more firms to participate in higher value-added 
activities, expand beyond the domestic market 
and support Malaysia’s transition towards a 
higher-value economy.
Quality Investment
Malaysia continues to strengthen its position 
as a favourable investment destination, 
supported by improvements in competitiveness 
and sustained investment appetite. These 
developments provide opportunities to advance 
semiconductors, AI, digital services, the energy 
transition, pharmaceuticals, logistics and 
aerospace industries through various national 
policies. While investment approvals continue 
to increase, several assessments by the Special 
Task Force to Facilitate Business (PEMUDAH) 
and the World Bank, among others, have 
highlighted persistent challenges in translating 
approved investments into operational 
projects. These challenges include regulatory, 
operational, skilled talents, domestic supply-
chain participation and technology adoption 
among local MSMEs.
Energy and Climate Resilience
Malaysia faces growing challenges in balancing 
decarbonisation with the need to strengthen 
resilience against climate-related risks. With 
a ranking of eighth among 24 Asia Pacific 
economies and 95th globally in the 2026 
Environmental Performance Index (EPI), 
expanding energy efficiency, renewable energy 
(RE) and low-carbon technologies requires 
substantial infrastructure and investment. 
Notwithstanding, climate mitigation and 
adaptation must keep pace with increasing 
exposure to extreme weather globally, which 
necessitate substantial financing requirements. 
As gaps in water efficiency and supply 
resilience persist, strengthening water security 
is critical to mitigate future supply disruptions.

information box 1.1
Malaysia's Journey in Financing for Sustainability
Joint Committee on Climate Change (JC3): Building climate resilience into the ﬁnancial system in Malaysia 
through a collaborative regulator-industry platform co-chaired by Bank Negara Malaysia (BNM) and the SC.
SRI Roadmap for the Malaysian Capital Market: Positioning Malaysia as a regional SRI centre through a 
ﬁve-year roadmap.
Ecological Fiscal Transfer (EFT): Introducing annual ﬁscal transfers from Federal to state governments to 
incentivise  conservation eﬀorts, biodiversity protection as well as the management of forest reserves and 
protected areas.
2019
National Conservation Trust Fund (NCTF): Establishing a dedicated trust fund which is currently managed by 
the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environmental Sustainability (NRES) to carry out long-term conservation 
eﬀorts supporting natural resource management, related research and development (R&D) as well as capacity 
building eﬀorts.
Mandatory Sustainability Statement in Main Market & ACE Market: Shifting focus from Corporate Social 
Responsibility (CSR) to ESG, requiring listed corporations under Bursa Malaysia to disclose management of 
material economic, environmental and social (EES) risks and opportunities. 
Sukuk Ihsan: Khazanah Nasional Berhad (Khazanah) issuing Malaysia’s ﬁrst SRI Sukuk for social and educational 
programmes.
2015
Green Sukuk: Launching of the world’s ﬁrst corporate green sukuk for solar farm ﬁnancing, strengthening Malaysia’s position 
as a leading Islamic ﬁnance marketplace and centre for sustainable ﬁnance.
Strategy Paper for Value-based Intermediation (VBI): Strengthening Islamic banking as a driver in 
value-based ethical banking towards generating positive, sustainable impacts on the economy, community and 
environment, aligning with Maqasid al-Shariah.
Islamic Fund and Wealth Management Blueprint: Establishing Malaysia’s ﬁve-year blueprint as a regional centre for 
Shariah-compliant sustainable and responsible investment.
2017
Green Technology Financing Scheme (GTFS) 2.0: Extending the ﬁnancing mechanism which supports the 
uptake of green technology projects, with an allocation of RM2 billion for the period from January 2019 until the 
end of 2020.
Guidance Documents on VBI: Facilitating the practical adoption of VBI to re-orient Islamic ﬁnance business 
models towards realising the objectives of Shariah that generate positive and sustainable impact through 
practices, processes, oﬀerings and conduct. 
Green SRI Sukuk Grant Scheme: Encouraging eligible issuers by defraying the external review costs of sukuk 
issuance under the SC’s SRI Sukuk Framework, ASEAN Bonds under the ASEAN Standards and SRI-linked Sukuk 
under the SRI-linked Sukuk Framework.
2018
Malaysia has always been at the forefront of laying the foundations for ﬁnancing sustainability through national environmental, energy 
and climate policies, complemented by ﬁscal incentives to encourage green investments. Commencing 2014 onwards, eﬀorts have 
been intensiﬁed towards developing the regulatory frameworks, ﬁnancial instruments and market infrastructure needed to mobilise 
public and private capital to support the country's sustainability goals including climate initiatives. The highlights are as follows:
2014
Sustainable and Responsible Investment (SRI) Sukuk Framework: The Securities Commission Malaysia’s (SC) 
pioneering regulatory framework which integrates Islamic capital market principles with global sustainability 
benchmarks to facilitate financing of sustainable and responsible investment initiatives.
Green Investment Tax Allowance (GITA)/Green Income Tax Exemption (GITE): Driving the growth of the 
country’s green economy through tax incentives on green technology purchases and green service providers. 
FTSE4Good Bursa Malaysia (F4GBM) Index: Catering to investors’ demand for environmental, social and 
governance (ESG).
Malaysian Code for Institutional Investors (MCII): Encouraging active responsible ownership and good 
corporate governance through the SC’s first institutional investor stewardship code in Southeast Asia.
2014

chapter 1   economic management and prospects
economic outlook   2027
17
Waqf-Featured Fund Framework: Incorporating the development of waqf assets as an eligible SRI project, while 
facilitating the public oﬀering of the world’s ﬁrst waqf shares through unit trusts and wholesale funds, 
integrating commercial and social objectives. 
2020
2021
SDG Sukuk Framework: Positioning Malaysia at the forefront of funding SDG-aligned development. 
Malaysia 2021 Global Sukuk Issuance: World’s ﬁrst sovereign USD Sustainability Sukuk to ﬁnance eligible 
programmes and projects aligned with the SDG Sukuk Framework.
SDG National Budget Tagging: First mapping exercise for the Government’s annual budget allocation against 
the SDGs.
Climate Change and Principle-based Taxonomy: Assisting ﬁnancial institutions to assess and classify economic 
activities aligned with climate objectives, as published by BNM.
SRI Sukuk and Bond Grant Scheme: Expanding and rebranding the Green SRI Sukuk Grant Scheme to include 
social and sustainability projects in accordance with ASEAN standards.
FTSE4Good Bursa Malaysia Shariah Index: Incorporating Islamic ﬁnance into sustainability investing as a 
Shariah-compliant version.
Capital Market Masterplan 2021 — 2025 (CMP3): Elevating SRI as one of its central development pillars through 
the establishment of holistic ESG disclosure guidelines and the SRI Taxonomy, with stronger integration of 
sustainability compared with CMP2.

the Malaysia-UN SDG Trust Fund with the United Nations (UN).
Government Green Procurement: Strengthening the Government’s commitment to environmental 
sustainability by integrating green criteria into government procurement through the implementation of the 
Treasury Circular on Government Procurement 1.9 (PP/PK 1.9 Dasar Perolehan Hijau Kerajaan).
Sustainability Malaysia Government Investment Issues (Sustainability MGII): Issuing Malaysia’s ﬁrst 
domestic Sustainability MGII issued via BNM, with proceeds channelled to ﬁnance and reﬁnance eligible social 
and green projects.
Principles-based SRI Taxonomy: Driving the climate and sustainability agenda within the capital market 
through the classiﬁcation of qualifying investment activities, as published by the SC.
Climate Risk Management and Scenario Analysis Policy: Enhancing ﬁnancial sector resilience by mandating 
banks and insurers to integrate physical and transition risk assessments, governance and scenario modelling into 
their core business operations, as published by BNM.
SRI-linked Sukuk Framework: Facilitating fundraising by companies in addressing sustainability concerns with 
features that relate to issuers’ sustainability performance commitments.
Bursa Carbon Exchange: Launching of the world’s ﬁrst Shariah-compliant multi-environmental product 
exchange that facilitates voluntary trading of carbon credits and renewable energy certiﬁcates.
Low Carbon Transition Facility: Supporting micro, small and medium enterprises (MSMEs) in adopting 
sustainable and low-carbon practices through a BNM loan facility. 
2022
2023
National Energy Transition Facility (NETF): Enabling catalytic blended ﬁnance for marginally bankable energy 
transition projects, established under the National Energy Transition Roadmap (NETR).
Simplified ESG Disclosure Guide (SEDG): Guiding MSMEs to adopt sustainability practices and meet ESG 
disclosure requirements, in unlocking sustainability ﬁnancing.
ESG Jumpstart Portal: Launching of JC3’s platform with curated ESG resources, step-by-step guides, 
self-assessment tools and access to green ﬁnancing for MSMEs.
The Sustainable Investing Standards (SIS): Guiding government-linked investment companies (GLICs) in 
integrating sustainability considerations into investment processes and disclosures, complementing the 
Principles on Good Governance (PGG) and MCII on governance and stewardship, while also serving as a reference 
for the broader investment community.

Moving forward, Malaysia remains ﬁrm in strengthening its sustainability ﬁnance ecosystem through coordinated policy reforms, 
regional collaborations and international engagements. Greater interoperability across ASEAN, alignment with evolving international 
standards and continued innovation in sustainability ﬁnancing will strengthen policy coherence and facilitate sustainability 
investment. These eﬀorts will support the country’s transition toward a low carbon, climate resilient and inclusive economy.

National Carbon Market Policy: Providing a foundational framework to regulate carbon credit generation, 
ensuring high environmental integrity and supporting future carbon pricing instruments like the domestic 
emissions trading scheme or carbon tax.
Capital Market Masterplan 2026 — 2030 (CMP4): Launching of the SC's fourth capital-market blueprint, where 
Sustainability was named as one of its core pillars. It aims to scale Malaysia's capital market to RM5.8 trillion – 
RM6.3 trillion by 2030, driving high-income economic transition through market vibrancy, inclusivity and 
sustainability as well as regional gateway opportunities.
Blue Sukuk: Pioneering two blue sukuk issued by Pengurusan Air Selangor Sdn. Bhd. and Pengurusan Aset Air 
Berhad, to support critical water infrastructure and non-revenue water reduction projects.

initiatives.

(Incorporated) (KWAP) to mobilise capital for the transition to a low carbon and climate resilient economy.

corporate fundraising for the nation's third sector, while guaranteeing transparency, measurable impacts and 
seamless ESG compliance for corporate donors, retail investors and platform operators.
JC3 Guidance on Sustainable and Transition Finance: Providing banks with practical rules to align ﬁnancing 
with green principles at both the asset and entity levels, oﬀering tools to assess client transition progress and 
handle projects falling short of strict taxonomies.
National Sustainability Reporting Framework (NSRF): Establishing a standardised baseline for corporate ESG 
and climate disclosures, through the adoption of reporting standards by the International Sustainability 
Standards Board (ISSB), IFRS S11 and IFRS S22, to ensure corporate sustainability data in Malaysia is consistent, 
reliable and globally comparable.
Climate Risk Stress Testing (CRST) Methodology Paper: Introducing a framework for ﬁnancial institutions to 
quantify climate risks, strengthen risk management and assess climate-related vulnerabilities through stress 
testing.

Emissions Trading Scheme (ETS): The ETS is expected to enhance carbon market transparency, drive industrial

chapter 1   economic management and prospects
economic outlook   2027
19
Food Security
Malaysia’s food security remains stable with 
the national Food Security Index scoring 61.5 
in 2024, compared to 54.5 in 2023. However, 
continued reliance on imported food and 
agricultural inputs creates significant long-
term structural risks. Out of 60 agricultural 
commodities, only 24 meet the self-sufficiency 
level needs of the country. This dependency 
exposes Malaysia to volatility in food prices, 
especially for staple food. In overcoming the 
challenges, there is a need in accelerating the 
adoption of technology in raising yields, 
reducing losses and increasing skilled 
employment. 
Digitalisation and Cybersecurity
Malaysia ranked 34th in the IMD World Digital 
Competitiveness Ranking 2025, improving 
from 36th in 2024, while its Future Readiness 
Indicator stood lower at 40th. This reflects 
Malaysia’s preparedness in digital readiness 
that requires further adoption among 
businesses which translates into higher 
productivity, innovation and competitiveness. 
Nevertheless, the digital transformation 
significantly increases the nation’s exposure to 
external cyber threats. Despite Malaysia’s early 
progress in post-quantum cryptography (PQC), 
readiness remains uneven, with a multitude 
of gaps in technical expertise, cryptographic 
inventories and migration planning across 
organisations.
Raising the Floor
Infrastructure and Regional Development
Disparities in access to quality infrastructure 
and public services remain a key challenge 
to balanced development across states and 
rural-urban areas. At the same time, rapid 
urbanisation continues to place pressure on 
public transport, housing affordability and 
urban liveability. Community development to 
uplift the socioeconomic status of the target 
groups also remains a challenge. These include 
limited Bumiputera economic participation 
and ownership as well as inadequate access 
to job opportunities and universally designed 
infrastructure for PWDs. The youth and women 
also continue to encounter social mobility and 
capability constraints to pursue a more decent 
living. 
Social Protection
Despite significant progress, Malaysia’s 
social protection landscape continues to 
face challenges in providing adequate and 
comprehensive protection across different 
forms of employment and household 
circumstances amid the rising cost of living. 
Employment-based protection frameworks, 
particularly for the self-employed and informal 
employment, require further administrative 
enhancements including mandatory registration 
and coverage depth. The effectiveness of Active 
Labour Market Policies (ALMPs) and income-
generating programmes is also constrained by 
skills mismatches, heavy reliance on low-skilled 
foreign workers and ineffective intervention. 
Meanwhile, fragmentation across cash and 
welfare assistance schemes face inefficiencies 
in social safety net delivery. While targeted 
assistance programmes have helped 
cushion vulnerable groups from rising living 
costs, persistent income disparities and 
socioeconomic challenges underscore the 
need for continued support. This presents 
an opportunity to enhance the mechanism 
and delivery of social safety net programmes, 
including STR, SARA and welfare schemes for 
children, senior citizens and PWDs. 
However, continued dependence on income 
assistance among vulnerable households 
highlights the need to complement social 
transfers with stronger pathways toward 
sustainable employment and income 
generation. Better integration of social 
assistance, contributory protection and ALMPs 
will therefore be important in strengthening 
household resilience and reducing longer-term 
vulnerability.

chapter 1   economic management and prospects
economic outlook   2027
20
Education and Healthcare
The education system has undergone various 
phases of change, aimed at developing the 
potential of the rakyat through a holistic 
and integrated approach. However, there 
are shortcomings in terms of quality and 
learning outcomes at different levels of 
education, despite substantial annual 
investments. Although Malaysia has achieved 
high preschool (93.5%), universal primary 
school (99%) and high secondary school 
(94.9%) enrolments, overall student outcomes 
such as the Programme for International 
Student Assessment (PISA) as well as Trends 
in International Mathematics and Science 
Study (TIMSS) remain below targeted 
standards. Tertiary institution outputs 
are often misaligned with actual industry 
talent requirements. Moreover, dilapidated 
infrastructures in remote rural schools need to 
be upgraded while urban areas are faced with 
overcrowded schools. 
At the same time, the healthcare sector 
is strained by shortages of specialists and 
escalating medical cost that risks service 
affordability and quality, alongside a growing 
prevalence of non-communicable diseases and 
unhealthy lifestyle trends. These healthcare 
pressures are further intensified by rapid 
demographic ageing, inadequate long-term 
care ecosystems and expanding fiscal burdens 
on the public healthcare infrastructure, 
underscoring the urgency for comprehensive 
reforms to strengthen both accessibility and 
quality of care.
Workers’ Well-being
Malaysia has made notable strides in 
strengthening the labour market ecosystem 
through targeted employment and decent-
wage initiatives. Nonetheless, driving sustained 
wage progression to elevate workers’ living 
standards remains a persistent structural 
challenge. Consequently, the distribution 
of economic growth remains imbalanced, 
with a low CE share of GDP. This reflects 
demand- and supply-side constraints including 
limited creation of high-paying jobs; heavy 
concentration of workers in semi-skilled 
occupations; low technology adoption; and 
skills-related underemployment that depress 
wage floors. Furthermore, wage-setting 
practices remain predominantly employer-
driven rather than determined through 
equitable collective negotiations, restricting 
wage growth and upward worker mobility. 
To ensure economic growth translates into 
inclusive outcomes, a coordinated approach 
is required in streamlining structural industrial 
reforms, controlling the hiring of foreign 
labour, formulating holistic wage policies 
and expanding collective bargaining  
platforms.

chapter 1   economic management and prospects
economic outlook   2027

feature article 1.1 
Lifting Wages from the Ground Up
In collaboration with THE FUTURE1
Introduction
Malaysia has made significant progress in strengthening the wage ecosystem and improving 
workers’ living standards through various decent wage policies. These initiatives have successfully 
raised the wage floor for low-paid workers, strengthened income progression and established 
important foundations for a more inclusive and productive labour market. As the market becomes 
more dynamic, the next policy challenge lies in ensuring workers are able to progress more 
meaningfully beyond the wage floor throughout the career path.
The challenge facing Malaysia’s wage ecosystem is deeply rooted in the structure of its economy, 
particularly the concentration of labour-intensive micro, small and medium enterprises (MSMEs) in 
persistently low-wage industries. While existing policies have strengthened wage protection and 
workers capabilities, further uplift towards sustainable wage growth requires firms to generate 
greater demand for high-skilled workers and better-paying jobs. This article analyses the current 
structural wage imbalances and proposes policy options needed for a targeted 'big push' that 
integrates MSMEs’ upgrading, industrial development and workforce interventions. By positioning 
productivity as the bridge between stronger business capabilities and wage progression, the 
country will be able to address structural wage imbalances, strengthen the wage ladder and ensure 
economic growth delivers broader improvements in workers’ living standards.
From a Higher Wage Floor to a Stronger Wage Ladder
Over the years, Malaysia’s economic expansion has generated employment and raised nominal 
wages, but the distribution of these gains across workers remains uneven. An important indicator 
of economic growth distributed to workers is the labour income share, measured by compensation 
of employees (CE). A rising labour income share suggests that workers are benefitting more from 
the gains in production, whereas a persistently low or declining share may indicate that economic 
growth is not being translated proportionately into employee compensation. 
As shown in Figure 1.1.1., the CE accounted for 33.6% of Malaysia’s GDP in 2024, which remained 
below the pre COVID-19 pandemic levels. Although GDP and employment have recovered, the post-
pandemic pattern highlights a continuing challenge in ensuring economic recovery translates into 
broad-based improvements in labour income. This situation points to an important dimension in 
Malaysia’s wage challenge, as the issue extends beyond the earnings of low-paid workers to the 
capacity of the economic structure in generating sustained and widely shared wage progression. 
To understand the overall wage dynamics, it is important to distinguish the varying impacts of the 
minimum wage policy, the compression of wages and the disproportionate wage growth.
1	 Centre for Future Studies Berhad (THE FUTURE) is a Malaysian think tank that supports evidence-based decision-making, capacity building and 
policy innovation. It has contributed to policy studies, strategic publications and reform initiatives to various governments.

economic outlook   2027

Minimum Wage Increases Have Raised the Floor, but Not Transformed the Distribution
Minimum wage legislation has played an important role in protecting low-paid workers. As shown 
in Figure 1.1.2., Malaysia’s wage distribution has shifted to a higher trajectory for the period of 
2015 to 2024, as a result of successive increases in the statutory minimum wage. The peak of the 
distribution moved from below RM1,300 in 2015 to approximately RM1,700 — RM2,000 in 2024. 
This pattern is consistent with minimum wage adjustments lifting the lower tail of the distribution 
and reducing the concentration of workers in the lowest wage brackets.

Nevertheless, a substantial proportion of wage recipients continues to earn close to the statutory 
minimum, while the middle-wage bracket remains comparatively narrow. This reflects the different 
wage dynamics across the distribution, as the statutory minimum wage directly lifts wages at the 
lower end, while workers in the higher brackets above the minimum wage are not necessarily 
offered wage adjustments. The lack of wage adjustments assurance would result in relatively 
weaker wage progression within the middle-wage bracket. While wage spillover effects remain 
limited, the minimum wage has been more effective as a protective floor than as a mechanism for 
generating sustained wage progression beyond the floor.
Figure 1.1.2. also shows that the actual wage distribution in Malaysia is skewed to the right, where 
the majority of workers earn relatively modest wages, while a small proportion of high-income 
earners receive substantially larger wages. This scenario presents a wider gap compared to the 
ideal distribution, which depicts the broad characteristics of a more mature high-wage economy. 
Moving towards the ideal distribution requires more than periodic adjustments to the statutory 
minimum wage, alongside stronger productivity growth, occupational upgrading and the creation of 
more middle- and high-skilled jobs.
The Missing Middle is Concentrated among Semi-Skilled Workers
Although skills command a wage premium, employment remains concentrated in semi-skilled 
occupations, reflecting the underlying structure of the economy. As shown in Figure 1.1.3., semi-
skilled occupations accounted for 55.4% of total employment in 2024, compared with 36.5% for 
skilled and 8.1% for low-skilled. Consequently, the wage distribution is shaped principally by the 
earnings trajectory of semi-skilled workers, rather than by low-skilled employment alone.
In general, average monthly wages across the three occupational groups experienced an increasing 
trend between 2010 and 2024. Skilled workers’ wages rose from RM3,083 to RM5,582, while semi-
skilled wages increased from RM1,384 to RM2,604 and low-skilled wages from RM987 to RM2,128. 
Despite these improvements, the skilled to semi-skilled wage gap in 2024 remained substantial

at 2.1 times, whereas the average semi-skilled to low-skilled wage was only about 1.2 times. The 
wage differential signifies the incidence of a lack of gradual ladder that supports continuous wage 
progression among the semi-skilled workers. Without clearer intermediate wage intervention, 
workers may have limited motivation and opportunities to progress, while employers may face 
greater difficulty retaining and upgrading workers.
Skilled Workers Contributed Disproportionately to Aggregate Wage Gains
Aggregate wage increase was contributed disproportionately by skilled workers, as shown in Figure 
1.1.3. Between 2010 and 2024, an estimated 56.8% of the overall increase in average wages was 
earned by skilled workers, compared with 38.1% for semi-skilled workers and 5.1% for low-skilled 
workers. This contribution was driven by the higher wage base for skilled workers who experienced 
the largest absolute wage increase with a sizeable share of wage recipients. Meanwhile, the semi-
skilled workers who represented the largest share of employment, contributed a relatively smaller 
share of aggregate wage gains. Hence, a substantial wage increase within this group is pertinent 
to accelerate the upward wage progression. 
Labour-Intensive Industries Associated with Low Wages and Weak Wage Progression
The industry-level analysis provides further insight into the structural factors shaping Malaysia’s 
wage landscape. Figure 1.1.4. shows that many of the largest employing industries are positioned 
below the median monthly wage. The concentration of workers in this high-employment, low-wage 
segment indicates the continued importance of labour-intensive industries in employment creation, 
often underpinned by business models that rely on relatively low labour costs. The overall wage 
level is therefore constrained by the composition of employment, with a large share of workers 
remaining concentrated in industries characterised by relatively low value-added and productivity 
per worker.
