Moreover, the challenge extends beyond wage levels at a particular point in time. It involves a 
limited improvement in the relative wage position of many industries, suggesting lower wage 
industries made little progress in narrowing wage gaps over time. Figure 1.1.5. shows that 
81% of industries remained in the same wage quadrant between 2015 and 2024, including 35 
industries that remained below the sectoral median wage in both years. Meanwhile, only eight 
industries managed to move from below to above the median wage between the same period. This 
persistence suggests that wage growth has generally been insufficient to alter the relative position 
of most lower-wage industries, pointing to structural constraints on wage progression rather than 
solely to temporary cyclical weakness.
Upgrading Labour-Intensive MSME Can Create a 'Big-push' Impact
Figure 1.1.6. shows the structural distribution of MSMEs across industries that dominate 93.7% 
of total establishments. However, MSMEs contributed only 51.1% of total wages, indicating an 
average establishment-to-wage gap of 42.6 percentage points (ppt). This gap is consistent with a 
fragmented production structure in which many smaller firms operate at a limited scale and remain 
concentrated in labour-intensive and lower-value activities. As MSMEs play a vital role in shaping 
the wage outcomes, it is important to accelerate MSMEs transformation by enhancing technology 
adoption, managerial sophistication and integration into high-value markets as well as translating 
productivity gains effectively into wage progression.

chapter 1   economic management and prospects
economic outlook   2027
26
An analysis of remuneration across skill categories suggests that Malaysia’s wage challenges persist 
despite favourable labour market conditions. Figure 1.1.7. confirms the substantial wage gap 
between skilled and semi-skilled workers, while a much narrower gap was observed between semi- 
and low-skilled workers. This reflects the wage challenges faced by MSMEs across all economic 
industries, particularly the concentration in labour-intensive and lower-productivity activities.

The MSME Wage Inequality Survey conducted by the Malaysia Productivity Corporation in 2025 
further underscores the case for strengthening wage systems within MSMEs. The survey shows 
that MSMEs generally have basic compensation arrangements, but these have not developed into 
coherent and institutionalised wage-progression systems (Figure 1.1.8.). Although basic fixed wage 
or salary (97.2%), allowances (83.1%) and performance-based bonuses or commissions (66.3%) are 
widely reported, only 35.9% of firms have workers covered by collective agreements that bind the 
employers and workers to mutually agreed wage and employment conditions. The survey also 
confirms that wage setting remains predominantly management-led, in which 91.9% of decisions 
are made through fully or partially centralised arrangements, while only 5.3% involve employee 
participation. Moreover, less than half of firms primarily use merit-based wage setting, suggesting 
that pay progression is not consistently anchored to skills, qualifications, responsibilities or market 
benchmarks.
The survey further highlights the relationship between remuneration and productivity remains 
misaligned. A total of 54.7% of firms offer performance-based compensation, accounting for 
between 1% — 10% of average employees’ total remuneration, while 11.3% of firms do not 
provide any performance-based pay. These patterns suggest that many MSMEs practise some form 
of individualised pay component but lack a transparent mechanism linking skills development, 
improved performance and productivity gains to sustained wage progression, hindering workers 
from obtaining a performance-based wage increment. To strengthen this, policy support could 
therefore help MSMEs introduce occupational wage bands, skills-based career pathways and 
productivity-based incentives, alongside technology adoption and business upgrading that 
strengthen a firm’s ability to finance higher wages sustainably.

Building a Stronger Wage Ladder Requires an Integrated Approach
Raising wages in labour-intensive industries should not be viewed solely as achieving labour-market 
or supply-side objectives. It must be complemented by demand-side initiatives that promote firm 
and industrial upgrading through automation and digitalisation, with productivity serving as the 
critical link between higher-value economic activities and sustainable wage progression. The key 
challenge is to ensure that wage increases are underpinned by productivity improvements that 
make such progression sustainable over time for the labour market to reach an ideal state, where 
ratios between skill categories are more balanced. 
Closing the Wage Gap Requires Productivity-Led Wage Progression
The ideal proportion of adjustment required to establish a stronger and more balanced wage 
ladder is illustrated in Figure 1.1.9. The simulation narrows the wage ratio between skilled and 
semi-skilled workers from approximately 2.1 times in 2024 to a ratio of 1.6 times, while the ratio 
between semi-skilled and low-skilled workers widens from approximately 1.2 times to 1.3 times. 
Together, these adjustments would strengthen wage progression in the lower and middle segments 
of the distribution while preserving an appropriate premium for higher levels of skill, occupational 
complexity and responsibility.
These ratios are intended to illustrate the desired direction of wage adjustment rather than 
serve as prescribed wage targets. The ratios characterise a more balanced wage structure in 
which each advancement in skills and responsibilities is accompanied by a meaningful increase in 
remuneration. The objective of having a more balanced wage structure is to establish a clearer and 
more sustainable pathway for workers to receive decent wages. 

chapter 1   economic management and prospects
economic outlook   2027
29
The simulated productivity-wage alignment in Figure 1.1.9. highlights a fundamental condition for 
sustainable wage progression where real wage needs to grow in tandem with productivity over the 
medium to longer term. Raising wages must correspond with productivity gains to avoid widening 
the gap between labour costs and productive capacity, as this would affect a firm’s competitiveness 
and employment decisions. On the other hand, the labour income share would weaken if workers’ 
productivity improvements are not adequately compensated through higher wages, resulting in 
constrained domestic demand. A sustainable high-wage economy therefore requires productivity 
and real wages to advance concurrently2.
Nevertheless, the central challenge in the labour market that impedes the intended outcome lies 
in the limited integration between the supply and demand for skills. While education and training 
institutions shape the supply of workforce, the types of skills, occupations and jobs demanded 
by the economy are determined by the industry. When these two sides are insufficiently aligned, 
the labour market may experience persistent skills mismatches, youth unemployment and 
underemployment, weak wage progression and a disconnect between productivity and wages. 
These indicate that improvements in education and training alone may not translate into better 
wage outcomes unless firms simultaneously create high-paying jobs and strengthen demand for 
more advanced skills.
Way Forward 
Malaysia’s next phase of wage reform should move beyond raising the wage floor towards building 
a stronger wage ladder that enables meaningful career progression for workers. While maintaining 
an effective national minimum wage remains important for protecting workers at the lower end 
of the labour market, wage regulation alone would not transform the underlying structure of 
employment and industries. Sustainable wage progression requires a broader approach that 
simultaneously strengthens workers’ opportunities to move up the wage ladder and firms’ capacity 
to support higher wages through productivity growth and higher-value economic activities. This is 
particularly important given the continued concentration of employment in labour-intensive and 
relatively low-wage industries.
A key priority is therefore to accelerate the upgrading of labour-intensive industries, particularly 
MSMEs. Policy support should enable these firms to adopt technology, improve managerial and 
organisational capabilities, modernise business processes, strengthen domestic and global market 
linkages as well as progressively transition into higher-value activities. Such upgrading would 
increase value-added and productivity at the firm level, strengthening the capacity of industries to 
support meaningful and sustained wage progression across skill levels. 
At the same time, workforce development must be more closely aligned with the evolution in 
demand for skills. Strengthening education, including TVET, reskilling and upskilling remains 
essential, but improvements in the supply of talent will generate stronger wage outcomes 
only when firms create jobs that utilise and reward those capabilities. This requires strategic 
coordination between education and training institutions, and industries to improve job matching. 
Existing initiatives such as the Progressive Wage Policy and Academy in Industry provide useful 
foundations for strengthening this connection.
2 The Malaysia Economic Monitor: Raising the Ceiling, Raising the Floor report by the World Bank documents the degree of convergence between 
wage growth and labour productivity growth in Malaysia.

Governance and Transparency
Governance reforms have continued to 
advance, strengthening Malaysia’s institutional 
foundations. However, the key challenge 
remains in translating these reforms into 
faster, more transparent and measurable 
outcomes. While Malaysia’s standing in 
governance has improved, challenges 
in the rule of law, politics and economy 
persist, highlighting the need to enhance 
implementation effectiveness, enforcement 
efficiency, as well as the timeliness of 
regulatory and administrative processes. 
Furthermore, increasing demands arising from 
education, population ageing, healthcare, social 
protection and digital transformation, together 
with the need to rebuild fiscal buffers and 
manage debt levels, highlight the importance 
of improving the value and effectiveness of 
every ringgit spent.
Stronger business capabilities should be accompanied by a more comprehensive and structured 
wage architecture. While the national minimum wage should remain the universal wage floor, 
consideration could be given to sectoral wage floors that better reflect productivity and wage 
conditions, particularly in persistently low-paid industries, without allowing any sectoral floor to 
fall below the national minimum. A structured annual wage progression framework could provide 
workers with clearer pathways for wage advancement based on experience, skills, competency, 
responsibilities and productivity. A wider coverage of collective agreements would complement this 
framework by providing an extensive channel for employers and workers to operationalise industry- 
and occupation-specific wage ladders, including appropriate annual increments, career pathways 
and productivity-linked wage adjustments.
Conclusion
Uplifting wages in labour-intensive MSMEs must be made the primary focus to generate a broad-
based 'big-push' impact in rebuilding the wage ladder, enabling more workers to benefit from 
higher-value activities and meaningful wage progression. This requires an integrated framework 
connecting both sides of the labour market. The demand-side interventions should support 
firms through industrial upgrading to create high-value jobs, while supply-side measures should 
strengthen skills, job matching and career progression. Productivity must serve as a bridge 
between the measures by converting stronger business capabilities and a more competent 
workers into higher output, greater competitiveness and sustainable wage growth. Therefore, 
strong foundations can expedite the development of a more progressive wage-ladder structure in 
Malaysia, ensuring that workers gain a sustainable income growth in line with the aspirations of 
Ekonomi MADANI.
Strategic Initiatives – 
Budget 2027
Budget 2027, the fifth MADANI Budget and 
the second under the Thirteenth Plan, marks a 
pivotal step in advancing the Ekonomi MADANI 
framework while striking a balance between 
addressing the immediate needs of the rakyat 
and facilitating business activities. Building 
on the reform momentum, the Budget will 
focus on strengthening Malaysia’s economic 
resilience and competitiveness while ensuring 
the benefits of growth are broadly shared. 
Strategic initiatives will be anchored on driving 
reform in governance to enhance institutional 
effectiveness and fiscal sustainability, raising 
the ceiling through higher-value economic 
activities and quality investments as well as 
raising the floor by improving living standards 
and social protection.

feature article 1.2 
The Role of Government Measures in Cushioning Rising Living Costs
Introduction
As a small and open economy, Malaysia is exposed to heightened global uncertainties, including 
volatility in global energy prices. Focusing on the price transmission channel, any disruption could 
raise import and production costs, exert upward pressure on consumer prices and, in turn, weigh 
on business activities and household spending. Nonetheless, Malaysia’s position as a net energy 
exporter provides buffers against the impact in the domestic market. Against this backdrop, this 
article examines:
 • the impact of higher global oil prices on producer and consumer prices; 
 • Government measures in buffering the impact of higher pump prices on households; and
 • the role of cash assistance in cushioning household expenditures.
Transmission Channel on Prices 
Higher global crude oil prices would increase domestic production costs through rising energy and 
intermediate input costs. These increased costs are subsequently reflected in the Producer Price 
Index (PPI) before being transmitted to the Consumer Price Index (CPI), particularly for food items 
and transport-related services that rely on energy and imported inputs. Given the role of Brent 
as the global crude oil benchmark, Brent is used to analyse the pass-through effects on domestic 
inflation. Figure 1.2.1. illustrates the transmission of external cost pressures through domestic 
production costs to consumer prices.

chapter 1   economic management and prospects
economic outlook   2027

Note: USD20 represents the difference between the Brent crude estimate in the Economic Outlook 2026 and the current price estimate

FIGURE 1.2.2. Macroeconomic Impact on PPI and CPI
0.74 ppt
0.14 ppt

CONTAINED COST PASS-THROUGH
The effect on CPI is only about
20%
of PPI
An empirical analysis was conducted to assess the transmission of higher crude oil prices to the 
PPI and CPI. In the short-run, the results indicate that an increase of USD20 in the Brent crude 
oil price will cause both PPI and CPI to increase by 0.74 percentage points (ppt) and 0.14 ppt, 
respectively. Figure 1.2.2. shows that the effect of pass-through in CPI is only about 20% of the PPI, 
suggesting that the transmission of higher production costs to consumer prices is contained.   
Further analysis indicates that the pass-through effect from producer prices to consumer prices 
experiences a sharp increase within the first four months before rising gradually, reaching around 
40% in the long-run, as highlighted in Figure 1.2.3. This relatively contained transmission is 
consistent with empirical evidence for Malaysia, which shows that consumer prices tend to respond 
less strongly to oil price shocks than producer prices, partly reflecting the role of energy subsidies 
and price-related measures in mitigating the impact on consumer prices (Husaini et al., 2019; Sek, 
2017). These findings demonstrate the role of Government interventions in cushioning external cost 
pressures and limiting the extent to which higher production costs are passed on to consumers.

chapter 1   economic management and prospects
economic outlook   2027
36
While the cost pass-through to consumer prices is relatively contained, households may still 
experience varying degrees of financial pressure depending on income levels and expenditure 
patterns. The transmission of higher production costs to consumer prices may be contained 
following various Government interventions such as fuel price stabilisation as well as targeted cash 
and subsidy assistance to enhance households’ resilience.
Government Measures to Cushion the Impact of Rising Living Costs
The Government has adopted a broad and complementary set of measures to cushion the impact 
of rising living costs and safeguard household wellbeing, particularly during periods of heightened 
external cost pressures. These measures operate through two main channels: price stabilisation 
measures which help moderate increases in the price of essential goods and services; and 
targeted assistance which provides direct financial support to improve the remaining income1 of 
eligible households and vulnerable groups. Together, these interventions help preserve households’ 
purchasing power by addressing both the increase in expenditure and strengthening the financial 
capacity to absorb higher living costs. 
Price stabilisation measures, particularly capping the prices of RON95 and diesel, help moderate 
the transmission of higher global energy prices to the costs of domestic essential items, as 
shown in Figure 1.2.4. This is particularly important for lower-income households, which have 
smaller income buffers and less capacity to absorb increases in essential expenditure. The price 
stabilisation measures have helped contain household expenditure pressures at the source, 
despite an increase of nearly RM40 billion in the Government's fiscal obligation by maintaining the 
subsidised pump price at RM1.99 per litre.

Targeted assistance of RM15 billion allocated for Sumbangan Tunai Rahmah (STR) and Sumbangan 
Asas Rahmah (SARA) in 2026 strengthens households’ financial capacity to cope with higher living 
costs. The household analysis in Figure 1.2.5. shows that Government assistance increases the 
remaining income of households after meeting the consumption expenditure. As the group with 
the smallest income buffers, Q1 households experienced a substantial increase in remaining 
income following the receipt of Government assistance. Nevertheless, the relative impact becomes 
progressively smaller for higher-income groups, reflecting greater capacity to absorb additional 
costs. 

chapter 1   economic management and prospects
economic outlook   2027
38
Universal access to affordable education 
and healthcare is fundamental in elevating 
living standards and driving social mobility. 
Under Budget 2027, investment in education 
will prioritise enhancing access to quality 
learning and advancing educational 
outcomes. Education reform, led by the 
National Education Council in line with the 
Thirteenth Plan, will emphasise literacy 
and numeracy, TVET, STEM, digital and AI 
capabilities, language proficiency and character 
development, while narrowing education gaps 
across regions and socioeconomic groups. 
Emphasis will be placed on mandating early 
childhood education to foster foundational 
cognitive development. Given the varying 
needs of localities nationwide, construction 
of schools will be instituted as a mandatory 
requirement in large-scale housing projects. 
Furthermore, digital and AI capabilities will be 
integrated across teaching and learning levels, 
complemented by stronger industry-academia 
collaboration and work-based learning models 
to prepare a future-ready workforce capable of 
sustaining productivity as the population ages.
Simultaneously, healthcare measures will 
focus on enhancing access, affordability and 
overall system capacity, reducing congestion 
at public facilities and strengthening the 
health workforce, while containing rising 
medical costs. In this regard, the Ministerial 
Joint Committee on Private Healthcare Costs 
will continue to lead integrated public-
private efforts to address rising treatment 
costs. Sustained capital investment in public 
healthcare infrastructure, primary and 
preventive care as well as AI-enabled electronic 
medical records remains central to healthcare 
system advancement. The adoption of Internet 
of Things (IoT) solutions will also be intensified 
to improve the efficiency and responsiveness 
of healthcare delivery. These initiatives will 
be reinforced by expanded public-private 
partnerships for high-value medical services 
and structural reforms in health financing to 
meet evolving demographic and healthcare 
demands.
Improving wage-setting mechanisms and 
employability remains an important component 
of labour market reforms. Budget 2027 will 
focus on key measures including accelerating 
industry shifts, particularly among MSMEs, 
towards high value-added activities through 
greater technology adoption, digitalisation 
and integration into global supply chains, 
making MSMEs a source of high-paying 
jobs. Synchronised collaboration between 
industries and educational institutions will 
also be reinforced, including through industry-
led TVET, apprenticeships, reskilling and 
Conclusion
Global uncertainties increase external cost pressures on Malaysia’s industries and consumers 
due to higher global oil and commodity prices. Given that the impact of higher living costs is 
heterogeneous across households, the household-level microsimulation indicates that Government 
assistance provides additional relative support for lower-income households. Overall, the findings 
highlight the complementary nature of the Government’s policy response in safeguarding the 
livelihoods of households, particularly during periods of elevated cost pressures. Price stabilisation 
measures help contain the increase in household expenditure through the price channel, while 
targeted assistance strengthens household capacity by improving remaining income. By combining 
these two approaches, the Government helps ease financial burdens, sustain consumption and 
reduce the risk of a significant decline in living standards, particularly for vulnerable groups.

economic outlook   2027

1 The increment should be reasonable, incorporating inflation and the living-wage concept. For instance, the indicative adjustment could be set around 3%–5% 
annually. This is broadly in line with the findings from Malaysian Employers Federation salary survey, which reported average salary adjustments of around 5% for 
executives and non-executives. The average wage adjustment across the board for employees covered under collective agreements was around 5% in 2024.
upskilling as well as AI literacy programmes, 
to ensure efficient talent development and job 
matching, while efforts to translate productivity 
gains into sustainable wage progression 
will be strengthened. Furthermore, higher 
wages should be recognised as a catalyst 
for sustainable business growth by boosting 
worker motivation and productivity. Employers 
are encouraged to prioritise local workers for 
job opportunities commensurate with skills 
and qualifications. At the same time, excessive 
reliance on low-skilled foreign labour will 
continue to be reduced through automation 
and better workforce planning.
In increasing employees’ purchasing power and 
enhancing domestic demand, the Government 
will continue undertaking comprehensive 
wage adjustments through reviews of the 
statutory minimum wage and the living wage 
as well as supporting performance-based 
wage mechanisms. Tripartite cooperation will 
be strengthened through social dialogues to 
deliberate on compulsory structured annual 
wage increment1.
The living wage adopted by GLICs and GLCs 
will also be revised and is expected to provide 
a benchmark for other corporate sectors, 
particularly in setting minimum starting 
salaries for graduates. Collective agreement 
coverage must be expanded to strengthen 
workers’ bargaining power leading to improved 
employment terms. Meanwhile, strategies 
to raise women's participation in the labour 
market through quality and affordable 
childcare, flexible work arrangement and 
return-to-work support will continue to be 
prioritised under Budget 2027. These strategic 
measures will strengthen labour market 
resilience and contribute to achieving the 
targets of 45% CE to GDP and 60% female 
labour force participation under the Ekonomi 
MADANI framework.
Conclusion
Malaysia enters the next phase of economic 
reform from a position of resilience. Supported 
by sound economic management, robust 
domestic demand and ongoing structural 
reforms outlined in Budget 2027, the economy 
is expected to grow between 4.2% and 5.2% 
in 2027. The priority ahead is an economic 
growth that is of a higher-value, with greater 
productivity and inclusivity. Meanwhile, 
investment must generate stronger domestic 
capabilities, deepen linkages with local firms, 
create high-paying jobs and improve living 
standards. 
Budget 2027 will ensure households are 
protected through targeted assistance, 
complemented by better access to 
infrastructure, education, healthcare, mobility 
and social protection. Achieving these 
outcomes demands fiscal discipline, sound 
governance and credible institutions. Policy 
commitments must translate into timely 
implementation, clear accountability and 
measurable outcomes. The Ekonomi MADANI 
framework will continue to serve as a coherent 
delivery agenda, raising the national potential, 
while improving the rakyat’s living standards.

chapter 2
Global Economic Outlook
Overview
Global economic growth is expected to remain 
resilient in the near term, balancing the severe 
impacts of the West Asia Crisis against a 
booming technology sector. The closure of the 
Strait of Hormuz has triggered energy supply 
shocks, driving global inflation higher and 
heavily burdening energy-importing economies. 
However, massive ongoing investments in 
artificial intelligence (AI), technology and digital 
infrastructure are offsetting these negative 
effects and keeping global employment 
steady. This situation is creating a diverging 
global economy where tech-heavy and 
energy-producing economies are experiencing 
stronger expansion, while economies reliant on 
imported energy and traditional manufacturing 
are facing constraints.
Global growth is projected to improve and 
inflation will ease as energy-related pressures 
gradually subside. While global trade is 
anticipated to remain fragmented due to 
ongoing geopolitical tensions and changes 
in the supply chain, the continued expansion 
of AI, technology and digital investments is 
expected to support economic growth. This 
tech-focused future will help to reshape the 
labour market, shifting demand toward high-
skilled workers. In essence, economies that are 
highly integrated into the technology supply 
chain and attain energy security will thrive, 
while economies dependent on traditional 
manufacturing industries will face challenging 
economic prospects. 

chapter 2   global economic outlook
economic outlook   2027
50
Global Economy 2026
Global economy growth is expected to remain 
resilient in 2026, despite geopolitical tension, 
energy disruptions and uncertainties in global 
trade. Global GDP growth is projected at 
3% in 2026, mainly supported by continued 
investment in AI, technology and digital 
infrastructure. Nevertheless, growth advantages 
will likely remain unequally distributed across 
economies, driven by structural differences in 
economic vulnerability to geopolitical conflicts, 
energy supply shocks and shifts in the global 
technology cycle.
 
In 2026, the global economy is facing 
significant pressure from the West Asia Crisis, 
mainly from energy supply disruptions. Global 
energy market volatility has escalated following 
the closure of the Strait of Hormuz, driving 
energy prices upward. These elevated energy 
expenses weigh on commercial operations 
by increasing production and transport costs, 
while concurrently dampening corporate 
activity and eroding domestic purchasing 
power. Energy-importing economies are 
particularly vulnerable, while economies with 
stronger energy supplies or technology sectors 
are in a better position to absorb the shock. 
Despite these pressures, strong investment 
in AI, technology and digital infrastructure is 
providing an important source of support to 
the global economy.
 
Among the advanced economies, growth 
is expected to remain moderate at 1.7% as 
higher energy costs, inflationary pressures 
and geopolitical uncertainty affect domestic 
demand and business confidence. However, 
economies with strong technologies are 
expected to benefit from increasing investment 
in AI, technology and digital infrastructure. 
This causes a growing divergence between 
economies that are closely connected to the 
global technology value chain and those that 
remain dependent on traditional manufacturing 
industries and imported energy.
 
Growth in emerging markets and developing 
economies (EMDEs) is also expected to remain 
uneven at 3.8%. Energy-importing economies 
are anticipated to experience greater pressure 
from higher energy and transportation costs, 
while energy-producing economies may benefit 
from stronger energy revenues. Meanwhile, 
economies that are integrated into AI-
related supply chains are expected to have 
better growth prospects as global technology 
investment continues. 
 
The global unemployment rate is expected 
to record 4.9%, indicating that labour markets 
remain resilient despite challenging economic 
conditions. Inflation is expected to remain 
elevated due to higher energy and commodity 
prices. Global inflation is projected to rise to 
4.7%, reversing some of the gains made in 
containing inflationary pressures in previous 
years.

information box 2.1 
ASEAN POWER GRID: THE MALAYSIAN PERSPECTIVE
The energy sector is undergoing a significant transformation as countries seek to reduce their 
reliance on fossil fuels and accelerate the adoption of cleaner sources of electricity. Electricity 
demand is increasing rapidly driven by the expansion of artificial intelligence (AI), data centres, 
electric vehicles (EVs) and the electrification of industries traditionally dependent on fossil fuels. 
The scale of this transformation is particularly evident in Southeast Asia. According to the 
International Energy Agency (IEA), regional electricity demand increased by more than 7% in 2024, 
which is nearly twice the global average. Under the IEA’s current-policies scenario, electricity 
demand in the region is projected to grow by approximately 4% annually through 2035, rising from 
more than 1,300 terawatt-hours (TWh) to over 2,000 TWh as shown in Figure 2.1.1.
The rapid growth in electricity demand is creating a need for additional generation capacity 
and investment in electricity transmission and distribution networks across Southeast Asia. IEA 
estimates that ASEAN will require more than USD300 billion in investment in electricity grids 
between 2025 and 2040, representing a 72% increase from USD174 billion investment during the 
2009 – 2024 period. Regional cooperation is becoming increasingly important because ASEAN 
Member States (AMS) have different electricity demand profiles, power supply mixes and renewable 
energy (RE) potential. 
The ASEAN Power Grid (APG) was established as a regional initiative to strengthen electricity 
connectivity, enhance energy security and facilitate cross-border electricity trade among AMS. 
Although the APG has been a longstanding regional objective since the 1980s, recent development

economic outlook   2027
52
chapter 2   global economic outlook
indicates substantial progress of this initiative. These developments include the expansion of the 
Lao People’s Democratic Republic (Lao PDR)–Thailand–Malaysia–Singapore Power Integration Project 
(LTMS-PIP), progress towards a regional framework for subsea power cables and the establishment 
of new financing initiatives to support APG development.
The LTMS-PIP provides a practical example of progress in regional electricity integration. The 
project became operational in 2022 and enables electricity trading among Lao PDR, Thailand, 
Malaysia and Singapore through existing transmission networks. In 2025, the project entered its 
next phase, increasing the maximum electricity transfer capacity from 100 megawatts (MW) to 200 
MW, thus flowing from Lao PDR to Singapore through Thailand and Malaysia.
Malaysia plays an important role in this regional integration process. Its participation in the LTMS-
PIP provides experience in multilateral cross-border electricity trading. Malaysia also contributed 
efforts to develop financing mechanisms for the APG during its 2025 ASEAN Chairmanship, 
including engagement with financial institutions, multilateral development banks (MDBs) and other 
capital providers. The ASEAN Power Grid Financing (APGF) Initiative was launched in Kuala Lumpur, 
Malaysia in October 2025 by the World Bank Group (WBG), the Asian Development Bank (ADB), 
ASEAN and the ASEAN Centre for Energy to support investment in regional electricity infrastructure.
However, the development of the APG also presents several challenges, including the need 
for stronger regulatory and technical coordination, harmonised arrangements for cross-border 
electricity trading, appropriate investment and business models as well as substantial financing for 
grid infrastructure. For Malaysia, these challenges are particularly relevant as the country seeks to 
balance regional electricity integration with domestic energy security, infrastructure requirements 
and the development of competitive cross-border electricity markets.
From Vision to Reality 
The APG is based on a simple idea that AMS can benefit by connecting their electricity systems. 
At present, AMS largely manage their electricity systems at the domestic level. However, demand 
and supply do not always move in the same direction. One economy may have excess electricity 
while another is facing a shortage. Cross-border connections can help balance these differences. 
An economy with surplus electricity can export it, while another can import power when it needs 
additional supply. 
The IEA report shows that ASEAN electricity consumption has increased ninefold since 1990 and 
is expected to continue growing at 3 – 4% a year through 2040. The APG requires substantial 
investment between 2025 and 2040 to expand and modernise its electricity-grid networks. Thus, 
APG cannot be developed through government funding alone and will require funding from 
development banks, commercial banks, private investors and other financial institutions.
Malaysia’s Strategic Advantage
Malaysia is not simply one node in the current APG map; it is the only economy spanning two of 
the grid’s three main subsystems, with Peninsular Malaysia positioning in the South subsystem and 
Sabah and Sarawak in the East subsystem. Geographically, Malaysia is a natural bridge between 
mainland Southeast Asia and Borneo. Peninsular Malaysia is already interconnected with Thailand 

economic outlook   2027
53
chapter 2   global economic outlook
and Singapore while Sarawak exchanges power with West Kalimantan. Malaysia is also a wheeling 
economy in ASEAN’s first multilateral electricity trade. In January 2026, LTMS-PIP enabled Malaysia 
to earn wheeling charges for the use of its transmission system independently of any energy sale.
Malaysia has been among the early AMS to participate actively in cross-border electricity trading. 
Its involvement began with the Lao PDR–Thailand–Malaysia Power Integration Project (LTM-PIP) 
in 2018 followed by the LTMS-PIP in 2022. Recently, Brunei Darussalam, Indonesia, Malaysia and 
the Philippines (BIMP) announced the BIMP Power Integration Project (BIMP-PIP) through a joint 
statement. The initiative aims to explore the potential for greater electricity interconnectivity across 
Borneo, strengthening regional energy cooperation and connectivity among the participating AMS.
Leveraging Malaysia’s Geographical Position
Malaysia’s unique geographical position gives it close physical links with several ASEAN electricity 
markets, including Brunei Darussalam, Indonesia, Singapore and Thailand. Existing and planned 
interconnections with these neighbouring systems create opportunities for Malaysia to play a larger 
role in regional electricity trade. Its position could allow electricity to move between different 
markets as more cross-border connections are developed. Malaysia is geographically divided into 
Peninsular Malaysia, Sabah and Sarawak with each region operating distinct energy networks 
and power infrastructure. Sarawak, in particular, has large hydropower resources and this gives 
Malaysia access to different sources of electricity that could support wider regional trade. 
Growing Electricity Demand
Malaysia is experiencing strong growth in electricity demand. In Peninsular Malaysia, peak 
electricity demand increased 4.9% from 20,066 MW in 2024 to 21,049 MW in 2025. This increase 
demonstrates the growing pressure on Malaysia’s electricity system and the need to ensure 
sufficient capacity to meet future demand. Malaysia therefore needs to expand and strengthen 
its electricity system even before considering additional demand that could arise from regional 
electricity exports. Tenaga Nasional Berhad (TNB) as the national electricity utility company, expects 
Peninsular Malaysia’s electricity demand to continue growing, forecasting approximately 141.87 TWh 
in 2026 and 148.66 TWh in 2027, representing an average annual growth rate of about 4.9%. These 
figures indicate that continued investment in electricity generation, transmission networks and grid 
infrastructure is necessary to ensure Malaysia meets growing domestic demand while maintaining 
reliable and secure electricity supply.
Infrastructure Readiness 
Malaysia’s electricity system is well-developed with an established power sector and extensive 
transmission infrastructure. TNB operates the National Grid in Peninsular Malaysia, which comprises 
132 kilovolts (kV), 275kV and 500kV transmission networks and is responsible for ensuring safe, 
reliable and economical operation of the electricity transmission system. However, the system will 
require expansion in line with increasing electricity demand, particularly in areas experiencing 
rapid growth in data centres and industrial projects. TNB reported that, as at December 2025, data 
centres accounted for 850 MW of actual load utilisation out of approximately 4.5 Gigawatts (GW) of 
capacity. TNB also reported projects in the pipeline that will require load utilisation up to 7.5 GW, 
demonstrating the potential for continued growth in electricity demand. 

economic outlook   2027
54
chapter 2   global economic outlook
These grid expansions are important not only for supporting regional electricity trade but also for 
meeting Malaysia’s own growing electricity needs. A reliable power system is also important for 
attracting new investment because energy-intensive industries such as data centres require scalable 
and reliable electricity connections. TNB’s continued investment in grid capacity and resilience is 
therefore important for supporting economic growth while maintaining the reliability and security 
of Malaysia’s electricity supply.
Renewable Energy Potential
Malaysia has tremendous potential for RE development, especially in solar power as Malaysia 
receives a high level of sunlight throughout the year and significant hydropower resources are 
abundant in Sarawak. The Ministry of Energy Transition and Water Transformation (PETRA) reported 
that RE accounted for 31% of the nation’s installed generation capacity by October 2025 as shown 
in Figure 2.1.2. Malaysia has set a target of achieving 40% RE capacity by 2035 and 70% by 2050. 
The development in RE could help Malaysia reduce its dependence on fossil fuels over time. It 
could also create more electricity that can be traded with neighbouring economies. RE is becoming 
more important for businesses as international companies are trying to reduce carbon emissions 
linked to their operations. Having access to cleaner electricity will make Malaysia more attractive to 
these companies.
Supportive Financial Ecosystem
Large energy projects require substantial funding, especially for transmission lines, RE projects, 
power plants and energy storage. In Malaysia, potential sources of financing are from the 
Government, local banks, development financial institutions, MDBs and private investors. For 
example, ADB has committed up to USD10 billion over the next 10 years to support the APG, 
including the strengthening of cross-border connections, National Grid upgrades and RE projects. 
The WBG has contributed USD12.7 million in grants for APG project preparation, including an initial 
USD5 million grant in 2024 and an additional fund of USD7.7 million in 2025.

Opportunities and Challenges
Electricity Trading Hub
One of the biggest opportunities for Malaysia to leverage on from this framework is electricity 
trading. Malaysia could potentially export electricity when it has surplus generation and import 
electricity when domestic supply is tight. This could create diverse opportunities for utilities 
and energy companies as well as financial institutions and other businesses to capitalise from 
this framework. Malaysia has started using mechanisms such as the Energy Exchange Malaysia 
(ENEGEM) to support cross-border RE sales to Singapore. The APG could allow such arrangements 
to grow over time and become part of a wider regional electricity market. 
Data Centre Growth
Data centres are one of the clearest examples of why Malaysia needs more electricity as Malaysia 
has attracted large amounts of investment in this sector. Recent data shows how electricity 
demand from data centres is increasing. By September 2025, 49 data centres had signed electricity 
supply agreements with TNB, representing a potential maximum demand of around 7.1 GW as 
shown in Figure 2.1.3. Actual electricity use from data centres had increased significantly, reaching 
approximately 710 MW, compared with 405 MW at the end of 2024. These figures show data 
centres are both an opportunity and a challenge for Malaysia as they bring significant economic 
activities but require a large and reliable electricity supply.

56
chapter 2   global economic outlook
PETRA has projected that electricity demand from data centres could reach 12.9 GW by 2030 and 
20.9 GW by 2040 and the need to mitigate these challenges. To manage this growth, Malaysia 
needs to expand its electricity grid while increasing the availability of RE. PETRA has been 
promoting the development of RE and stronger regional electricity interconnections as part of 
Malaysia’s energy transition. APG can support this strategy by enabling greater cross-border 
electricity trade, giving Malaysia access to additional sources of electricity from neighbouring 
economies. Malaysia’s participation in the LTMS-PIP is an example of this regional approach and 
forms part of the wider APG initiative.
Green Manufacturing
Malaysia is expected to benefit from the growing shift towards green manufacturing, as 
multinational companies increasingly consider the carbon intensity and reliability of electricity in 
deciding the location of production. This is particularly relevant to semiconductors, electronics, 
electrical equipment and EV-related manufacturing, which require substantial and reliable power. 
The Government is also expanding corporate access to renewable electricity through the Corporate 
Renewable Energy Supply Scheme (CRESS), which allows businesses to procure green electricity 
through the National Grid. These initiatives strengthen Malaysia’s attractiveness to manufacturers 
seeking to reduce their carbon emissions and meet increasingly stringent environmental, social and 
governance standard. 
APG further supports this opportunity by increasing cross-border electricity interconnections and 
providing access to a more diverse electricity source, including RE. Malaysia’s National Energy 
Transition Roadmap (NETR) explicitly identifies the APG as a pathway for Malaysia to participate in 
regional green energy trade. Greater regional integration could therefore complement Malaysia’s 
domestic RE expansion, improve electricity system resilience and support the growing power needs 
of high value industries. Combined with reliable grid infrastructure and accessible renewable 
electricity, the APG could help Malaysia position herself as a competitive hub for lower carbon, 
technology intensive manufacturing while attracting investment from companies seeking cleaner 
production locations.
Workforce Development 
The Energy Commission has projected that Malaysia will require approximately 62,000 highly-skilled 
workers by 2050 and has placed continuous training and reskilling at the centre of its Energy 
Competency Excellence ST (ST-ECE) initiative. Grid infrastructure of the future will necessitate the 
need for specialised proficiency beyond conventional roles like wiremen, chargemen and substation 
personnel to skilled workers knowledgeable in high-voltage direct current (HVDC) technology, 
subsea power cables, variable renewable integration, battery storage systems, advanced digital 
controls and operational technology cybersecurity. Many of these skills are within Malaysia’s 
technical and vocational education and training (TVET) ecosystem, whereby certified competency 
and industrial excellence are equally important as academic qualifications.
Regulatory Harmonisation
AMS do not operate under one common electricity law or regulatory system. Each AMS retains 
authority over its own electricity sector including generation, transmission, distribution, licensing, 
electricity pricing, market access and system operation. This means that the legal rules governing 
cross-border electricity trade can differ significantly from one economy to another. The APG 

economic outlook   2027
57
chapter 2   global economic outlook
framework recognises this issue and requires cooperation on national and regional legal and 
institutional frameworks, including regulatory harmonisation, electricity-trading arrangements and 
third-party access to transmission networks. One important legal issue that needs to be addressed 
is third-party access that concerns whether an electricity producer or trader from one economy 
should be allowed to use another economy’s transmission network to deliver electricity to a 
customer. To address this, ASEAN has specifically identified the need for arrangements governing 
third-party access and for a regional transmission-pricing framework. 
 
Another issue is electricity pricing and wheeling charges. Electricity prices are determined 
through different national market structures, subsidies, tariffs and regulatory mechanisms. Cross-
border trade therefore requires agreement on how the cost of transporting electricity across 
national transmission systems will be calculated. ASEAN studies have identified transmission 
pricing, wheeling charges and congestion management as important mechanisms for developing 
multilateral power trade. There are legal questions concerning contracts, liability and reliability, 
therefore, cross-border electricity agreements need to establish the responsible party in the event 
of electricity supply interruption, transmission infrastructure failure or any unfulfilled contractual 
obligations. ASEAN’s approach on multilateral power trading therefore emphasises not only physical 
interconnection but also appropriate institutional and contractual arrangements.
 
Further challenges faced by AMS are the different levels of electricity-market development and 
national policy priorities. Some markets are more liberalised, while others remain dominated by 
state-owned enterprises or centrally-regulated utility providers. ASEAN has therefore favoured a 
gradual and voluntary approach rather than immediately imposing one uniform electricity market 
on all AMS. APG’s implementation strategy requires stronger market rules, regional institutional 
coordination and regulatory frameworks to operationalise multilateral power trade. Recent ASEAN 
efforts also highlight differences in technical standards and regulatory arrangements as obstacles 
to expanding multilateral electricity trading.
Strengthening Grid Resilience
While regional electricity trade under the APG enhances Malaysia’s ability to manage rising 
electricity demand, the increasing interconnectedness of power systems also makes grid resilience 
a critical priority. Malaysia therefore needs to maintain adequate domestic generation capacity, 
robust transmission infrastructure and diversified supply routes to ensure that disruptions, 
congestion or failures along any single corridor do not cascade across the wider system. A resilient 
grid will allow Malaysia to manage fluctuations in RE generation and cross-border power flows 
more effectively, while supporting reliable participation in regional electricity trading without 
compromising domestic system stability or its capacity to fulfil regional export commitments. 
Conclusion
The ASEAN Power Grid is expected to become the regional electricity network by 2045, allowing 
AMS to trade electricity seamlessly and increase the use of RE. The APG is also expected to shift 
from a mainly bilateral to multilateral electricity trading arrangement, allowing economies with 
excess electricity to supply to other AMS in need. Malaysia’s strategic geographical position, well-
developed power infrastructure and resilient financial framework are uniquely suited to capitalise 
on regional energy grid integration. However, challenges such as regulatory disharmonisation 
and grid flexibility need to be managed. Sustained investments in energy storage, transmission 
networks, grid technology, and renewable power remain essential to achieve APG goals. Beyond 
boosting economic growth and reinforcing ASEAN’s energy security, the APG offers Malaysia a vital 
opportunity to solidify its position in the regional energy market.

than 3.8% recorded in the second quarter of

exacerbated inflationary pressures, posing 
risks to the growth outlook. However, the

net energy exporter since 2019. Overall, the 
second quarter slowdown suggests that the 
US economy remains resilient amid a more 
challenging external environment, particularly 
from higher energy prices and geopolitical 
uncertainty.

on investment and consumer confidence.

Global Outlook 2027 
Global growth is expected to improve to 3.4% 
in 2027, as impact from energy shock gradually 
subsides and investment in AI, technology 
and digital infrastructure continues to support 
economic activity. The stronger growth outlook 
suggests that technological investment could 
increasingly offset some of the negative effects 
of geopolitical tensions. AI-related investment 
is expected to expand beyond the major 
technology economies, supporting demand 
for AI, technology and digital infrastructure. 
However, growth is likely to remain uneven 
because economies with weak technology 
sectors may not benefit to the same extent. 
 
Global unemployment is expected to 
remain around 4.9%, indicating continued 
resilience in global labour markets. However, 
technological changes and AI are expected to 
gradually affect the structure of employment 
by increasing demand for high-skilled workers 
while reducing demand for low-skilled 
conventional jobs. Meanwhile, global inflation 
is projected to decline to 3.9% in 2027 as 
energy-related pressures ease. Nevertheless, 
inflation is expected to remain an important 
concern because geopolitical disruptions could 
continue to affect energy and commodity 
prices. 
 
Global trade is expected to remain affected by 
geopolitical tensions, supply chain disruptions 
and increasing economic fragmentation 
throughout 2026 and 2027. Businesses may 
continue to diversify their supply chains to 
reduce exposure to geopolitical risks and 
disruptions to major transport routes. At 
the same time, investment flows are likely 
to focus on technology, AI, energy security 
and strategic industries. These may create 
new opportunities for economies that can 
attract technology-related investment, while 
economies that are less integrated into these 
sectors could face weaker investment and 
growth prospects.
Looking ahead to 2027, the US economic 
growth is projected at 2.2% supported by 
favourable financial conditions and continued 
investments in AI, technology and digital 
infrastructures. The US economy has remained 
relatively resilient despite the ongoing conflict 
in West Asia, supported by its position as a net 
energy exporter and its prominent role in the 
global technology value chain. While household 
consumption is expected to moderate 
from previous years, business investment 
in equipment and intellectual property is 
anticipated to contribute significantly to 
growth.
 
Private consumption is expected to remain 
sensitive to broader macroeconomic and 
financial conditions. A potential pullback in 
AI technology-driven expectations heightens 
downside risks, while depleted policy buffers 
could further exacerbate these vulnerabilities.  
Inflationary pressures are expected to ease 
gradually as energy market disruptions 
subside. While the labour market is projected 
to remain resilient with the unemployment rate 
at 4.2%, inflation is expected to remain above 
the US Federal Reserve’s 2% target throughout 
2027.
Growth in the UK is expected to recover 
to 1.3% in 2027 as the disruptions from 
the global energy shock begin to dissipate. 
However, the unemployment rate is projected 
to continue its upward trajectory into 2027, 
reaching a higher rate of 5.3% as the labour 
market continues to experience lagging 
adjustments. Inflation is projected to record 
2.4% in 2027 as the effects of higher energy 
prices gradually fade and the softening labour 
market cools wage pressures. 
Downside risks to the UK’s economic outlook 
in 2027 remain predominant stemming from 
localised vulnerabilities as well as cascading 
global shocks. Domestically, the lingering 
impacts of elevated energy costs continue

in 2027, mainly supported by strengthening

pressures and worsen the country’s debt 
dynamics. On the external front, a prolonged 
conflict in West Asia risks disrupting energy 
supplies and trade, fueling inflation and 
hampering economic growth. Additionally, a 
potential disorderly correction in the AI market 
could disrupt financial flows and dampen 
broader market sentiment.

Conclusion
The global economy will remain resilient in 
2026 while its outlook in 2027 is vulnerable 
to geopolitical tensions, energy disruptions 
and increasing economic fragmentation. The 
West Asia crisis may continue to affect energy 
prices, inflation and global trade. Energy-
importing economies are particularly more 
vulnerable, while economies with strong 
technology sectors and highly integrated in the 
AI supply chains are expected to benefit more 
from continued technological investment.
 
Overall, the global economy in 2026 and 2027 
is anticipated to be shaped by two opposing 
factors, namely geopolitical and energy-
related pressures which could dampen growth 
while technological innovation including 
AI, technology and digital infrastructure 
are expected to be an important source of 
economic resilience. Nevertheless, sustainable 
global growth is contingent upon economies 
effectively managing inflation, energy security, 
financial stability and geopolitical risks.

chapter 3   macroeconomic outlook
chapter 3
Macroeconomic Outlook
Overview
Malaysia’s economic trajectory is reflected in 
real GDP growth of 5.7% in the first six months 
of 2026 and is forecast to expand by 4.7% in 
the second half of the year. Domestic demand 
continues to power the nation’s growth, driven 
by sustained private sector expenditure. 
Labour market conditions are likely to remain 
firm, supported by continued employment 
growth. Steady income growth, bolstered by 
sustained economic expansion and moderate 
headline inflation, is set to underpin private 
consumption. In addition, investment activities 
are expected to maintain its momentum as 
the gradual realisation of approved investment 
projects is set to provide a solid foundation 
for continued economic expansion. The robust 
implementation of national masterplans and 
strategic initiatives across both the private 
and public sectors will provide further impetus 
to economic activities. On the external 
front, Malaysia is poised to benefit from the 
continued technological advancements, which 
are reflected in export growth, notwithstanding 
persistent global uncertainties. 
At the sectoral level, most economic sectors 
are set to expand, with the services and 
manufacturing sectors remaining as the 
mainstay to overall growth. The services sector 
is expected to leverage on the Visit Malaysia 
2026 – 2027 Campaign (VM2026 – 2027) to boost 
inbound visitors, supported by diverse tourism-
related activities alongside continuous adoption 
of digital technologies. Within the manufacturing 
sector, the E&E industry is poised to benefit 
from the ongoing global semiconductor upcycle, 
driven by strong demand for AI-related products 
and digitalisation. A vibrant economy, coupled 
with steady domestic consumption, will continue 
to anchor Malaysia’s economic stability and 
maintain its growth momentum. Overall, the 
economy is projected to grow between 4.8% and 
5.3% in 2026 and expand between 4.2% and 
5.2% in 2027.
Economy in 2026 
Sectoral
Services Sector
Robust growth momentum 
The services sector expanded by 5.7% in the 
first half of 2026 and is expected to grow 
by 5.3% in the second half, underpinned 
by diverse tourism-related activities in 
conjunction with VM2026 – 2027, steady trade 
flows and an accelerating pace of digital 
adoption. Overall, the sector is projected to 
increase by 5.5% in 2026, with all subsectors 
registering growth.

74
chapter 3   macroeconomic outlook
The wholesale and retail trade subsector grew 
by 4.8% in the first half of 2026, sustained 
by steady household spending and tourism-
related activities following festive celebrations, 
public holidays and the mid-year school 
holidays. This growth was also partly driven 
by phase two of the salary adjustment under 
the Public Service Remuneration System 
(SSPA) for public servants. The subsector is 
anticipated to expand by 4.4% in the second 
half of the year supported by growth in all 
segments, particularly wholesale and retail 
trade segments, buoyed by higher spending 
from ongoing various targeted government 
assistance programmes. These include the 
expansion of the monthly Sumbangan Asas 
Rahmah (SARA), with a higher allocation by 
60% to RM8 billion (2025: RM5 billion) and 
eligible recipients increasing to 8.1 million 
(2025: 5.4 million), alongside Sumbangan Tunai 
Rahmah (STR) assistance and the expansion of 
the Program Jualan Rahmah MADANI (PJRM) 
to 30,000 programmes in 2026. Meanwhile, 
the growth of the motor vehicles segment is 
supported by new xEV1 model launches, the 
newly amended legislation on hire-purchase 
financing framework for motor vehicles and 
year-end promotional activities. Reflecting 
favourable market demand, the Malaysian 
Automotive Association (MAA) revised its 2026 
total industry volume (TIV) forecast upward 
to 800,000 units from 790,000 units, with xEV 
sales expected to account for about 120,000 
units. Overall, the subsector is projected to 
grow by 4.6% in 2026.
The information and communication subsector 
grew by 7.8% in the first half of 2026 
supported by the sustained demand for digital 
connectivity, cloud computing and other data 
intensive services reflecting the continued 
efforts across industries and businesses towards 
digitalisation as well as operationalisation 
of data centres. Meanwhile, major sporting 
events such as the 2026 FIFA World Cup, 2026 
Commonwealth Games and the 20th Asian 
Games Aichi-Nagoya 2026 are also expected to 
boost streaming subscriptions this year. The 
subsector is projected to increase by 7.9% in 
the second half of the year, driven by continued 
demand for ICT-related services and further 
expansion in data centre activities, resulting in 
an overall growth of 7.8% in 2026.
The transportation and storage subsector 
registered a growth of 7.8% in the first half of 
2026, attributed to strong trade performance 
resulting in increased cargo and logistic 
operations alongside various tourism- and travel-
related activities. The subsector’s performance 
was led by growth in the land and water 
transport segments, supported by higher 
traffic volumes on toll highways and increased 
port activities. In the second half of 2026, the 
subsector is expected to increase by 7.6%, 
particularly driven by growth in land transport 
on account of higher traffic volumes on toll

chapter 3   macroeconomic outlook
highways and increased rail ridership. Growth 
will be further supported by the implementation 
of BUDI MADANI Diesel (BUDI Diesel) and the 
revised BUDI MADANI monthly eligibility limit. 
These measures are expected to mitigate fuel 
cost pressures on eligible individuals, small 
businesses and logistics operators. Higher 
domestic travel during school holidays and 
festivities, together with ongoing tourism-related 
mobility under VM2026 – 2027, will also boost 
land transport activities. The water transport 
segment is expected to remain supported 
by sustained trade activities despite elevated 
shipping and logistics costs. Meanwhile, growth in 
the air transport segment may be affected due to 
persistent geopolitical uncertainties in West Asia, 
elevated fuel costs and potential disruptions from 
transboundary haze. Overall, the subsector is 
estimated to expand by 7.7% in 2026.
The real estate and business services 
subsector rose by 6.8% in the first half of 
2026 underpinned by the strong performance 
of professional, scientific & technical activities 
under the business services segment, following 
robust construction-related activities, including 
data centre projects. The subsector is expected 
to grow by 6.7% in the second half of the year 
buoyed by continued realisation of approved 
investment activities in tandem with ongoing 
industrial expansion particularly related to 
data centres and manufacturing as well as 
infrastructure development. Furthermore, the 
stable Overnight Policy Rate (OPR) is expected to 
stimulate demand for properties. For the year, 
the subsector is anticipated to expand by 6.8%.
The food & beverages and accommodation 
subsector increased by 7.3% in the first half 
of 2026, supported by sustained domestic 
demand and continued growth in visitor 
arrivals albeit at a slower pace. The subsector 
is expected to expand by 7.4% in the second 
half of the year, supported by tourism activities 
under VM2026 – 2027, with further momentum 
gains following various international events 
including meetings, incentives, conferences 
and exhibitions (MICE) programmes; Malaysia 
Agriculture, Horticulture and Agrotourism Show 
(MAHA 2026); Formula 1 Gulf Air Bahrain Grand 
Prix 2026 in Malaysia and PETRONAS Grand 
Prix of Malaysia 2026. These promotional 
activities are primed to stimulate visitor 
arrivals and tourism expenditure. Overall, the 
subsector is projected to grow by 7.4% in 2026.
For the first half of 2026, the finance and 
insurance subsector grew by 2.4%. The growth 
was supported by higher loan disbursements 
in the finance segment, in line with stronger 
financing demand, particularly for working 
capital purposes. Meanwhile, the insurance 
segment benefited from higher new business 
and gross direct premiums, particularly from 
investment-linked, medical and health as well 
as motor insurance, although higher claims 
partly offset the gains. In the second half 
of the year, the subsector is anticipated to 
register a growth of 2% in tandem with ongoing 
economic activities. The finance segment is 
expected to be supported by favourable credit 
conditions and continued financing demand, 
including ongoing investment activities in high 
value-added industries such as E&E and digital 
technologies. Meanwhile, household financing 
activity is also anticipated to remain supported 
by conducive labour market and stable 
financing conditions. The insurance segment 
is expected to benefit from sustained demand 
for insurance and takaful products, although 
medical cost pressures and higher claims 
severity may temper its performance. Overall, 
the subsector is estimated to register a growth 
of 2.2% in 2026.
The utilities subsector registered a growth of 
5.3% in the first half of 2026 driven by higher 
electricity demand which was attributed to 
increased industrial and commercial activities 
as well as data centre operations. In the 
second half of the year, the subsector is 
estimated to increase by 5.9% attributed to 
higher electricity and water consumption amid 
stronger industrial, commercial and economic 
activities particularly in E&E-related activities, 
together with the continued demand from data 
centres. Overall, the subsector is expected to 
grow by 5.6% in 2026.

The other services subsector grew by 6.1% 
in the first half of 2026, mainly supported 
by the private healthcare and other private 
services. The subsector is projected to grow by 
5.3% in the second half of the year, alongside 
vibrant entertainment, recreation and personal 
services activities. The private health services 
is expected to benefit from the Malaysia Year 
of Medical Tourism 2026 (MYMT 2026), backed

services is anticipated to remain favourable, 
supported by higher student enrolment, 
particularly among international students.

by 5.7% in 2026.

recorded a growth of 6.2% in the first half 
of 2026 underpinned by the second phase 
of salary adjustments for public servants 
under the SSPA. The subsector is expected 
to expand moderately by 4.7% in the second 
half, reflecting continued austerity measures 
implemented to prioritise operational savings 
in lieu of the energy crisis due to the 
protracted West Asia situation. The subsector is 
expected to register a growth of 5.4% for the 
whole year.
INFOrMatION BOX 3.1
More than a Destination: The Value of Tourism to Malaysia’s Growth
In collaboration with the Ministry of Tourism, Arts and Culture, Malaysia & Malaysia Tourism Promotion 
Board
Introduction
Tourism generates economic value through foreign and domestic spending, creates jobs, provides 
opportunities for local businesses as well as communities and improves public infrastructure. 
According to the World Bank (2026), the arrival of visitors for business or leisure purposes is a 
powerful economic driver for the national growth and livelihoods particularly in developing and 
middle-income economies. The World Tourism Barometer report by UN Tourism in September 2026 
stated that international tourist arrivals grew by 4.7% in 2025, with an estimated 1.5 billion arrivals 
worldwide and exceeding pre-pandemic levels. During the same period, international tourism 
receipts increased by 7.8%, reaching around USD1.9 trillion, reflecting robust global travel demand 
and higher visitor spending. The continued expansion of international tourism underscores the 
industry’s significant contribution to global economic activity, employment, trade and services.
Malaysia’s Tourism Overview 
Malaysia benefits from ongoing tourism and travel activities, particularly across high-touch services 
including retail, food and beverages as well as accommodation, in addition to employment, 
foreign exchange earnings and regional development. As the National Tourism Policy 2020 – 2030 
continues to revitalise the industry, the strategies under the Thirteenth Malaysia Plan, 2026 – 2030 
further accelerate tourism as one of the strategic sectors towards high-income and sustainable 
economic growth. The Visit Malaysia 2026 – 2027 Campaign (VM2026 – 2027) complements these 
efforts by boosting tourism activities nationwide. The national strategic framework for tourism 
development is illustrated in Figure 3.1.1.

ecONOMIc OUtLOOK   2027
78
chapter 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
Malaysia’s Recognition and Performance 
Globally, Malaysia’s value proposition lies in the ability to offer high value and diverse experiences 
without the premium price tag of competing destinations, anchored on these appeals:
 
• Cultural diversity and inclusivity - multicultural identity captured in “Malaysia Truly Asia” and the 
nation’s status as a global halal hub;
 
• World-class ecotourism and biodiversity - mega-biodiverse ecosystems and globally-recognised 
heritage sites;
 
• Premier niche offerings - medical and wellness tourism, business events covering meetings, 
incentives, conferences and exhibitions (MICE); and
 
• Connectivity, infrastructure and value - seamless logistics and value for money for 
accommodation, dining and attraction experiences at competitive rates.

Benchmarks Driving Malaysia’s Tourism Appeal
Malaysia’s international tourism proposition is recognised across global benchmarks, which 
highlight the country’s competitive strengths as shown in Figure 3.1.2. 
Tourism’s Contribution to the Economy 
As international borders reopened and travel abroad resumed after the COVID-19 pandemic, 
Malaysia’s tourism industry has steadily regained momentum since 2022, in tandem with higher 
visitor arrivals and increasing contributions to GDP, travel receipts and employment. In 2025, 
the tourism industry contribution returned to pre-pandemic levels at 15.9% of GDP while travel 
receipts accounted for 7.6% of nominal exports. The industry also accounted for about 3.7 million 
jobs, representing 22.1% of total employment, underscoring tourism’s significant contribution 
to Malaysia’s economic activity, services exports and employment. The summary of tourism’s 
contribution to the economy is shown in Figure 3.1.3, Figure 3.1.4 and Figure 3.1.5, respectively.

Multiplier Effects of Inbound Tourism
An assessment by the Ministry of Finance indicates that inbound tourism expenditure generates 
spillovers across a broad range of industries. Inbound visitors spending of RM10 billion will result 
in an additional RM6.8 billion gross output to the economy. The total gross output is estimated 
at RM16.8 billion, generated across 27 industries, representing an output multiplier of 1.68 times. 
Primary tourism-facing industries generate the highest output totalling RM11.4 billion, mainly in 
the transportation and storage, restaurants, accommodation as well as wholesale and retail trade 
as illustrated in Figure 3.1.9. Meanwhile, nearly a third of the economic impact is contributed 
by indirect and supporting industries including agriculture and manufacturing. The broad-based 
spillovers underscore the importance of maximising value capture from higher visitor arrivals, 
particularly by increasing spending and length of stay. The impact extends beyond tourism-facing 
industries through supply chain linkages, benefitting economic activity, employment, household 
incomes and services exports. Maximising this impact requires enhancing connectivity; improving 
tourism infrastructure, strengthening supply chain linkages and integrating local businesses directly 
into tourism activities.

Navigating Headwinds
While increasing overall arrival numbers remains essential, relying solely on volume exposes 
Malaysia to strategic vulnerabilities, requiring a concerted effort to address key operational 
and market challenges. Geographically, heavy source-market concentration leaves the industry 
vulnerable to localised economic shocks, while intense competition from neighbouring destinations 
necessitates continuous product diversification. Accessibility remains constrained by limited direct 
flights, volatile jet fuel prices, high airfares and airspace rerouting due to geopolitical tensions. 
Furthermore, a notable rise in excursionists limits yield per visitor across accommodation and 
extended service subsectors. Environmental risks threaten the sustainability of destinations 

ecONOMIc OUtLOOK   2027
83
chapter 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
including overtourism, habitat degradation and resource depletion across vulnerable ecosystems. 
Talent gaps also hinder service delivery across specialised segments such as luxury, healthcare, 
ecotourism and business events.
Conclusion
Malaysia has always captivated the world with its diversity, warmth and natural beauty. Yet, 
preserving that appeal in an unpredictable global landscape requires constant reinventing. As 
market dynamics shift, competitive advantage will belong to destinations that offer seamless 
connectivity, premium experiences and sustainable infrastructure. Stronger connectivity, 
digitalisation, upskilling, sustainability, coordination and quality investment will be essential to 
strengthen Malaysia’s tourism competitiveness and resilience as shown in Figure 3.1.10.
Translating potential into long-term economic yield will not only enable the nation to meet key 
economic targets but will establish a durable, world-class tourism model built for the decades 
ahead. In this pursuit, the VM2026 – 2027 will also serve as a strategic measure, leveraging on 
extended promotional timeframe to maximise international marketing impact. Malaysia can ensure 
its travel brand remains vibrant, resilient and uniquely “Truly Asia” and “Made by Malaysia” for 
generations to come.

Manufacturing Sector
Technology upcycles powering growth
The manufacturing sector grew by 6.6% in 
the first half of 2026, supported by expansions 
in both export- and domestic-oriented 
industries. Export-oriented industries, which 
accounted for 76.4% of the sector, recorded 
a growth of 7.5% mainly driven by the E&E 
segment, reflecting sustained demand for 
semiconductors and electronic components. 
This is in line with the worldwide expansion 
of AI applications and digital infrastructure. 
The petroleum, chemical, rubber and plastic 
products subsector also contributed to the 
export-oriented industries growth. Meanwhile, 
domestic-oriented industries, expanded by 
4.7%, supported by the recovery in transport 
equipment, other manufacturing and repair 
subsector, particularly the production of motor 
vehicles and related parts and accessories, 
alongside stable demand for construction-
related materials.
In the second half of 2026, the sector is 
expected to increase by 5.7%, reflecting the 
normalisation of export front-loading activities 
and lower domestic crude oil and condensate 
production. In addition, rising oil prices amid 
the West Asia conflict also contributed to the 
moderate growth of the sector. Within the 
export-oriented industries, the E&E segment 
remains a key driver of growth, supported 
by sustained demand for semiconductors, 
electronic and AI-related components in 
line with the global technology upcycle. 
Meanwhile, domestic-oriented industries are 
expected to benefit from consumer- and 
construction-related demand. Increased 
tourism activities during VM2026 – 2027 and 
sustained household spending continue to 
support demand for food and beverages 
subsector. In addition, the development of 
data centres, infrastructure and energy-related 
projects will generate demand for building 
materials.
The ongoing implementation of the New 
Industrial Master Plan 2030 (NIMP 2030) 
and the National Semiconductor Strategy 
(NSS) is expected to further enhance the 
sector’s competitiveness by strengthening 
industrial clusters and manufacturing 
capabilities, alongside greater adoption of 
digital technologies and automation. In line 
with these policies, local firms have expanded 
their capabilities into designing homegrown 
advanced chip and developing higher value-
added semiconductor within the global 
semiconductor value chain. Overall, the 
manufacturing sector is projected to record a 
growth of 6.2% in 2026.

FeatUre artIcLe 3.1
Strengthening Malaysia’s Manufacturing Resilience Amid Geopolitical 
Uncertainties in West Asia
Introduction
The Strait of Hormuz is one of the world’s most important energy chokepoints, while the 
Bab el-Mandeb Strait and the Suez Canal form part of a major trade corridor between Asia 
and Europe. The Strait of Hormuz links the Persian Gulf and the Gulf of Oman, serves as a key 
gateway connecting the Middle East with global markets and facilitating oil and gas exports. 
Meanwhile, Bab el-Mandeb Strait, connects the Gulf of Aden with the Red Sea, which leads to 
the Mediterranean Sea via Suez Canal. 
Data from the U.S. Energy Information Administration (EIA) in Figure 3.1.11 indicate a marked shift 
in energy flows along these routes amid geopolitical tensions in West Asia. Oil flows through the 
Strait of Hormuz averaged approximately 21 million barrels per day (mmb/d) in the first half of 
2025, equivalent to about 20% of global petroleum liquids consumption before declining sharply to 
an estimated 9.9 mmb/d in the first half of 2026. Over the same period, oil flows through the Suez 
Canal and SUMED Pipeline increased from 4.9 mmb/d to 5.8 mmb/d, while flows through the Bab 
el-Mandeb Strait rose from 4.2 mmb/d to 6.9 mmb/d. Liquefied natural gas (LNG) flows through 
the Straits of Hormuz also declined substantially, from approximately 11.4 billion cubic feet per day 
(bcf/d) to 4.1 bcf/d, as summarised in Table 3.1.12. Disruptions along these routes can force vessels 
to reroute, raise fuel and war-risk insurance costs, lengthen delivery times and increase congestion 
at transhipment hubs.
1 Analysis is based on Vortexa tanker tracking, World Bank and Panama Canal Authority data, using EIA conversion factors and calculations.
2 First-half figures are calculated as the average of the first and second quarters. LNG averages for the Bab el-Mandeb Strait are not presented due 
to incomplete quarterly observations. Total may not add up due to rounding.
FIGURE 3.1.1. Map Of Daily Transit Volumes Of Petroleum and Other Liquids through World 
Maritime Oil Chokepoints
Source: U.S. Energy Information Administration (EIA)
