Recent geopolitical tensions in West Asia, trade restrictions and disruptions to major shipping 
routes have increased uncertainties for global manufacturers. The immediate effects include 
greater volatility in energy and transport costs as well as interruptions to the availability and 
delivery of raw materials and intermediate inputs. Through global supply chains and domestic 
production linkages, these disruptions may have far reaching effects to manufacturers. 
In the case of Malaysia, the most direct exposure to potential disruptions in the Strait of Hormuz 
lies in its crude oil supply chain, where nearly 40% of the nation’s crude oil requirements transit 
through the chokepoint. Based on 2025 inbound trade volume data, Hormuz-linked countries 
accounted for 69.2% of crude oil imports, an estimated 13.5% of refined petroleum product imports 
and 6% of petroleum gas imports, including LNG as illustrated in Figure 3.1.2. Nevertheless, the 
effects extend beyond direct energy imports, with the Federation of Malaysian Manufacturers 
(FMM) June 2026 survey identifying freight and logistics costs as the key constraint affecting 
manufacturers, followed by raw material shortages as well as higher energy and fuel costs.

chapter 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
This article examines how geopolitical shocks can affect Malaysia’s manufacturing sector through 
energy markets, maritime logistics and global supply chains. It combines industry evidence with 
input-output (IO) analysis to identify sectoral exposure, estimate the potential impact on production 
costs and assess which industries could transmit shocks more widely across the economy. 
Manufacturing Performance and External Exposure
Despite the latest episode of geopolitical uncertainty, Malaysia’s manufacturing sector remained 
robust supported by sustained production and strong external demand. Manufacturing value-
added expanded by 6.6% in the first half of 2026, underpinned by stronger activity in E&E as well 
as petroleum, chemical, rubber and plastic products subsectors. This performance is aligned with 
total trade which increased by 22.4% to RM1,795.2 billion, supported by higher exports (27.5%) 
and imports (16.8%). These developments indicate sustained external demand for Malaysia’s 
manufactured products.
However, the sources of growth and external exposure varied between export- and domestic-
oriented industries. Export-oriented industries particularly E&E as well as petroleum and chemical 
products, continued to drive growth, supported by sustained external demand. The E&E industry 
benefited from continued investment in artificial intelligence (AI) and other technology-intensive 
activities which support demand for semiconductors and electronic components. Nevertheless, 
export-oriented industries could still be affected by demand and shipping disruptions. Meanwhile, 
domestic-oriented industries, including food processing, fabricated metal products and transport 
equipment were less affected by changes in external demand, but could still face input shortages 
and higher production costs.
The impact of these risks on production depends on a firm’s sourcing flexibility, inventory 
coverage and the availability of substitutes for critical inputs. Production is particularly at risk 
when specialised inputs have limited alternatives since a temporary delay can halt operations and 
disrupt other firms dependent on the affected output. These interdependencies can amplify a 
localised external shock and lead to wider supply chain disruptions across Malaysia’s manufacturing 
ecosystem.
Supply Chain Ripple Effects on Malaysian Manufacturers
As illustrated in Figure 3.1.3, disruptions to energy supplies, shipping routes and critical inputs 
can spread rapidly across Malaysia’s interconnected manufacturing industries. Shortages such as 
petrochemical feedstocks, specialty chemicals, metals and packaging materials will raise costs and 
constrain production in downstream industries including plastics, rubber, E&E and food processing. 
The FMM’s survey illustrates the resulting financial strain among manufacturers, with 74% of 
respondents reported production costs increasing between 10% and 50% above pre-conflict levels, 
while 58% of respondents claimed to be operating at or below break-even on affected product 
lines. Firms might be able to absorb part of the initial increase due to limited pricing flexibility, but 
prolonged disruptions could force firms to pass the higher cost to consumers, leading to higher 
prices, weakening export competitiveness and delaying investments.

Input-Output Analysis
The IO analysis is used to examine the impact of external shocks to Malaysia’s manufacturing sector 
through an industry’s direct exposure to energy and imported-input; the estimated transmission of 
cost shocks; and its potential to spread disruptions through domestic production linkages. Using 
Malaysia’s 2021 IO table and fi xed production coeffi  cients, the analysis traces how initial cost 
pressures are transmitted across industries.

As shown in Figure 3.1.4, petroleum refi ning records the highest energy exposure, accounting for 
approximately 43% of gross output3. This is followed by basic chemicals at 40.4%, refl ecting the 
sensitivity of both industries to changes in energy and feedstock costs. Import exposure is highest 
for engines and machinery at 46.8%, followed by plastics products (35.8%), fertilisers (32.6%), general 
machinery (29.8%) and electronics (27.6%) industries. Meanwhile, electronics industry has relatively 
low direct energy exposure of 2.5%, indicating that its vulnerability arises mainly from higher 
imported-input costs and disruptions to global supply chains.
Based on the combined shock scenario4 in Figure 3.1.5, petroleum refi ning records the largest 
estimated production cost eff ect among manufacturing industries at approximately 21.9%, refl ecting 
its direct exposure to the assumed shocks and the transmission of cost pressures through domestic 
production linkages. This is followed by basic chemicals at 10.2%, driven mainly by cost pressures 
transmitted through the energy and logistics network, while the estimated eff ects on rubber processing 
(8.2%), other chemical products (8.1%), basic non-ferrous metals (8.1%), fertilisers (7.3%), and engines 
and machinery (6.8%) are driven mainly by imported-input costs. The fi ndings show that external cost 
pressures are not confi ned to industries directly exposed to energy prices but also aff ect downstream 
manufacturers through their dependence on domestic suppliers and imported materials.

The assessments also show different patterns of external exposure. Communication equipment 
and consumer electronic is highly dependent on imported-inputs and export markets, while 
rubber gloves and other chemical products are highly export-oriented and strongly linked to their 
suppliers. Basic chemicals have relatively low import dependence but a high export share of 60.4%, 
supported mainly by strong backward and forward linkages. Plastics products have relatively high 
import dependence, but the weaker domestic production linkages suggest that the effects may be 
more concentrated within the industry. Overall, the wider economic impact of a disruption depends 
not only on an industry’s external exposure but also on its size and linkages within the economy.

ecONOMIc OUtLOOK   2027
92
chapter 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
Government Responses 
In light of the heightened tensions, the Government reactivated the National Economic Action 
Council (MTEN) in March 2026, to coordinate a whole-of-government response to the economic 
repercussions and global supply chain disruptions. The Government’s immediate response focused 
on sustaining business liquidity and securing essential supplies. Bank Negara Malaysia (BNM) 
introduced the RM5 billion SME Stabilisation Relief Facility to provide working capital financing to 
viable SMEs affected by the West Asia conflict. As of August 2026, approximately RM2.8 billion was 
approved to more than 4,900 SME accounts. More targeted assistance was provided through an 
additional RM5 billion guarantee facility under Syarikat Jaminan Pembiayaan Perniagaan (SJPP) with 
financing coverage to 80% and the guarantee tenure extended to 10 years for micro, small and 
medium enterprises affected by the West Asia conflict. The facility was further complemented by 
the broader RM10 billion BNM–CGC Guarantee Scheme, to support SMEs productivity, innovation 
and resilience. 
In the energy sector, PETRONAS leveraged its integrated value chain to support domestic petrol 
and diesel supplies while coordinating with the Government and other industry players. Diplomatic 
engagement also facilitated the passage of Malaysia-linked vessels through the Strait of Hormuz, 
including a PETRONAS-chartered tanker carrying crude oil to Pengerang. These measures helped 
reduce the immediate supply risks and maintain the availability of essential fuels.
The Government is assessing the impact to formulate appropriate measures to ease cost pressures 
to the affected industries, particularly consultations with the plastics industry are being undertaken 
due to its important linkages with food packaging, E&E, automotive and medical devices industries. 
Concurrently, continuous engagements with other affected subsectors players were also conducted 
as part of the Government’s effort to strengthen the long-term resilience of the manufacturing 
ecosystem. 
Way Forward
While the Government’s responses helped address immediate financing and supply pressures, a 
more systematic approach is required to identify critical inputs, monitor concentrated supply risks 
and coordinate early intervention. The immediate priority is to enhance visibility over critical inputs 
and concentrated sources of supply. A targeted supply chain mapping programme should identify 
common upstream suppliers, source country concentration, inventory coverage, lead times and 
the availability of technically acceptable substitutes. The programme should focus on industries 
with both extensive production linkages and high exposure, including refined petroleum, basic 
chemicals, electronic components and selected machinery industries. A shared risk dashboard could 
then support early warning signals and a more timely coordination and necessary action to be 
taken by government agencies as well as the industries.
Further policy measures should facilitate alternative sourcing as preparation for potential disruption 
arising from any crises. This includes pre-qualifying substitute suppliers and materials, accelerating 
technical approvals where safety and quality requirements are met, and utilising regional supplier-
matching platforms. For critical inputs with limited substitutes and high disruption costs, firms 
should be encouraged to establish appropriate inventory buffers and strategic business viability. 
Simultaneously, public support should be targeted, finite and conditional to limit costs as well as 
minimise distortions. 

ecONOMIc OUtLOOK   2027
93
chapter 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
Over the long term, Malaysia should strengthen its domestic and regional capacity in selected 
intermediate products where supply is highly concentrated and feasible alternatives remain limited, 
rather than pursue broad-based import substitution. Consistent with the New Industrial Master 
Plan 2030 (NIMP 2030), support for petroleum and chemicals products, as well as E&E, should 
prioritise higher-value-added activities, technological upgrading and supplier development in areas 
where Malaysia can build competitive capabilities. The country should also deepen its participation 
in complementary ASEAN production networks by working together with regional partners to 
harmonise product standards, streamline rules of origin and improve customs procedures to 
facilitate the movement of intermediate inputs across the region. Greater coordination of cross-
border investment could further encourage complementary production capabilities among ASEAN 
member states and provide manufacturers with a broader and more diversified regional supplier 
base.
Conclusion
Malaysia’s strong manufacturing sector and trade performance are exposed to vulnerabilities 
arising from its deep integration with global energy, logistics and production networks. The 
disruptions can spread beyond directly exposed industries due to the economic importance and 
extensive linkages with downstream producers. While immediate measures provide necessary 
short-term support, long-term policies should focus on increasing domestic value creation in 
critical production segments and deepening complementary manufacturing networks within 
ASEAN. Investment incentives should be aligned with productivity gains, technological upgrading 
and broader industrial spillovers. These measures will strengthen Malaysia’s capacity to withstand 
external shocks without compromising the nation’s trade openness and global integration that 
underpin manufacturing competitiveness.
Agriculture Sector
Anchoring food security amid subdued growth
The agriculture sector contracted by 0.7% in 
the fi rst half of 2026, weighed down by lower 
output in most subsectors except livestock 
and other agriculture. The oil palm subsector 
declined by 2.8% attributed to lower fresh fruit 
bunches (FFB) yield from Peninsular Malaysia. 
The weaker yield performance was partly due to 
a decline in the mature area which outweighed 
gains in Sabah and Sarawak. In addition, the 
rubber subsector also contracted by 16.3% 
mainly due to shortages of rubber tappers 
and adverse weather conditions that distracted 
tapping activities despite favourable market 
conditions. Similarly, both the fi shing as well 
as forestry and logging subsectors contracted. 
In contrast, the livestock and other agriculture 
subsectors grew by 5.6% and 1.3%, respectively, 
partially off setting the sector’s decline.
For the second half of 2026, the sector is 
forecast to decline by 3.3%. The oil palm 
subsector is expected to decline slightly due to 
weather-related disruptions. Although FFB yield 
production in 2025 was high, the biological 
yield cycle may limit production in 2026. In 
addition, field-level variation in harvesting 
conditions and inefficient crop recovery 
could contribute to overall lower yield. The 
rubber subsector is also expected to record 
a contraction as weather-related risks and 
higher production costs will constrain output. 
The growth of fishing as well as forestry and 
logging subsectors are also anticipated to 
remain subdued. On the other hand, livestock 
and other agriculture subsectors will continue 
to grow, underpinned by improved in farm 
productivity, higher poultry production and 
large-scale rollout of MARDI’s new grain corn 
varieties known as MZ1 and MZ2. Meanwhile, 
fruits and vegetables segments will contribute 
to the growth of other agriculture subsector.

Growth driven by resilient domestic demand

economic growth, bolstered by commendable 
private sector spending. In the first half 
of 2026, domestic demand registered a 
5.1% growth and is expected to maintain 
its momentum in the second half of the

estimated to increase by 5.4%. Propelled by 
both consumption and investment spending, 
private and public sector expenditures are 
estimated at 5.6% and 4.9%, respectively, in 
2026.

the first half of 2026 and is anticipated to 
grow by 5% over the full year. This reflects

favourable domestic economic activities and 
steady labour market conditions. Income 
growth has been further supported by wage-

of the civil service salary adjustments under 
the SSPA. Household consumption continues 
to be supported by government measures to

is expected to cushion households against

activities are expected to further increase

FeatUre artIcLe 3.2
Beyond the Headline: Transforming Investment into Economic 
Resilience
Background
Malaysia is shifting beyond headline investment fi gures towards a stronger focus on integrating 
technology as well as economic complexity. Supported by strategic policy frameworks such as the 
National Investment Aspirations (NIA), New Industrial Master Plan 2030 (NIMP 2030) and the National 
Semiconductor Strategy (NSS), this shift drove approved investments to a historic-high in 2025, refl ecting 
Malaysia’s growing position in technology-intensive and higher value-added economic activities.
High investment particularly in technology sectors is generally expected to enhance economic development in 
several stages. It starts with potential domestic and foreign capital infl ows, which are converted into approved 
investment, guided by clear government policies and incentives. This is further translated into project 
implementation, technology adoption and stronger supply chain links. Once a project commences, it typically 
uplifts productivity through technological capacity, upskilling and innovation, thereby accelerating domestic 
fi rms to higher-value segments of global supply chains, while strengthening export competitiveness and 
economic resilience. The investment transmission mechanism is refl ected in Figure 3.2.1.
Source: Ministry of Finance, Malaysia (various references)

chapter 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
Malaysia’s investment profile is relatively balanced, with foreign and domestic capital contributing 
almost equally to approved investments. As shown in Figure 3.2.2, domestic investment (DI) made 
up RM222.4 billion or 51.6% of total approved investments in 2025, while foreign investment (FI) 
contributed the remaining share at RM208.7 billion. Robust FI continues to serve as an anchor 
for technology transfer and global market access, while the expanding share of domestic capital 
reflects heightened confidence among local investors in the nation’s long-term economic growth 
trajectory. The balanced mix minimises exposure to external shocks, as DI provides a strong 
buffer against FI. Delving into the details, this article highlights two focus areas shaping Malaysia’s 
current investment landscape. 
Synergy between Private and Public Investment
Figure 3.2.3 and Figure 3.2.4 illustrate the dominance of private investment over public investment 
indicating positive investor sentiment. The increasing trend of private investment share reflects 
its leading role in supporting the country’s economic development. Meanwhile, public investment 
serves a crucial role as a catalyst in key industrial areas and de-risking new growth drivers, paving 
the way for greater private investment and supporting long-term growth. Notwithstanding this 
trend, there is a recurring debate concerning the relationship between private and public sector 
investment.

chapter 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
Analysis 1: The Dynamics of Private and Public Capital Formation
This section analyses the relationship between private and public investment, evaluating whether 
public investment acts as a driver or a deterrent to private capital investment. Empirical findings 
indicate that a 1% increase in public investment is associated with an immediate 0.57% contraction 
in private investment in the short run. The inverse relationship suggests the crowding out of 
private investment due to direct competition for raw materials, specialised equipment and skilled 
labour which could reduce profitability (Sundararajan & Thakur, 1980; Cavallo & Daude, 2008). In 
addition, the heavy financing requirements of large-scale public initiatives tighten credit conditions 
and elevate borrowing frictions, which prompt private companies to delay their investment (Islam & 
Nguyen, 2026). 
Meanwhile, the analysis indicates a crowding-in effect, where a 1% increase in public investment 
is associated with a 0.48% rise in private capital formation in the long run. This shows that public 
investment projects, such as roads, ports, electricity and digital networks, act as a critical enabler 
to private sector activities and stimulate broader economic activity particularly investment in special 
economic zones or industrial parks. The positive correlation states that stronger economic growth 
encourages businesses to invest more, while direct government spending plays a supportive role 
(OECD, 2024).
Analysis 2: Private Investment Transmission to GDP
While a growing economy naturally attracts greater capital inflows, private investment is vital in 
stimulating the domestic economy through capital formation, job creation, technology adoption and 
supply-chain integration.1 However, the magnitude and sustainability of these impacts on Malaysia’s 
real GDP depend on the composition of capital outlays, the pace of project execution and cross-
sector linkages.
1 In terms of share to GDP, data shows that private investment has grown steadily from 15.3% in 2022 to 17.2% in 2025.
Source: Department of Statistics, Malaysia 
Source: Department of Statistics, Malaysia

99
chapter 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
Based on MoF’s estimates, a 10% increase in real private investment is associated with an 
immediate expansion of approximately 0.49% in real GDP. Malaysia’s structural growth momentum 
reflects the evolution of investment and industrial policies from the New Economic Model (NEM) 
and Economic Transformation Programme (ETP) in 2010, Tenth Malaysia Plan (2011 – 2015) as 
well as the National Policy on Industry 4.0 (Industry4WRD) in 2018 towards a more targeted, 
outcome-oriented investment framework anchored by the NIA in 2022 and reinforced by the 
NIMP 2030. This shift places greater emphasis on economic complexity, technology adoption, 
high-skilled employment, domestic linkages and the development of high growth high value 
(HGHV) activities. This finding is in line with Malaysia’s strategic focus on accelerating investment 
realisation and suggests that policies facilitating efficient capital deployment can help drive broader 
macroeconomic growth across sectors.
The economic impacts in the short run reflect the dynamism of the private investment ecosystem, 
demonstrating how capital spending flows through diverse economic activities during project 
execution. Initial capital outlays on civil works, site preparation and machinery procurement 
generate immediate demand across the domestic economy by contracting local SMEs, sourcing 
domestic raw materials and hiring technical services. Building on this momentum, technology-
driven investment has emerged as a strategic anchor, positioning Malaysia among the top 
developing economies for announced digital economy projects in recent years (World Bank, 2025). 
To translate these multiplier effects into sustained structural transformation, the NIMP 2030 set 
targets to raise manufacturing sector value added to RM587.5 billion, expand employment to 3.3 
million workers and increase the median salary to RM4,510 by 2030. Recent data through the 
second quarter of 2026 indicates continued progress towards these targets, with manufacturing 
value added growing by 7.3% to RM104 billion. Meanwhile, the expansion in manufacturing 
activities was accompanied by a 0.2% increase in employment to 2.72 million workers. Crucially, 
the median salary increased by 6.9% from RM3,200 in 2023 to RM3,422 in 2025, highlighting 
improvement in wages alongside continued employment stability in the manufacturing sector 
(DOSM, 2026). 
Realisation Rate Against Approved Investment 
The realisation of high approved investments provides a solid foundation for continued expansion. 
Figure 3.2.5 illustrates the trends in approved and private investment between 2006 and 2025, 
underscoring the persistent divergence between approved commitments and their physical 
realisation.2 Although high approved investment figures provide a strong foundation for future 
economic expansion, a structural risk persists in the form of implementation lags, whereby 
approved projects do not fully materialise into physical private investment. Currently, the time-lag 
dynamics and conversion rates between official approval and actual physical execution calls for 
greater coherence.
2 Official data from MIDA is used for approved investment while aggregate private investment data from DOSM is used as a macro-level proxy for 
realised investment value.

ecONOMIc OUtLOOK   2027
100
chapter 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
Analysis 3: From Approved to Realised Investment 
Based on the empirical analysis, a 10% increase in the total value of approved investment is 
estimated to increase private investment by 5.2%. This impact on private investment is expected 
to be realised within a 6- to 18-month period. This finding supports the lag effect between official 
approval and physical realisation due to the complexity of projects and implementation procedures. 
Nevertheless, the establishment of the Invest Malaysia Facilitation Centre (IMFC) in 2023 is 
expected to accelerate the conversion from approval to operation, underscoring the continued 
importance of sustained monitoring and targeted execution to further narrow the approved to 
realisation gap.
Way Forward
The success of investments should be measured not merely by the amount of capital it brings 
but through its contribution to build lasting domestic capabilities, strengthen technology transfer, 
innovation-sharing and the integration of domestic firms into global value chains. Achieving 
this requires a continued focus on quality investments through strategic policy and sustained 
investor confidence, alongside targeted public investment. In particular, public investment in 
core infrastructure, such as power grids, roads and utilities remain essential in emerging areas 
with strong potential for future economic activity. Public and private investment should play 
complementary roles in sustaining investment activities and generating broad-based economic 
benefits.
The implementation of the New Incentive Framework (NIF) in 2026 marks a strategic shift towards 
a selective, outcome-based regime designed to maximise long-term national economic returns as 
shown in Figure 3.2.6. Moving beyond conventional capital metrics, the Framework prioritises HGHV 
creation by measuring GDP contribution for every ringgit invested. This targeted approach ensures 
that capital allocations yield tangible structural benefits, directly driving domestic supply chain 
integration and accelerating technology transfer across key growth sectors.
Source: Department of Statistics, Malaysia and Malaysian Investment Development Authority

Parallel to its economic objectives, the NIF embeds sustainability and human capital development 
directly into the incentive evaluation process. It places significant weight on ESG performance 
alongside clear decarbonisation targets to support overarching climate goals. Concurrently, 
incentives are structured to catalyse high-skilled job creation, ensuring that future investments 
cultivate a resilient, future-ready workforce while driving sustainable development.

Sustaining Malaysia’s private investment momentum will require a more aggressive approach by all 
investment promotion agencies. Close engagement with relevant authorities and utilities providers 
will expedite project execution and resolve regulatory bottlenecks. In addition, streamlined approval 
processes, clearer reporting lines and better data synergies across agencies would further support 
timely project implementation and ensure that committed investment translates into actual 
economic output.
Conclusion
Malaysia’s investment momentum provides a strong foundation and sustaining it requires a decisive 
shift towards quality, innovation and sustainability. By aligning investment policies with national 
priorities, Malaysia can translate capital utilisation into inclusive economic development. Moving 
forward, economic success will depend not only on the volume of investment secured, but also on 
how effectively the nation transforms investment into resilient growth and greater socio-economic 
development.

Public consumption expanded by 5.9% in the 
first half of 2026 and was primarily supported 
by higher expenditure on supplies and services, 
alongside an increase in emoluments following 
the second phase of the salary adjustment 
under the SSPA. The pace of expansion is 
expected to moderate in the second half of the 
year, consistent with measures to reprioritise 
non-critical operating expenditure and enhance 
spending efficiency. Allocations for essential 
services, including health, education and 
security, will continue to be safeguarded. State 
elections are also expected to support public 
consumption through election-related spending 
and associated activities. These developments 
reflect the Government’s continuous 
commitment to maintaining efficient public 
service delivery while strengthening fiscal 
discipline and ensuring prudent utilisation of 
public resources. Therefore, growth for the 
year is estimated to register at 3.8%.
Public investment expanded by 5.8% in the 
fi rst half of 2026 and is projected to strengthen 
to 7.7% for the year. This growth will be 
supported by continued capital spending 
by both the Federal Government and non-
fi nancial public corporations (NFPCs). Federal 
Government expenditure will be channelled 
into ongoing transport and connectivity works, 
including the Penang LRT Mutiara Line, SSLR 
and Pan Borneo Highway Sabah, alongside 
the implementation of Cakna MADANI projects 
encompassing the education, health, housing 
and transport sectors. The small- and medium-
scale projects under Cakna MADANI are 
designed for immediate implementation and 
to deliver direct benefi ts to local communities. 
Meanwhile, NFPC investments will continue to 
be supported by energy and utility projects, 
including gas infrastructure, hydroelectric 
and solar initiatives such as the PFLNG3, 
Nenggiri Hydroelectric Project, Sungai Perak 
HLEP and LSS5. Collectively, these investments 
are expected to strengthen connectivity and 
essential infrastructures while supporting 
sustainable and inclusive economic growth. 
The gross national income (GNI) at current 
prices is projected to grow by 7% in 2026, 
driven by stronger domestic activity. Gross 
national savings (GNS) is also expected to 
accelerate by 10.2%, representing 23.7% 
of GNI. With total investment projected at 
RM455 billion or 21.7% of GNI, the savings-
investment gap for the year is set to yield a 
RM41.3 billion surplus or 2% of GNI, providing 
funds for long-term productive investments, 
primarily from domestic sources.

chapter 3   macroeconomic outlook
Gross operating surplus3 (GOS) growth 
moderated by 2.1% but remained the largest 
income component totalling RM1,255.1 billion, 
despite its share of GDP narrowing to 62% in 
2025. The growth in business capital income 
was attributed to favourable commodity prices, 
structural momentum in the technology and 
infrastructure segments, sustained rebound 
in tourism industry and resilience of other 
consumer-related services industries. Likewise, 
mixed-income registered a robust growth of 
10.9%, supported by increased participation 
among self-employed individuals and 
unincorporated enterprises. In 2026, corporate 
profitability prospects are expected to grow 
further by 9.1%, bolstered by vibrant tourism 
landscape, the ongoing global technology and 
semiconductor upcycle and rapid digital-related 
infrastructure. The country’s position as a 
net energy exporter may also boost earnings 
prospects across energy-related and upstream 
service industries. Meanwhile, enforcement 
of the Gig Workers Act 2025 [Act 872], 
alongside targeted institutional support for 
self-employment and micro-entrepreneurship 
may further encourage more participation in 
the gig economy, small business ventures and 
freelancing activities. As a result, the share 
of mixed-income is expected to reach 16% of 
GOS. Overall, the share of GOS is expected to 
increase to 62.6% in 2026. 
The net taxes4 component improved by 
53.7% and contributed 4.1% of GDP in 2025, 
driven by higher growth of taxes income 
on production and imports (12.9%), mainly 
from sales tax and service tax. Meanwhile, 
subsidy and incentive expenditures dropped 
substantially by 36.5% due to targeted 
cuts in petroleum and diesel subsidies. For 
2026, taxes less subsidies is projected to 
decrease by 10.5%, particularly due to larger 
expenditure expected for fuel subsidies. 
Government expenditure for subsidies and 
incentives is likely to increase substantially 
to assist households and industries to cope 
with increasing price pressures from supply-
chain disruptions due to the ongoing West 
Asia conflict. Hence, the overall share of 
net taxes on production and imports is 
estimated to be lower at 3.4% of GDP in 
2026.
External Sector 
Exceptional performance in the external sector 
amid global headwinds 
Trade Performance
Total trade is estimated to surge by 27.2% 
to RM3,895.5 billion in 2026. The growth 
is boosted by global technology upcycle 
and favourable energy sector amid global 
headwinds and anticipated slower global trade 
growth. 
Gross exports are expected to expand by 31% 
to RM2,107.8 billion in 2026, on the back of 
technology upcycle and rapid advancement 
in AI applications globally. This growth is 
anchored by manufactured goods exports, 
which is projected to surge by 34.7%. Exports 
of E&E products are expected to register a 
significant increase of 44.3%, attributed to 
stronger global semiconductor sales. Being the 
key exports driver, semiconductor accounted 
for more than 65% of E&E products exports, 
followed by telecommunication equipment 
parts and automatic data processing 
equipment. Higher exports in E&E products 
are partially supported by a substantial 
increase in semiconductor exports to the 
US, which accounted for more than 20% 
of total semiconductor exports, following 
tariff exemption. Similarly, receipts from 
non-E&E products are projected to grow by 
24.6%, driven by higher demand for other

the manufacturing sector, particularly for 
parts and accessories as well as industrial 
supplies. This growth is in line with the rising 
purchasing managers’ index (PMI), an indicator 
of business activity and economic health, which 
consistently signal upcoming shifts in import 
volume. 
Similarly, imports of capital goods are 
anticipated to increase by 9.5%, underpinned 
by ongoing infrastructure and utilities 
investment as well as business expansion 
and diversification. Furthermore, robust 
demand for cloud computing and online 
businesses partly contribute to the increasing 
imports related to data centre development. 
Likewise, consumption goods are projected 
to expand by 1.4%, mainly buoyed by higher 
imports of consumer goods following rising 
domestic purchasing power, shifting consumer 
preferences and favourable exchange rates.

Balance of Payments
In the first half of 2026, the current account 
of the balance of payments registered a 
surplus of RM25.9 billion or 2.6% of GNI. The 
surplus was attributed to a higher net inflow 
in the goods account as well as a turnaround 
to surplus in the services account, which more 
than offset the wider deficit in the income 
accounts. In the second half of 2026, the 
current account surplus is expected to record 
RM15.3 billion or 1.4% of GNI. This surplus is 
contributed by net inflow in the goods account 
and narrowing deficit in the primary income 
account, despite lower surplus in the services 
account and wider deficit in the secondary 
income account. Overall, the current account 
surplus is anticipated at RM41.3 billion or 2% of 
GNI in 2026. 
The goods account is expected to record a 
higher surplus of RM135.9 billion in 2026, 
boosted by stronger exports of manufactured 
goods and mining goods, despite lower exports 
of agriculture goods. Exports of E&E products, 
other manufactures as well as optical and 
scientific equipment, are envisaged to record 
significant increases, in tandem with stronger 
global semiconductor sales driven by technology 
upcycle and robust demand for AI. Furthermore, 
as a net energy exporter, Malaysia is poised 
to benefit from higher exports of LNG and

INFOrMatION BOX 3.2
Malaysia's Trade Resiliency: Navigating Global Trade Uncertainties
Introduction
The global trade environment has become increasingly volatile since 2018, shaped by persistent 
geopolitical tensions, inflationary pressures, supply chain disruptions, protectionist trade measures 
and the intensifying strategic competition among major economies. These developments have 
altered global production networks and created significant challenges for export-oriented 
economies. As a small and open economy with a trade-to-GDP ratio consistently exceeding 100%, 
Malaysia’s trade performance from 2022 to June 2026 demonstrates a remarkable resilience, 
underpinned by diversified export base and markets, strong manufacturing capabilities, extensive 
FTAs and proactive government policies. 
Global Headwinds
The World Economic Outlook (WEO), published by the IMF, reported a slower global trade growth 
from 5.8% in 2022 to 1% in 2023, before rebounding to 3.7% in 2024 and further strengthening to 
5% in 2025. In addition, according to the UN Trade & Development (UNCTAD), between 2011 and 
2025, global goods exports recorded an average annual growth of 4.1%, while Malaysia’s average 
gross exports growth was 6.7% during the same period. This is a reflection of Malaysia’s exports, 
which have consistently outperformed global goods exports in most years, underscoring the 
country’s trade resilience in the face of major global headwinds, as shown in Figure 3.2.1.

ecONOMIc OUtLOOK   2027
111
chapter 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
Among major global shocks, the exports of global goods experienced the sharpest contraction 
of 12.9% in 2015 stemming from the steep commodity price collapse, which began in 2014. 
Global commodity prices declined by approximately 38% between June 2014 and February 2015, 
largely due to the significant collapse of 48% in Brent crude oil prices, from USD112 per barrel 
to around USD58 per barrel, alongside declines in metal and agricultural commodity prices. In 
contrast, Malaysia demonstrated resilience amid the commodity price crash, supported by its 
diversified export structure. Gross exports grew by 1.6% in 2015, attributed to stronger demand for 
manufactured goods (6.5%), which more than offset by lower exports of agriculture goods (-2.8%) 
and mining goods (-22.9%). 
The COVID-19 pandemic, which began in March 2020, further underscored Malaysia’s trade 
resilience, as the country weathered severe disruptions to most industries arising from movement 
restrictions. During that year, the contraction in global goods exports reached 7.2%, steeper 
than the 1.1% decline recorded in Malaysia’s gross exports. The weaker export performance 
reflected a significant 28.3% decline in the exports of mining goods, despite the gains of 1.1% in 
manufactured goods and 8.7% in agriculture goods. 
Nonetheless, due to a slowdown in global growth and goods trade, Malaysia’s gross exports in 2023 
contracted by 8%, in tandem with the 4.1% decline in global goods exports. This was refl ected in the 
lower exports of manufactured goods (-6.8%), agriculture goods (-21.6%) and mining goods (-9.6%). 
Notwithstanding persistent global uncertainties, Malaysia’s exports regained its strong momentum, 
recording a growth of 5.8% in 2024 to RM1,509.3 billion and attaining RM1,609.4 billion in 2025. 
The world faced global supply chain disruptions following the escalation of conflicts in West Asia, 
which began in February 2026. Nonetheless, Malaysia is able to withstand the impact of the 
conflict, leveraging its status as a net energy exporter amid disruptions in global energy supply 
chains. Malaysia registered a higher trade balance during the first half of 2026, supported by 
exponential export performance of E&E products following global technology upcycle, alongside the 
country’s solid economic fundamentals and facilitative policies.
Source: Department of Statistics, Ministry of Finance, Malaysia and UN Trade & Development (UNCTAD)

Malaysia’s Trade Landscape and Milestones
Malaysia is transforming into a high growth high value (HGHV) hub, driving innovation and 
sustainable economic development across key industries. The nation’s economic development 
policies have attracted MNCs, thus integrating Malaysia into global production networks and value 
chains, particularly in the E&E industry. This structural transformation has significantly diversified 
Malaysia’s export base, reduced dependency on commodity exports and enhanced trade resiliency 
to external shocks. 
The share of commodities export declined from 26.6% in 2011 to 12.7% in 2025, despite receipts 
of commodities expanding from RM186 billion in 2011 to RM204.2 billion in 2025. The trend is 
in tandem with Malaysia’s transition to manufacturing oriented sector and translated through a 
significant increase of exports in manufactured goods from RM507.4 billion or 72.7% of Malaysia’s 
total exports in 2011 to RM1,390.6 billion or 86.4% of total exports in 2025, as shown in 
Figure 3.2.2. Major exports of manufactured goods include E&E products; petroleum products; 
machinery, equipment and parts; chemicals and chemical products as well as optical and scientific 
products. As a result, the manufacturing sector has become one of the key engines of economic 
growth, employment, exports and technological advancement.
As one of the world’s leading players in the E&E industry, Malaysia has established itself as 
a regional hub within the global semiconductor value chain. The E&E industry is the largest 
contributor to manufactured exports and remains a cornerstone of the country’s economy. The 
country has developed strong capabilities across the semiconductor value chain, particularly in 
assembly, testing and packaging (ATP), while increasingly expanding into integrated circuit (IC) 
design, wafer fabrication, advanced packaging and semiconductor equipment manufacturing. 
Between 2011 and 2025, exports of E&E products recorded a threefold expansion from 
RM237.1 billion to RM712.3 billion. The World Semiconductor Trade Statistics (WSTS) projected 
the global semiconductor market to surge by 90% in 2026 to USD1.5 trillion, followed by a 27% 
Source: Department of Statistics, Malaysia and Malaysia External Trade Development Corporation

increase to USD1.9 trillion in 2027. This strong growth outlook is expected to further boost exports 
of E&E products and strengthen Malaysia’s position as a key player in the global semiconductor 
industry. 
The West Asia conflict has emerged as a major cause of geopolitical risk to global trade. Its 
repercussions extend far beyond the region, reflecting the region’s pivotal role as a global hub 
for energy supplies and key maritime trade routes. Nonetheless, Malaysia is leveraging from the 
conflict as external trade remained resilient and maintained its stellar performance during the first 
six months of 2026 (H1 2026), with total trade expanding by 22.4% to RM1,795.2 billion. The growth 
was supported by higher trade with major trading partners, particularly China (30.6%), the US 
(25.7%), Taiwan (Province of China) (34.7%), Singapore (21.5%) and Hong Kong (45.8%). 
Gross exports increased by 27.5% to RM971.6 billion, attributed to stronger exports of manufactured 
goods (30.5%) and mining goods (24.5%), which cushioned lower exports of agriculture goods 
(-8.9%). The growth was boosted by robust exports of E&E products (42.5%), other manufactures 
(101.6%), metalliferous ores and metal scrap (209.3%), petroleum products (27.4%) as well as optical 
and scientifi c equipment (40.8%). Gross imports grew 16.8% to RM823.6 billion on account of higher 
imports of intermediate goods (5.8%), consumption goods (4.3%) and capital goods (0.8%). Malaysia 
recorded a trade surplus of RM148 billion during H1 2026, soared by 161.4% from RM56.6 billion 
registered in the corresponding period in 2025.
The escalating conflict, worsened by the closure of the Strait of Hormuz has emerged as a major 
shock to the global economy, disrupting energy supplies, causing Brent crude prices to surge 
above USD110 per barrel in April 2026. Furthermore, the situation exacerbated global supply chains 
and led to a widespread price increase of fuel, food and plastics. Notwithstanding the ongoing 
conflict, Malaysia registered a higher trade surplus of LNG, expanded by 16.5% to RM26.2 billion 
during H1 2026 (H1 2025: RM22.5 billion). This surplus was buoyed by stronger LNG demand, which 
increased by 12.4% to RM29.1 billion, and lower imports, which decreased by 15% to RM2.9 billion. 
Correspondingly, trade surplus of petroleum condensates and other petroleum oil widened by 
16.1% to RM2.5 billion (H1 2025: RM2.1 billion), contributed by higher exports. 
On the other hand, Malaysia is a net importer of crude petroleum and registered a narrower trade 
defi cit of RM11 billion during H1 2026 (H1 2025: -RM16.3 billion) following softer imports. Imports of 
crude petroleum shrank by 24.6% to RM19.8 billion and exports decreased by 11.5% to RM8.8 billion. 
Similarly, Malaysia registered a trade shortfall of petroleum products amounting to RM0.2 billion 
compared with a surplus of RM4.3 billion recorded in H1 2025.
Strengthening Market Diversification through Free Trade Agreements
Malaysia is extensively integrated into global markets through trade and investment with major 
economies. To date, Malaysia has signed and implemented 17 FTAs, comprising eight bilateral FTAs 
and nine regional FTAs, as depicted in Figure 3.2.3 and Figure 3.2.4. A significant milestone was 
achieved in 2022 with Malaysia as a party in two mega FTAs, namely the Regional Comprehensive 
Economic Partnership (RCEP) and Comprehensive and Progressive Agreement for Trans-Pacific 
Partnership (CPTPP). 

ecONOMIc OUtLOOK   2027
114
chapter 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
In the post-Global Financial Crisis era, Malaysia’s active engagement in bilateral and regional trade 
agreements had become a key driver of trade expansion, reinforcing its position within regional 
production networks and global markets. In 2011, almost 70% of Malaysia’s total trade, amounting 
to RM881.8 billion, was conducted with countries that had trade agreements with Malaysia, as 
shown in Figure 3.2.5. Exports to FTA partner countries stood at RM498.8 billion, accounting for 
71.5% of total exports, while imports amounted to RM383 billion or 66.8% of total imports. Hence, 
Malaysia recorded a trade surplus of RM115.8 billion in 2011. China was Malaysia’s largest FTA 
trading partner, accounting for 13.2% of total trade, followed by Singapore (12.7%), Japan (11.5%), 
Thailand (5.5%) and Indonesia (4.4%).

Total trade with FTA partner countries continued to expand, registering a compound annual 
growth rate (CAGR) of 6.1% between 2012 and 2025. Trade with FTA partner countries reached 
RM2,007.3 billion in 2025, accounting for 65.6% of Malaysia’s total trade, supported by sustained 
growth in both exports and imports. Exports to FTA partner countries increased by 3.2% to 
RM1,069 billion, while imports grew by 5.1% to RM938.3 billion. Among Malaysia’s key FTA trading 
partners, China accounted for the largest share of total trade at 17.7%, followed by Singapore 
(13.2%), Japan (4.7%), Thailand (3.9%) and Republic of Korea (3.8%).
During the first six months of 2026, trade with FTA partner countries improved by 26.2% to 
RM1,424.8 billion, accounting for 79.4% of Malaysia’s total trade (H1 2025: RM1,129.4 billion or 
77%). The stronger performance was supported by robust growth in both exports and imports, with 
exports soaring by 22.4% to RM738 billion and imports surging by 30.4% to RM686.9 billion. The 
major FTA partner countries during the period were China (18.1%), Singapore (13.2%), Hong Kong 
(4.4%), Japan (4.4%) and Republic of Korea (4.2%).
Trade with FTA partner countries is proven strategically important to Malaysia, providing greater 
market access, reducing trade costs, integrating SMEs into the international platform as well as 
enhancing competitiveness of exports and technology transfer. Importantly, the integration provides 
greater resilience against global trade disruptions by offering diversified markets and more reliable 
supply chains, thus enhancing Malaysia’s attractiveness as an investment and production hub. 
Following the successful implementation of AFTA, ASEAN acts as a vital trade buff er, regulatory safety 
net and regional supply chain anchor for Malaysia that drives economic growth, secures regional 
stability and amplifi es the country’s global presence. As Malaysia’s largest trading bloc, ASEAN 
accounted for more than 25% of the country’s total trade, with trade expanding at a CAGR of 6.3% 
between 2012 and 2025. Total trade with ASEAN member countries amounted to RM331.1 billion 
or 26% of total trade in 2011 and doubled to RM778.1 billion or 25.4% in 2025. During the fi rst six 
months of 2026, trade with ASEAN member countries expanded by 21.5% to RM454.5 billion, supported

respectively, as depicted in Figure 3.2.6. In H1 2026, Malaysia’s exports to ASEAN were primarily driven 
by E&E products, which accounted for 43.6% of total exports, followed by petroleum products (14.4%), 
machinery, equipment and parts (6.9%) as well as chemicals and chemical products (5.1%). From an 
import perspective, E&E products also constituted the largest share at 38%, followed by petroleum 
products (14.7%), chemicals and chemical products (4.9%) as well as machinery, equipment and parts 
(4.7%).
Malaysia’s Trade Resiliency
The Global Trade Resilience Index 2026 (GTRI 2026) evaluates countries’ trade resilience across two 
primary dimensions, namely absorptive capacity and recovery capacity. The absorptive capacity is 
to determine the ability of a country to withstand the immediate impact of external shocks while 
maintaining stability in trade operations. Absorptive capacity captures both the structural strength 
and the responsiveness of a country’s trade system when confronted with sudden disruption. 
Meanwhile, the recovery capacity measures the ability to restore trade performance and adapt to 
new norms in the short to medium term following a disruption. 
With a score of 66.03, Malaysia was placed 23rd globally in the GTRI 2026 behind China, Japan and 
Republic of Korea, which ranked 15th, 17th and 18th, respectively. Among ASEAN member states, 
Singapore ranked 7th with a score of 75.35, while Thailand, the Philippines, Indonesia and Viet Nam 
ranked 32nd, 35th, 36th and 41st, respectively. Among 56 countries assessed, Malaysia was positioned 
28th with a score of 66.12 for absorptive capacity and ranked 16th with a score of 65.89 for recovery 
capacity, as summarised in Table 3.2.1. 
Source: Department of Statistics, Malaysia and Malaysia External Trade Development Corporation

Malaysia ranked 28th for absorptive capacity partly due to slightly lower score for exposure 
pillar, which scored 59.06 and ranked 40th, reflects the country’s higher risks to external shocks 
and global uncertainties. Notwithstanding, being ranked 16th for recovery capacity, Malaysia 
demonstrates a relatively strong capacity to rebound from shocks, bolstered by a diversified 
export base and solid policy framework focused on economic resilience, inclusive and sustainable 
growth, structural reforms, investment and trade diversification as well as domestic capabilities. 
These measures are crucial in enabling Malaysia to navigate global uncertainties while enhancing 
its long-term economic competitiveness. Additionally, the Government continues to strengthen the 
capabilities of exporters and local industry players through targeted financial and non-financial 
assistance, including export promotion, market diversification, supply chain integration and industry 
upgrading. These efforts aim to enhance firms’ competitiveness, deepen domestic competencies as 
well as fortify Malaysia’s resilience and position within global value chains. 
Clarion Call
While the current trade regime has driven Malaysia’s economic growth for decades, rising 
geopolitical tensions and market fragmentation posed direct risks to production costs and supply 
chain stability. In response to the recent conflict, the Government mobilised the National Economic 
Action Council’s Crisis Management Task Force as well as the Trade Resilience Taskforce. These 
ongoing mechanisms have executed swift daily interventions, safeguarding strategic fuel reserves 
and shielding rakyat from inflationary pressures. 
Moving forward, strengthening national trade resilience requires holistic structural economic 
reforms to further enhance Malaysia’s competitive advantage in a dynamic global landscape. It is 
crucial for Malaysia to strategically strengthen trade security and build national resilience against 
future global disruptions, including exploring new export markets and import sources. Diversifying 
import sources is essential to offset immediate supply deficits as well as to build a flexible and 
future-proof supply chain. Nonetheless, it is imperative for Malaysia to build a comprehensive 
and multi-layered trade resilient strategy to achieve domestic sustainability. The Government is 
intensifying efforts to accelerate integration and sophistication with higher value-added supply 
chains to cushion exposure to external shocks, including through technological advancement and 
domestic innovation. 

ecONOMIc OUtLOOK   2027
118
chapter 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
Malaysia is charting the path toward attracting HGHV investments, increasing economic complexity 
and expanding skilled jobs as well as strengthening domestic supply chain. Anchored by the 
Ekonomi MADANI framework, complemented by the Thirteenth Malaysia Plan, 2026 – 2030, policies 
including the National Semiconductor Strategy (NSS), New Industrial Master Plan 2030 (NIMP 
2030), New Incentive Framework (NIF), National AI Action Plan (2026 – 2030) and National Energy 
Transition Roadmap (NETR) were formulated to capture greater global value chain. These policies 
prioritise deepening local manufacturing capabilities, accelerating technological innovation and 
expanding domestic participation in higher value-added segments, particularly within the advanced 
electronics and semiconductor ecosystem.
Conclusion
Malaysia’s trade resilience requires holistic and forward-looking policies that strengthen trade 
security, accelerates trade diversification and deepens domestic supply chain capabilities to 
mitigate external shocks. It is timely for Malaysia to further move up the global value chain, 
reduce vulnerabilities to external disruptions and reinforce the country’s competitiveness, economic 
resilience and long-term sustainable growth.
Prices
Inflation remains moderate despite emerging cost 
pressures
Headline inflation, as measured by the 
Consumer Price Index (CPI), averaged at 1.8% 
for the first eight months of 2026, compared 
with 1.4% in the corresponding period of 2025. 
Core inflation averaged at 2%, consistent with 
moderate underlying overall price pressures.
Transport inflation averaged at 1.7% during 
the same period (January – August 2025: 
0.6%), mainly driven by higher unsubsidised 
fuel prices stemming from increases in global 
crude oil prices amid the West Asia conflict. 
However, the implementation of the BUDI 
Diesel mechanism in July 2026 helped contain 
transport-related price pressures during the 
latter part of the period, particularly through 
a slower increase in the cost of operating 
personal transport equipment. Inflation for 
insurance & financial services averaged at 
4.1% (January – August 2025: 2.4%), partly 
reflecting the adjustments in medical insurance 
premiums and financial service charges. 
Meanwhile, the food & beverages category, 
which constitutes 29.8% of the CPI weight, 
eased to 1.5% during the period (January – 
August 2025: 2.2%), reflecting slower price 
increases in the food away from home sub-
category and effective domestic supply-
management measures.
Headline inflation is expected to increase 
gradually towards the end of the year as 
higher energy, freight, food and other input 
costs pass through to retail prices. For the 
full year, headline inflation is estimated to 
range between 1.5% and 2.5% in 2026 and is 
expected to remain manageable. Targeted fuel 
subsidies, domestic price-stabilisation measures 
and a relatively stable ringgit will help mitigate 
the transmission of external cost pressures. 
Meanwhile, domestic demand is expected 
to remain firm without generating excessive 
demand-driven inflation.
On the producer side, upstream cost conditions 
were comparatively steeper. During the first 
eight months of 2026, the Producer Price 

economic outlook   2027
119
chapter 3   macroeconomic outlook
Index (PPI) averaged at 4.6%, compared with 
a decline of 2.3% in the corresponding period 
of 2025. Producer-price increases were driven 
mainly by higher prices for crude materials for 
further processing and intermediate materials, 
supplies & components, alongside price 
pressures in manufacturing sector, including 
refined petroleum as well as computer, 
electronic & optical products.
By stage of processing, prices of crude 
materials for further processing increased by 
an average of 15.4% during January – August 
2026, compared with a decline of 4.1% in the 
same period of 2025. Prices of intermediate 
materials, supplies & components rose by an 
average of 2.8% (January – August 2025: -2.2%), 
while finished goods producer prices increased 
moderately by 0.9% (January – August 2025: 
-1.3%). The concentration of cost pressures at 
the earlier stages of production suggests that 
the transmission to consumer prices is likely to 
remain gradual. The comparatively reasonable 
increase in finished-goods producer prices 
also indicates that the pass-through of firmer 
upstream costs to retail prices remained limited.

Labour Market
Sustained labour market performance 
The labour market remained stable in the first 
half of 2026, underpinned by stronger-than-
expected economic growth attributed to robust 
domestic demand and external sector. The 
labour force participation rate rose to 70.9%, 
reflecting expanding workforce engagement 
amid healthy employment prospects. Total 
employment expanded by 1.1% to 16.8 
million persons, while the unemployment 
rate remained low at 3%, equivalent to 
509,700 unemployed persons. The services 
sector continued to account for the largest 
share of employment at 66.7%, followed by 
manufacturing and agriculture at 16.2% and 
8.7%, respectively.
Demand for labour also remained encouraging 
in the first half of 2026, evidenced by 
sustained job creation and expanding 
vacancies. Establishments generated 64,270 
new jobs, marginally lower by 1.3% compared 
with 65,138 jobs in the corresponding 
period of 2025, while total vacancies edged 
up by 0.4% to 195,730 positions. Despite 
the continued availability of employment 
opportunities, job placements declined by 
20.6% to 74,874 persons, indicating moderation 
in job matching and cautious onboarding by 
businesses navigating lingering supply chain 
risks and external headwinds.
The labour market is projected to maintain 
a favourable trajectory in the second half 
of 2026, supported by resilient household 
demand and steady expansion in services and 
manufacturing sectors. Vibrant tourism-related 
activities, global semiconductor upcycle and 
ongoing digital infrastructure development will 
help to sustain business appetite for hiring and 
headcount retention. Against this backdrop, 
the full year unemployment rate is estimated 
to remain low at 3% in 2026.

economic outlook   2027
122
chapter 3   macroeconomic outlook
As at end-August 2026, registered low-skilled 
foreign workers contracted by 2.9% to 
2.08 million compared to 2.14 million in the 
corresponding period in 2025, driven by tighter 
quota controls aligned with the Government’s 
policy commitment to reduce economic 
reliance on foreign labour. Meanwhile, sectoral 
manpower shortages will be addressed through 
measures focused on enhancing job conditions 
to attract local talents, alongside productivity 
improvements through accelerating automation 
and technology adoption. Foreign workers 
were predominantly sourced from Bangladesh 
(36.2%), Indonesia (26.4%) and Nepal (16.7%). 
The manufacturing sector accounted for the 
largest share of foreign worker employment 
at 28.8%, followed by construction (26%) and 
services (19.2%). Meanwhile, the number of 
expatriates increased by 1.5% to 123,088 
persons as at end-August 2026, reflecting 
sustained demand for specialised expertise. 
Expatriates were mainly from China (32%), 
India (15.5%) and Indonesia (7.2%). In terms of 
sectoral distribution, expatriates were mainly 
employed in information technology (36.5%), 
followed by services (16.6%) and construction 
(14.7%).
Labour productivity, measured by value-
added per worker, increased by 4.6% to 
RM52,763 in the first half of 2026, anchored 
by strong output growth in manufacturing 
and construction, alongside a recovery in 
mining and quarrying sectors. Manufacturing 
productivity gains were supported by output 
expansion, particularly in E&E and metal 
industries, with value-added outpacing 
employment growth. For the whole year, 
overall productivity is projected to expand by 
3.4% to approximately RM108,100, supported 
by ongoing strategic investments in high-
impact sectors such as digital technology, data 
centres and E&E-related, alongside greater 
technology adoption and talent development. 
The construction sector is expected to record 
the highest productivity growth at 4.4%, 
followed by manufacturing at 4% and services 
at 3.6%.

chapter 3   macroeconomic outlook
Outlook 2027
Sectoral
Services Sector
Steady growth in all subsectors
The services sector is projected to register 
a strong growth of 5.2% in 2027, with all 
subsectors expected to perform positively. The 
growth will be anchored by high-value digital 
services and resilient private consumption 
alongside higher visitor arrivals following 
VM2026 – 2027. Furthermore, the sector’s 
performance will be boosted by the initiatives 
on the national agenda to accelerate growth of 
digital economy.
The wholesale and retail trade subsector 
will remain a key contributor to the services 
sector, with a growth of 4.4% mainly driven 
by the wholesale trade segment. Vigorous 
seasonal sales and promotional campaigns 
across physical stores as well as e-commerce 
and social media platforms will continue to 
stimulate consumer spending in tandem with 
the VM2026 – 2027 marketing and promotional 
events. Meanwhile, the motor vehicles segment 
is expected to record a growth supported by 
resilient demand for new private vehicles, 
particularly for locally-assembled cars.
Meanwhile, the information and communication 
subsector is expected to maintain a strong 
growth of 7.9% in 2027, supported by the 
growing demand for cloud computing, 
ongoing digitalisation across businesses, 
continued operationalisation of data centres 
and expansion of digital connectivity as well 
as the development in telecommunication 
infrastructure including Jalinan Digital Negara 
Phase 2 (JENDELA Phase 2) and Sambungan 
Kabel Dasar Laut MADANI (SALAM). In 
addition, the substantial approved investment 
in information and communication subsector 
in the first half of 2026 is expected to spur 
further economic activity and contribute to the 
subsector’s growth in 2027. 
The transportation and storage subsector 
is forecast to grow by 7.4%, supported by 
continued trade and logistics activities, 
sustained demand for warehousing and 
distribution services as well as improved 
passenger traffic in conjunction with VM2026 
– 2027. The land transport segment will 
be partly bolstered by the commencement 
of the East Coast Rail Link (ECRL) that is 
envisaged to boost transportation and enhance 
connectivity between the East Coast and major 
ports, along with the expansion of railway 
networks supporting cargo movement and 
logistics activities. Furthermore, the expected 
commencement of the Johor Bahru-Singapore 
Rapid Transit System Link (RTS Link) will 
enhance cross-border connectivity and support 
passenger traffic, tourism, retail and business 
activities. Likewise, continued travel, as well 
as tourism activities and airport capacity 
upgrades will further promote demand in 
both passenger and cargo in the air transport 
segment. The growth of the water transport 
segment is projected to remain stable in 2027 
on account of high trade activities, supported 
by Malaysia’s strategic position along major 
regional shipping routes.
The real estate and business services 
subsector is forecast to increase by 7% driven 
by continued investment and construction 
activities, including the development 
and operationalisation of data centres. 
Moreover, ongoing projects under the JS-SEZ, 
development of industrial parks and logistics 
facilities, and the completion of several data 
centre projects are expected to increase 
demand for property-related, engineering, 
professional and business services. Meanwhile, 
the commencement of the RTS Link will be 
able to support transit-oriented development 
envisaged to unlock multi-dimensional 
economic benefits.
The food & beverages and accommodation 
subsector is expected to grow steadily, 
expanding by 6% in 2027, in lieu of 
higher visitor arrivals in conjunction with 
the VM2026 – 2027, as well as robust 

economic outlook   2027
124
chapter 3   macroeconomic outlook
domestic tourism spending. The subsector’s 
performance will also be bolstered by various 
international events scheduled including 
the Langkawi International Maritime and 
Aerospace Exhibition (LIMA) 2027, 34th 
South East Asian Games (SEA Games) 2027 
as well as numerous MICE programmes. In 
addition, visit state campaigns by Sabah and 
Terengganu are also expected to stimulate 
tourism activities and provide further impetus 
to the subsector.
The finance and insurance subsector is 
anticipated to expand by 2.3%, supported by 
sustained economic activities. Within the finance 
segment, demand for financing is expected 
to remain supported by ongoing investment 
activities, particularly in high value-added 
industries, as well as conducive labour market 
conditions. Meanwhile, the insurance segment is 
expected to benefit from the nationwide rollout 
of MediAsas, although rising medical costs may 
temper premium growth.
The utilities subsector is projected to grow by 
3.7%, driven by increasing electricity demand 
and water consumption from industrial 
and commercial users, partly as a result of 
the expansion of data centre operations. 
Meanwhile, ongoing development of industrial 
parks and digital infrastructure will require 
utility consumption, particularly in regions 
experiencing rapid investment activity. 
Notwithstanding, the effects of the Super El 
Niño are also expected to increase demand for 
electricity due to higher air conditioning usage 
to weather the hot and dry spell.
The other services subsector is projected to 
rise by 5.8%, driven by the private health and 
education services. The Malaysia Healthcare 
Travel Council (MHTC) will continue efforts to 
strengthen Malaysia’s position as a preferred 
medical tourism destination by expanding 
its international market presence and 
promoting Malaysia’s quality medical services 
and healthcare offerings, particularly in key 
markets such as Indonesia, China and India. 
Meanwhile, the private education services 
is expected to strengthen further, driven by 
continued efforts to position Malaysia as an 
attractive international education destination. 
Promotional initiatives by Education Malaysia 
offices, Education Malaysia Global Services 
(EMGS) and local universities, particularly 
through mobility and edutourism programmes, 
are expected to further support growth in 
international student enrolment.
The government services subsector is 
forecast to record a growth of 4.5% in 
2027. This is partly attributed to outlays for 
supplies and services as well as emoluments.
Manufacturing Sector
Harnessing the AI wave
The manufacturing sector is projected to grow 
by 4.1% in 2027, attributed by a moderate 
expansion in export-oriented industries. 
Growth in the E&E segment is expected 
to remain stable, supported by sustained 
demand for AI-related products, despite 
the easing of global semiconductor cycle in 
2027. Continued implementation of the NSS 
will support domestic capabilities in higher 
value-added activities including integrated 
circuit design and advanced packaging. 
Meanwhile, domestic-oriented industries are 
expected to benefit from sustained household 
spending and tourism activity. The vibrant 
promotional events under VM2026 – 2027 
together with the hosting of LIMA 2027 and 
34th SEA Games 2027, are expected to support 
tourist arrivals and spending, generating 
additional demand for food, beverages and 
other consumer goods. Continued expansion 
in construction activity, particularly industrial 
and infrastructure projects, will also support 
demand for building and other intermediate 
materials. 

economic outlook   2027
125
chapter 3   macroeconomic outlook
Agriculture Sector
Recovery in production
The agriculture sector is projected to rebound 
and improve marginally by 0.5% in 2027. 
The oil palm subsector is projected to record 
marginal declines in FFB yields and crude palm 
oil (CPO) production in line with the biological 
yield cycle. However, better FFB quality is 
expected to support a marginal improvement 
in oil extraction rate. On the other hand, 
despite CPO production declining marginally, 
its prices are expected to remain high, 
averaging between RM4,450 and RM4,600 per 
tonne in 2027 (2026: RM4,300 – RM4,500). This 
is in tandem with tighter supply conditions 
following continued global demand especially 
from biodiesel programmes, thus sustaining 
plantation incomes.
A recovery in agro-food production, particularly 
in the livestock and fishing subsectors, is 
expected to further support the growth driven 
by sustained household spending and tourism-
related demand. Efforts to expand paddy 
cultivation and improve irrigation, alongside 
greater adoption of agricultural technologies, 
are expected to support food production 
and productivity. The rubber subsector is 
anticipated to recover, attributed to the 
rehabilitation of untapped mature rubber 
areas and further enhancement of plantation 
initiatives by the Government. However, the 
extent of the production recovery across 
other subsectors will remain stable, subject to 
weather conditions and input costs.
Mining and Quarrying Sector
Sustaining growth through new field 
developments
The mining and quarrying sector is projected to 
expand by 1.2% in 2027, supported by both the 
natural gas and the crude oil and condensate 
subsectors. The anticipated operationalisation of 
PFLNG3 in Sipitang, Sabah and the progressive 
ramp-up of the Rosmari and Marjoram projects 
in Sarawak will support natural gas production, 
with demand sustained by domestic power 
generation and LNG export markets. 
Additional output from newly commissioned 
fields in Kasawari Phase 2 (Offshore Sarawak), 
including projects Berantai East and Irong 
Timur, are expected to partially mitigate the 
decline in crude oil and condensate production 
at the Gumusut-Kakap and Malikai fields. 
Continued redevelopment of existing assets and 
efforts to improve recovery rates will provide 
further support to production. In terms of 
pricing, Brent Crude oil is projected to average 
USD85 per barrel in anticipation of moderate oil 
demand growth due to the growing adoption of 
xEV, biofuels and energy efficiency.
Construction Sector
Projects in pipeline to sustain growth
The construction sector is forecast to register 
a growth of 5.7% in 2027, driven by continued 
activities across all subsectors. The non-
residential buildings and specialised construction 
activities subsectors are expected to remain as 
the main contributors to the sector’s growth. The 
development of new data centres in Selangor, 
Johor and Negeri Sembilan will continue to 
sustain the non-residential buildings subsector’s 
expansion. Progress in these projects, together 
with other building and infrastructure projects, 
is projected to generate demand for mechanical, 
electrical and plumbing installation works, thus 
supporting the specialised construction activities 
subsector’s growth.
Major infrastructure projects such as the 
Gombak-Port Klang section of the East Coast 
Rail Link (ECRL Phase 2), Elevated Autonomous 
Rapid Transit (E-ART) system, Kuala Lumpur 
Northern Dispersal Expressway (KL NODE), 
LRT3 Phase 2 and Juru-Sungai Dua Traffic 
Dispersal Project will steer the civil engineering 
subsector’s performance. In addition, the 
residential buildings subsector is expected to 
benefit from the implementation of measures 
under the National Housing Policy 2026 – 2035,

Domestic Demand
Domestic demand continues to anchor growth
In 2027, domestic demand is forecast to 
increase by 5.1%, underpinned by continued 
growth in private sector expenditure at 
5.3%. Resilient consumption and investment 
activities are expected to sustain the private 
sector’s contribution to GDP growth at 4.1 ppt. 
Meanwhile, public expenditure is anticipated to 
grow by 4.2%, contributing 0.8 ppt to overall 
growth.
Private consumption is projected to grow by 
4.8% in 2027, supported by sustained domestic 
economic activities and favourable employment 
prospects. Meanwhile, tourism-related activities, 
including the VM2026 – 2027, together with 
major events such as the LIMA 2027 and 34th
SEA Games are expected to provide additional 
impetus to consumer spending.
Private investment is projected to expand by 
7.1% in 2027, supported by continued capital 
expenditure, particularly in the technology-
intensive services and manufacturing sectors. 
Areas of investment include hyperscale data 
centres and AI infrastructures, advanced 
semiconductors, as well as chemical and 
petrochemical activities. While investment 
activity remains on a fi rm footing, private 
investment growth is anticipated to normalise 
following the strong growth in the preceding 
years. The execution of ongoing multi-year 
projects will further sustain investment 
momentum. In addition, robust adoption of 
advanced technologies and digitalisation is 
expected to support productivity improvements 
and enhance production capacity. The policy 
environment will also remain supportive, with 
the ongoing implementation of national policies 
targeting high-impact sectors and the outcome-
based NIF prioritising investment quality 
over the volume of approvals, contributing to 
continued private investment activity.
Public consumption is forecast to grow 
by 3.2% in 2027. Growth will be supported 
by emolument spending and expenditure 
on supplies and services. The Government 
Procurement Act 2026 [Act 882] which is 
expected to come into force in 2027, will 
reinforce these efforts by strengthening 
governance, enhancing transparency and 
embedding value-for-money principles in 
procurement, thereby reducing the risks of 
leakage, wastage and mismanagement of 
public funds. Together, these measures are 
expected to support efficient public service 
delivery while maintaining fiscal discipline and 
ensuring prudent public spending. 
INFOrMatION BOX 3.3
Anchoring Malaysia’s Momentum Through GEAR-uP
In 2024, the Ministry of Finance (MoF) initiated the Government-Linked Enterprises Activation 
and Reform Programme (GEAR-uP) to strengthen strategic investments, innovation and social 
inclusion in supporting the nation’s economic development agenda. The Programme mobilises 
six government-linked investment companies (GLICs), namely Employees Provident Fund (EPF), 
Permodalan Nasional Berhad (PNB), Retirement Fund (Incorporated) (KWAP), Khazanah Nasional 
Berhad (Khazanah), Lembaga Tabung Haji (Tabung Haji) and Lembaga Tabung Angkatan Tentera 
(LTAT), together with the government-linked companies (GLCs) under their ownership. The initiatives 
under these GLICs are aligned with the twin pillars of Ekonomi MADANI, namely Raising the Ceiling 
of national potential and Raising the Floor of living standards. The six GLICs collectively manage 
RM2.2 trillion in assets under management, exceeding Malaysia’s GDP in 2025.

ecONOMIc OUtLOOK   2027
127
chapter 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
Under GEAR-uP, the GLICs have committed RM120 billion in domestic direct investments (DDI) 
from 2024 to 2028, in addition to RM440 billion in steady state public market investments already 
channelled in the economy. In 2025, GLICs allocated and deployed RM20.3 billion domestically. 
While below the projected RM25 billion target announced in Budget 2025, this represents a 
threefold increase from the RM6.6 billion allocated and deployed in 2024, with momentum carrying 
into the first quarter of 2026. This is patient capital intended to crowd in private investments and 
build a productive base that lasts.
In 2025, the programme widened its reach to the GLCs owned by the GLICs, extending coordination 
from capital allocation to the operating companies that put it to work. The combined scale provides 
a substantial base for implementation, with the 37 GLCs sustaining 40,126 vendors across their 
supply chains and, together with the GLICs, employing 206,987 Malaysian employees, setting a 
benchmark for the wider corporate sector. Figure 3.3.1 summarises GEAR-uP’s 2025 contributions. 
 Raising the Ceiling: Beyond Capital to Capabilities
Raising the Ceiling entails growing Malaysia’s capability to build and make, widening the productive 
capacity of the economy for the years ahead. GLICs and GLCs are pursuing this by investing across 
three themes, namely advancing economic complexity, strengthening firms of all sizes and maturity, 
and catalysing national priorities.
 Advancing economic complexity
Malaysia holds approximately 13% of the global market share in semiconductor testing and 
packaging. The focus is on moving into higher margin segments of the value chain. Under this 
initiative, GLICs invested RM1.4 billion in the semiconductor ecosystem and RM1.8 billion in digital 
and logistics infrastructure in 2025.
There are several highlights in this ecosystem driven by GEAR-uP. Among others is SkyeChip, 
a Pulau Pinang-based integrated circuit (IC) design firm, which debuted on Bursa Malaysia’s 
MAIN Market on 20 May 2026 following early stage backing from Khazanah’s Dana Impak. The 
offering raised RM352 million in gross proceeds and was oversubscribed 95.03 times, with GLICs 
subscribing 15.5% as cornerstone investors and more than 60% of proceeds allocated to R&D in 
silicon intellectual property. Technology transfer is progressing on the same basis. Khazanah’s 
2024 investment in the edge artificial intelligence (AI) firm Syntiant anchored the expansion of its 
operations in Pulau Pinang, where a new 220,000 sq. ft. campus localises complex micro-electro-
mechanical systems (MEMS) processing and AI engineering, creating 200 high-skilled roles out of 
800 new jobs.
Source: Ministry of Finance, Malaysia
