 Strengthening firms of all sizes and maturity
GEAR-uP also contributes to building the capacity of businesses across the whole spectrum 
beginning with Government agencies, including Cradle Fund, Perbadanan Usahawan Nasional 
Berhad (PUNB) and VentureTech, supporting companies at the inception stage. Khazanah’s 
Jelawang Capital and KWAP’s Dana Perintis anchor the venture stage, channelling RM588 million to 
startups in the consumer, digital, agritech and edtech sectors in 2025. Khazanah’s Dana Impak and 
KWAP’s Dana Pemacu subsequently provide growth capital to firms ready to scale, before GLICs 
take cornerstone positions at the point of listing, with Ekuinas deepening Bumiputera ownership 
throughout.
Between 2022 and June 2026, more than 130 Malaysian startups were supported through 11 
partner venture funds, with close to 30 being Bumiputera-owned, as illustrated in Figure 3.3.2. One 
of the companies backed include Aonic, a drone technology firm now serving 15 countries, whose 
farmer clients report income gains of up to 50%. In addition, international fund managers including 
AppWorks and Antler have localised operations in Malaysia to support the capital deployment. 
Source: Ministry of Finance, Malaysia
FIGURE 3.3.2. Malaysia’s Venture Capital and Crowd-In the Startup Ecosystem
While mid-tier companies (MTC) contribute 36% of GDP and employ 16% of the workforce, yet 
many face constraints in capital, capabilities and market access at the point of scaling. GLICs 
address these constraints through a staged pathway to enable companies to progress from one 
stage of growth to the next with continuity of institutional backing. KWAP’s Dana Pemacu allocated 
and deployed RM1.2 billion through 12 fund managers in 2025, each paired with an international 
partner under a co-investment model. Investments comprise RM254 million through Seraya 
Partners in Empyrion Digital, a hyperscale AI data centre in Johor, with first energisation targeted 
for November 2026; RM51 million through Mekar Capital and Navis Capital in a local nutraceutical 
company; and RM210 million through Foster Capital and Castleforge for the first phase of 
centralised labour quarters in the Klang Valley, providing 9,000 beds. Furthermore, capacity building 
is provided through the MTC Growth Innovation Programme (MGIP) and ELEVATE Programme 2.0 
with Capital Markets Malaysia, targeting up to 100 mid-tier companies.

ecONOMIc OUtLOOK   2027
129
chapter 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
Catalysing national priorities
GLICs actively steward the portfolio of 37 GLCs, targeting 7.5% annualised total shareholder returns 
from 2024 to 2028, a trajectory that could unlock RM100 billion in value. Performance has outpaced 
expectations, with GLICs delivering total shareholder returns of 8% for full year 2025. As GLCs 
account for around 27% of market capitalisation on Bursa Malaysia’s MAIN Market, the gains flow 
back to EPF, KWAP, PNB and Tabung Haji members, lifting ordinary Malaysians’ savings.
This stewardship rests on clear targets benchmarked against best-in-class sector peers, comprising 
10.3% return on equity, RM50 billion in net profit, 23% EBITDA margin and 55% dividend payout 
ratio by 2028, as summarised in Figure 3.3.3. Beyond the targets, GLICs set the standard for 
the wider corporate sector in disclosure and the discipline of long-term value creation. Under 
the Capital Market Masterplan 2026 – 2030, the Securities Commission Malaysia (SC) and Bursa 
Malaysia introduced MY Value Up in April 2026, encouraging the 88 largest listed companies, which 
account for around 80% of market value, to disclose value creation plans.

GLICs and GLCs deploy patient capital in foundational sectors where national priorities align with 
the mandates to deliver sustainable returns. Focus has been given to progress the identified 
sectors leveraging the GLCs’ strength to advance the industries’ needs.
In healthcare, the RM1.9 billion privatisation of APEX Healthcare, Malaysia’s largest pharmaceuticals 
buy-out to date, establishes an integrated manufacturing and distribution platform. Pharmaniaga, 
strategically supported by LTAT, has established a locally-owned insulin and vaccine manufacturing 
segment with capacity for 25 million vaccine doses and 30 million insulin cartridges annually, 
alongside a three-year RM281.7 million supply contract with the Ministry of Health Malaysia.
In uplifting the agriculture sector, emphasis is placed on modernisation and improvement of 
median wage. For example, SD Guthrie’s Mechanisation, Automation and Digitalisation (MAD) 
programme has deployed 950 machines since 2021 and created 514 local machine specialist 
positions with a median monthly salary of RM3,115, above the RM3,100 living wage benchmark 
(EPF, 2025). Meanwhile, in the energy sector, Tenaga Nasional Berhad (TNB) is undertaking a 
RM42.8 billion grid investment from 2025 to 2027, the largest in its history, having invested RM12 
billion in 2025. These investments will support the target of 10 GW of renewable capacity by 2030.

ecONOMIc OUtLOOK   2027
130
chapter 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
In strengthening national connectivity, a consortium anchored by Khazanah and EPF has committed 
RM11 billion over five years to upgrade airports and logistics infrastructures. Building on an annual 
passenger volume that reached 104.5 million in 2025, this capital deployment will drive major 
capacity expansions across KLIA, Pulau Pinang and Kota Kinabalu alongside logistics hubs such 
as the Penang International Logistics Aeropark. On a separate note, within digital infrastructure, 
investments span corridors in Pulau Pinang, Port Klang and Iskandar Malaysia, including 
KWAP’s RM319 million investment in a Google data centre at Puncak Alam and a RM550 million 
commitment by EPF and LTAT to Sime Darby’s New Economy Venture Fund. As at March 2026, 
Malaysia had secured 59 data centres and hyperscale projects, of which 36 are operational.
While driving economic complexity, GEAR-uP programmes also encompass the preservation 
of Malaysia’s heritage. Warisan KL, launched in April 2025, driven by Khazanah, PNB Merdeka 
Ventures, public institutions and government agencies to conserve the capital’s-built heritage. 
Warisan KL is projected to generate RM7.9 billion in economic value and attract more than 9.1 
million visitors by 2035. Khazanah’s restoration of Bangunan Sultan Abdul Samad and the old 
General Post Office reopened in early 2026 and records an average daily footfall of 5,000. Seri 
Negara, where the 1948 Federation of Malaya and 1957 Independence agreements were signed, 
reopened in December 2025, while Carcosa is being restored as an eco-sanctuary hotel. PNB 
Merdeka Ventures acquired Stadium Merdeka, which was then restored to the original 20,000-
seat capacity, a project recognised with a UNESCO Asia Pacific Award, while the Merdeka Textile 
Museum was launched in August 2026.
 Raising the Floor: Ensuring a Quality and Just Life for All
Raising the Floor entails ensuring the benefits of growth are felt by all, measured not only by 
the wealth an economy creates but also by how widely that wealth is shared. With approximately 
207,000 permanent Malaysian employees, more than 40,000 vendors and a presence in every state, 
GLICs and GLCs are positioned to lift the floor across three themes, namely future-proofing the 
workforce, strengthening community livelihoods and wellbeing as well as championing decent living 
wages.
 Future-proofing the workforce
In Q2 2026, approximately 35% of tertiary educated Malaysians worked below their qualification 
levels (DOSM, 2026), while strategic sectors such as energy, digital and advanced manufacturing 
report shortages of credentialled talent. GLICs and GLCs funded an average of 7,526 scholars 
annually over 2020 to 2025, with 88% from lower-to-middle income households, exceeding the 
Budget 2025 aspiration that at least half of scholarships be allocated to these families.
In enhancing youth employability, Khazanah’s K-Youth has trained 35,000 youths since 2023 and 
placed 9,934 in 2025 with employers including Infineon, Intel, Onsemi, SkyeChip and Vitrox. At 
the same time, TNB’s PROTEGE-Ready to Work programme has trained 7,216 Malaysians since its 
inception in 2011, with 559 hired into TNB. In June 2026, these efforts were consolidated under 
BAKAT MADANI, a national talent platform uniting PETRONAS, the six GLICs and their GLCs, 
providing an integrated pathway from education and training to certification and placement, 
targeting 25,000 Malaysians by end-2027. 

ECONOMIC OUTLOOK   2027
131
CHAPTER 3   MACROECONOMIC OUTLOOK
 Strengthening community livelihoods and wellbeing
GLICs and GLCs directed RM641 million into community investment in 2025, an increase of 20% 
from RM534 million in 2024, across more than 775 programmes reaching at least 5.1 million 
beneficiary touchpoints. Cumulative contributions from 2021 to 2025 totalled RM2.6 billion. The 
MOF Social Impact Matching Grant doubles every foundation ringgit for community use, while 
the Sekolah Angkat and Santuni MADANI programmes have reached 399 communities. GLC Demi 
Rakyat dan Negara (GDRN), the collaborative philanthropy platform of GLICs and GLCs, pools 
member resources for disaster response, channelling RM22.4 million in humanitarian assistance 
and reaching 580,426 beneficiary touchpoints throughout the country over the 2025 and 2026 
monsoon season.
 Championing decent living wages
In May 2025, following the joint commitment by the six GLICs, 189,817 out of 206,987 (91.7%) 
permanent Malaysian employees across the GLIC and GLC ecosystem, earned at least a decent 
living wage of RM3,100 monthly as of December 2025. This figure is reviewed by GLICs and GLCs 
every three years, based on the cost of living published under EPF’s Belanjawanku. As at July 2026, 
25 entities out of the six GLICs and 37 GLCs had all permanent Malaysian employees receiving 
decent living wages. Figure 3.3.4 highlights the progress of decent living wages initiative. 
 Outlook
The majority of the RM120 billion commitment remains to be deployed over the next two years. 
On economic complexity, the semiconductor ecosystem continues to widen through SemiconStart 
Malaysia’s cohorts and the cross-border funds anchored in 2025, while hyperscale and logistics 
capacity keeps building. Google’s data centre in Selangor adds a further 320 MW and 26,500 jobs 
through 2026 and 2027, alongside Empyrion Digital’s phased build-out in Johor.

On firms and capital markets, GLICs’ funds including Dana Impak, Dana Perintis, Dana Pemacu and 
Ekuinas continue to carry companies from venture to growth stage, with Khazanah’s planned Dana 
Ciptawan adding a further RM200 million for Bumiputera enterprises and other mid-tier Malaysian 
firms. GLCs remain on course for RM100 billion in additional market value by 2028, with MY Value 
Up now extending the same discipline to Malaysia’s 88 largest-listed companies. 
On national priorities, TNB grid investment continues under Regulatory Period 4, rising from 
RM12 billion in 2025 towards RM15 billion in 2027, as the country works towards 70% renewable 
energy in installed capacity by 2050. MAHB continues its five year, RM11 billion upgrade 
programme, with KLIA’s capacity plan targeting over 100 million passengers annually and Penang 
International Airport’s capacity set to double from 6.5 million passengers. Ultimately, by expanding 
talent platforms like BAKAT MADANI to 25,000 Malaysians and entrenching decent living wage 
commitments across the ecosystem, the programme ensures that gains in economic complexity 
directly translate into higher living standards for all.
Conclusion
Guided by the Ekonomi MADANI framework, the GEAR-uP initiative is successfully building an 
economy that simultaneously raises the nation’s ceiling of growth and floor of social well-being. 
By converting strategic capital deployment into enduring national capability, the initiative fosters a 
deep, competitive ecosystem that turns local firms into market champions, strengthens domestic 
supply chains and accelerates high-value job creation. True economic success goes beyond 
attracting headline investment, measured by the lasting impact capital leaves behind, delivering 
higher living standards for the rakyat, towards a strong and resilient Malaysia.
Public investment is projected to expand by 
6.8% in 2027, driven by targeted development 
spending and steady capital outlays. Federal 
Government development spending will continue 
to prioritise rakyat-centric projects, particularly 
upgrades in critical infrastructures for healthcare 
and education. Meanwhile, capital expenditure by 
NFPCs is expected to remain focused on strategic 
sectors, including energy, utility and transport, 
reinforced by targeted government-linked 
investment companies’ (GLICs) commitments 
under Government-Linked Enterprises Activation 
and Reform Programme (GEAR-uP) initiative. 
Investment activity will also be supported by the 
continued implementation of ongoing high-impact 
projects, including the Penang LRT Mutiara Line, 
ECRL Phase 2, SSLR, Pan Borneo Highway Sabah 
and the upgrading of Klang Valley Double Track 
Phase 2 (KVDT2). These investments are expected 
to strengthen national connectivity, improve 
public service delivery, expand the economy’s 
productive capacity as well as support sustainable 
and inclusive growth.
Driven by expanding domestic activity, GNI at 
current prices is forecast to grow by 6.2% in 
2027. GNS and total investment are projected 
to reach 24% and 22.2% of GNI, respectively, 
yielding a savings-investment gap of RM41.8 
billion or 1.9% of GNI to ensure investment can 
be funded largely through domestic sources.
Income
Higher wage progression required to uplift 
income share
The CE is projected to maintain a steady 
growth trajectory of 6.8% in 2027. Sustained 
wage progression will be driven by institutional 
and regulatory labour market reforms, 
enhanced productivity-linked wage-setting 
frameworks and more synchronised labour 
supply-demand initiatives. Additionally, the 
periodic review of the GEAR-uP living wage 
framework, aimed at mitigating cost of living 
pressures among workers in GLCs and GLICs, is

expected to generate positive wage spillovers 
and motivate other private sector players 
to pay higher wages to their employees. On

expected to further boost tourism-related 
industries’ growth momentum specifically 
related to retail trade, food and beverage,

as well as support services activities, thereby

support a higher labour income share of

by 5.2%, consisting 62.1% share of GDP 
in 2027. Malaysia’s strategic integration 
along the global value chain, coupled with

by 16.5%, attributed by ongoing efforts in 
improving tax compliance and tightening 
enforcement to comply with strict fiscal 
discipline goals. Meanwhile, expenditure on 
subsidies and incentives is expected to decline 
by 7.5% as the Government remains committed 
to implement subsidy targeting to reduce fiscal 
leakages. Hence, income share from net taxes 
on production and imports is projected to 
contribute 3.8% of GDP in 2027. 
External Sector
Resilient and Supportive Outlook
Gross exports in 2027 are forecast to 
increase by 3%, driven by buoyant demand 
for manufactured goods, mining goods and 
agriculture goods. Exports of manufactured 
goods are projected to expand by 2.6% 
following steady performance in the E&E 
industry, particularly semiconductors, backed by 
continuous demand for AI-related products. The 
World Semiconductor Trade Statistics anticipated 
global semiconductor sales to increase by 27% 
in 2027, contributed by higher technology-
related activities as well as increasing demand 
for E&E products from major trading partners. 
The solid performance is aligned with stronger 
global trade and economic growth in major 
trading partners as forecast by the IMF in July 
2026. Hence, Malaysia is expected to benefit 
from the positive outlook as reflected in 
stronger demand for E&E products and non-E&E 
products. 
Likewise, receipts from mining goods are 
anticipated to grow by 8.3% attributed to 
sustained exports of LNG, crude petroleum as 
well as metalliferous ores and metal scrap. This 
growth will be supported by stronger demand 
from major trading partners subsequent 
to market diversification and expansion. 
Furthermore, exports of agriculture goods are 
projected to rise by 4.8%, mainly driven by 
higher shipments of palm oil and palm oil based 
agriculture products following an increase in 
export volume and favourable commodity prices. 
Gross imports are estimated to expand by 3.5% 
on the back of higher imports of intermediate 
goods, capital goods and consumption goods. 
Imports of intermediate goods are expected to 
increase by 3.4% following optimistic prospect 
in the manufacturing sector. Correspondingly, 
imports of capital goods are forecast to grow 
by 3.9% on account of stronger investment 
in services-related activities, expansion

in manufacturing sector and upgrading 
in technology infrastructure. Imports of 
consumption goods are expected to increase by 
3.1%, among others, supported by robust and 
competitive online shopping platforms. 
The current account of the balance of 
payments is forecast to register a wider surplus 
of RM41.8 billion or 1.9% of GNI in 2027, 
contributed by positive balances in the goods 
and services accounts as well as narrowing 
deficit in the secondary income account, 
despite higher net outflow in the primary 
income account. Surplus in the goods account 
is projected to narrow to RM130.6 billion 
owing to growth in imports outpacing exports. 
Higher imports are expected particularly 
in the healthcare and education sectors as 
well as infrastructure projects towards the 
implementation of rakyat-centric projects. 
Meanwhile, the services account is projected 
to record a wider net inflow of RM17.8 billion, 
attributed to higher surplus in the travel 
account and narrowing deficit in the other 
services account, despite a larger net outflow  
in the transport account. The travel account  
is forecast to register a higher surplus of 
RM61.3 billion, propelled by vigorous  
tourism activities. Earnings in the travel  
account is estimated to increase following 
VM2026 – 2027, LIMA 2027 and 34th SEA 
Games, which are anticipated to attract more 
international tourist arrivals and encourage 
higher per capita tourist spending. Nonetheless, 
stronger residents’ spending abroad for 
business, education and pilgrimage is expected 
to increase payments in the travel account. 
The other services account is anticipated to 
register a narrowing deficit of RM14.1 billion 
backed by higher income contributed by 
data centre- and cloud computing-related 
activities. The realisation of investment in data 
centre and robust adoption of digitalisation 
is expected to be reflected by higher receipts 
in the telecommunication, computer and 
information services. Similarly, net inflow  
in the manufacturing services on physical 
inputs owned by others is expected to  
increase following an encouraging outlook 
in the E&E industry. Nonetheless, deficit in 
the transport account is anticipated to widen 
to RM29.4 billion stemming from continuous 
reliance on foreign transport providers in 
tandem with sustain trade activities. 
The primary income account is projected to 
record a wider net outflow of RM94.7 billion 
due to higher deficits in the investment 
income and compensation of employees. The 
investment income account is anticipated to 
register a larger net outflow subsequent to 
increasing repatriation of profits and dividends 
by foreign investors in Malaysia. Similarly, 
deficit in the compensation of employees 
account is estimated to widen due to increasing 
participation of foreign professionals. On the 
other hand, a net outflow in the secondary 
income account is estimated to narrow to  
RM12 billion, attributed to higher inward 
remittances by Malaysians working abroad. 
Prices
Inflation remains contained amid gradual cost 
pass-through
Headline inflation is projected to range 
between 1.8% and 2.8% in 2027. The forecast 
reflects carry-over effects and lagged 
transmission of higher energy, food and other 
input costs. Inflation is expected to be firmer in 
the early months of 2027 before easing as the 
impact of earlier cost increases diminishes and 
upstream costs gradually normalise. Domestic 
price pressures are anticipated to remain 
manageable, with economic activity unlikely to 
generate demand-pull inflationary pressures. 
Nevertheless, services-related categories, 
including food away from home, housing-
related services and personal care, remain the 
main sources of underlying price pressure.
Risks to the outlook are tilted to the upside. 
A renewed escalation of geopolitical tensions, 
prolonged disruptions to energy and supply 
chains, higher freight and fertiliser costs as well 
as weather-related food supply shocks can lead 
to stronger and more persistent inflation. Further 
adjustments to administered prices, utilities 
or subsidy mechanisms could also affect the 

economic outlook   2027
135
chapter 3   macroeconomic outlook
inflation trajectory. On the downside, a faster 
normalisation of global crude oil and food prices, 
stronger ringgit and softer domestic demand 
could keep inflation below the baseline projection.
Labour Market
Labour market remains resilience 
 
The labour market in 2027 is projected to 
remain resilient, anchored by robust domestic 
demand despite persistent geopolitical and 
trade-related headwinds. On the supply 
side, structural reforms under the Ekonomi 
MADANI framework such as strengthened 
wage structures, expanded social protection 
and improved workplace environments are 
expected to boost labour force participation. On 
the demand side, targeted implementation of 
national master plans, including the Thirteenth 
Malaysia Plan, 2026 – 2030 (Thirteenth Plan), 
NSS and National Energy Transition Roadmap 
(NETR) are anticipated to accelerate job 
creation. Accordingly, the unemployment 
rate is expected to remain low at 3% in 2027. 
Meanwhile, total employment is projected to 
expand by 2% to 17.2 million persons, with the 
services and manufacturing sectors continuing 
to account for more than 80% of employment 
opportunities.
Under the Thirteenth Plan, the threshold 
of foreign workers will not be allowed to 
exceed 10% of total employment by 2030. 
Therefore, the number of low-skilled foreign 
workers is expected to continue to decrease 
in 2027. In contrast, expatriate employment 
is anticipated to increase slightly, driven by 
demand for specialised talent across high 
growth high value (HGHV) sectors such as 
advanced engineering, digital technology and 
green-energy jobs. To reduce over reliance 
on migrant workers, greater emphasis will be 
placed on enhancing productivity through wider 
adoption of technology and shifting towards 
capital deepening, specifically automation and 
digitalisation in labour-intensive industries. 
These efforts will also be complemented by 
higher levy structures and expanded talent 
development through TVET and upskilling 
programmes to build local technical capacity.
Labour productivity is projected to increase 
by 3.7% to RM112,098 in 2027, primarily 
driven by sustained productivity momentum 
in the services sector, particularly tourism-
related industries following the VM2026 – 2027. 
Long-term productivity gains will be further 
reinforced by industrial upgrading, resource 
efficiency and workforce upskilling. Additionally, 
institutionalising productivity-linked wage 
structures through initiatives like PROSHARE5 
will help align real wage growth with output, 
boosting economic value creation and supporting 
a higher CE share of GDP over the longer term.
Conclusion
Malaysia’s solid economic fundamentals provide 
ample buffer against global shocks and enables 
the economy to sustain its growth momentum. 
Domestic demand remains the primary source 
of growth, anchored by a strong labour 
market and vigorous private consumption, 
while investment activity is anticipated 
to continue expanding, supported by the 
ongoing realisation of approved projects. 
Concurrently, trade performance remains well-
positioned to leverage continuous technological 
advancements. The services sector will 
be boosted by increased international 
connectivity and vibrant tourism activities 
tied to VM2026 – 2027. Simultaneously, the 
E&E segment in the manufacturing sector 
is expected to benefit from strong global 
demand for semiconductors, AI and digital 
technologies. Looking ahead, the continued 
implementation of structural reforms and 
national development initiatives under the 
Ekonomi MADANI framework are expected 
to strengthen productive capacity, enhance 
overall competitiveness, ensure a meaningful 
economic growth and generate greater 
prosperity for the rakyat.
5 PROSHARE or Productivity for Shared Prosperity is a Malaysia Productivity Corporation initiative to help firms assess productivity, identify 
performance gaps and implement targeted improvements to translate productivity into sustainable growth, profit and fair wages.

economic outlook   2027
chapter 4   monetary and financial developments
143
Overview 
Financial market remains resilient to withstand 
external headwinds
Amid a complex global environment, Malaysia’s 
financial market remains resilient and orderly 
in supporting economic activity. The banking 
sector continues to meet the financing needs 
of businesses and households, while the 
capital market provides market-based funding 
to drive business expansion and productive 
investment. The current monetary policy stance 
remains consistent with the outlook for price 
stability and sustainable economic growth. 
The ongoing development of Islamic finance 
further broadens financing and investment 
opportunities, reinforcing depth and diversity 
of the domestic financial ecosystem.
Monetary Developments
Steady monetary policy supports stable prices 
and sustainable growth 
The Monetary Policy Committee (MPC) of 
Bank Negara Malaysia (BNM) maintained the 
Overnight Policy Rate (OPR) at 2.75% in the 
first nine months of 2026, unchanged since  
the 25 bps reduction in July 2025. The 
monetary policy stance is consistent with 
the outlook of continued price stability and 
sustainable economic growth. Domestic 
economic activity is anchored by sound 
fundamentals, while inflationary pressures 
remained contained. The Statutory Reserve 
Requirement (SRR) ratio was maintained at 
1.00%, ensuring sufficient liquidity and effective 
financial intermediation within the domestic 
banking system.
chapter 4
Monetary and Financial Developments
Board fixed deposit rates moderated, while the 
interbank rate and lending rates were broadly 
stable as at end-July 2026. The 1-month and 
12-month board fixed deposit rates declined by 
2 and 10 bps to 2.06% and 2.20% respectively, 
reflecting banks’ funding strategies to manage 
cost of funds. The interbank rate, as indicated 
by the 3-month Kuala Lumpur Interbank 
Offered Rate (KLIBOR) remained at 3.45% 
in July after rising during the first half of 
2026 amid seasonal funding pressures and

in line with domestic growth and inflation 
considerations.
Performance of Ringgit
Ringgit remained resilient amid external 
headwinds 
The ringgit strengthened in early 2026, 
appreciating by 4.3% against the US dollar 
over the first two months of the year 
(NEER1: +3.2%). The positive momentum was 
subsequently reversed following escalation of 
the West Asia conflict at the end of February 
2026, with the ringgit depreciating by 3.3% 
against the US dollar at end-August 2026 
(NEER: -2.9%). Nevertheless, on a year-to-date 
basis, the ringgit appreciated by 0.9% against 
the US dollar as at end-August 2026, while 
strengthening against most major and regional 
currencies, including euro (1.7%), Japanese 
yen (3.0%), Philippine peso (6.7%), Indonesian 
rupiah (6.6%) and Thailand baht (5.1%). 
Despite external headwinds, Malaysia’s strong 
economic position and ongoing structural 
reforms have sustained market confidence, 
providing underlying support to the ringgit. 
BNM has also continued to strengthen the 
domestic foreign exchange market through 
engagements with government-linked 
investment companies (GLICs), government-
linked companies (GLCs) and private  
corporates to encourage the timely repatriation 
Monetary policy stances across major and 
regional economies remained divergent for 
January – September 2026. The US Federal 
Reserve (Fed) has increased the federal funds 
rate by 25 bps to 3.75% – 4.00% in September 
2026. In the region, Bank Indonesia raised 
the BI-Rate from 4.75% to 5.75%, as policy 
geared towards supporting rupiah stability 
and containing inflationary pressures. On

Banking Sector 
Performance 
Banking sector remains well positioned to absorb 
shocks and support growth 
The banking system remained resilient 
to withstand adverse shocks, sustaining 
the provision of credit to households and 
businesses, underpinned by strong capital 
buffers and sufficient liquidity. As at end-July 
2026, the Common Equity Tier 1 Capital (CET1), 
Tier 1 Capital and Total Capital ratios were 
above the regulatory minimum level2 at 14.6%, 
15.1% and 18.2%, respectively. Liquidity was 
robust, with the aggregate Liquidity Coverage 
Ratio (LCR) at 148.7% and Net Stable Funding 
Ratio (NSFR) at 114.8%3. This reflects banks’ 
strong capacity to manage short-term liquidity 
needs and maintain stable funding over the 
longer term. The loan-to-fund ratio held 
steady at 82.8%, driven by continued growth 
in deposits, which accounted for 73.2% of total 
funding. 
From an asset quality perspective, the low 
gross impaired loans ratio of 1.4% as at 
end-July 2026 indicates that most borrowers 
continue to meet their repayment obligations. 
Additionally, the banking system’s loan loss 
coverage ratio (including regulatory reserves) 
remained adequate at 125% of gross impaired 
loans, reflecting banks’ continued prudent 
provisioning practices.

(RWA). Meanwhile, for LCR and NSFR, the minimum requirement is 100%.

Total outstanding loans rose by 5.6% to 
RM2,435.1 billion as at end-July 2026, 
underpinned by continued expansion in 
financing to the business sector, which grew 
by 8%. Compared with the same period in the 
previous year, financing demand was robust 
with loan applications and approvals recording 
double-digit growths of 10% to RM975.7 billion 
and 14.7% to RM517.4 billion, respectively. The 
increase in credit demand was mainly 
for working capital purposes. In addition, 
banking system’s repayment activities 
rebounded by 3.6% to RM1,287.7 billion, in 
line with higher loan disbursements of 4.3% to 
RM1,340.9 billion.

The household segment continued to account 
for the largest share of total banking system 
loans, particularly for purchase of residential 
properties and passenger cars. As at  
end-July 2026, loan applications rose by 3.3% 
amounting to RM507.6 billion, while  
loan approvals and disbursements  
rebounded by 1.9% to RM221 billion and 
3.3% to RM303.6 billion, respectively. Loan 
repayment performance increased by 3.7% 
or RM298.4 billion, consistent with steady 
income growth and household debt-servicing 
capacity. Moving forward, household financing 
is expected to be supported by stable labour 
market conditions, sustained household 
spending and income-related policy measures, 
including the implementation of phase two of 
the salary adjustment under the Public Service 
Remuneration System (SSPA). 
Financing conditions remained favourable, 
supported by continued access to financing, 
resilient credit flows and ongoing  
policy support. As at end-July 2026,  
financing activity in the business segment 
was firm, supported by sustained demand 
for working capital and investment-related 
loans. Loan applications expanded by 19.2% to 
RM441 billion, while approvals rose by 29.8% 
to RM275.1 billion, reflecting stronger financing 
needs. Disbursements to the business sector 
increased by 5.1% to RM899.3 billion, with 
SMEs accounting for 35.1% during the period. 
Higher disbursement growth was observed 
particularly in the E&E and ICT sectors.

economic outlook   2027
chapter 4   monetary and financial developments
148
As at June 2026, Malaysia’s household debt 
stood at RM1,754.2 billion, equivalent to 83.2% 
of GDP, improved from 84.7% at end-2025, 
backed by stronger nominal GDP growth. 
Household debt growth also moderated and 
broadly aligned with historical trends, mainly 
driven by financing residential properties and 
passenger cars, which collectively accounted 
for 75.3% of total household debt. Despite 
the elevated debt level, household financial 
positions remained sound, supported by strong 
asset holdings and healthy net financial wealth. 
Households demonstrated adequate debt-
servicing capacity, supported by favourable 
labour market conditions and steady income 
growth. The median debt service ratio (DSR) 
stood at 32.1% with median DSRs across all 
income groups remaining below 60%, while 
the median debt-to-income ratio was stable at 
1.3 times. The household impaired-loans ratio 
remained low at 1.1%. These indicators suggest 
that households generally maintain sufficient 
financial capacity to meet debt obligations, 
thereby mitigating risks to overall financial 
stability.
Moving forward, household credit risk is 
expected to remain stable and manageable. 
The Government and BNM continue to 
promote sustainable household borrowing 
through prudent underwriting and loan 
affordability assessments under BNM’s 
responsible financing requirements and 
guidelines. Targeted repayment assistance 
is also available to borrowers experiencing 
financial difficulties. BNM is strengthening 
pre-emptive credit risk management 
through enhanced requirements to promote 
responsible borrowing and more risk-sensitive 
underwriting. Starting 1 January 2027, financial 
consumers applying for new personal financing 
exceeding RM100,000 will be required to 
complete a financial education module 
conducted by the financial service provider or 
the Credit Counselling and Debt Management 
Agency (AKPK). Financial service providers will 
also be required to apply prudent DSR and 
minimum net disposable income thresholds 
that take into account borrowers’ income levels 
and household circumstances. Complementing 
these measures, initiatives by the Financial 
Education Network (FEN) under the National 
Strategy for Financial Literacy 2026 – 2030, 
will further enhanced households’ financial 
capability and long-term resilience.
At the same time, utilisation of alternative 
financing and payment arrangements,  
including Buy Now, Pay Later (BNPL) facility 
continues to grow. These arrangements provide 
flexibility in managing purchases and short-
term cash flows. However, this may increase 
financial commitments, particularly when 
multiple credit facilities are used concurrently. 
This reinforces the need for consumers to 
assess their repayment capacity before taking 
on additional financial obligations, highlighting 
the importance of prudent financial 
management.

chapter 4   monetary and financial developments
149
Capital Market 
Performance
Capital market fundraising gains momentum
Fundraising activities in the domestic capital 
market were vibrant during the first seven 
months of 2026, supported by sustained 
investor demand and conducive domestic 
market conditions. Gross private sector 
fundraising increased by 58.9% to  
RM120.7 billion, driven by higher activities in 
both the equity and corporate bond markets. 
Gross funds raised in the equity market 
expanded by 34.4% to RM5.8 billion, while 
corporate bond issuances surged by 60.4% to 
RM114.9 billion.
Corporations continued to tap the debt  
capital market to diversify funding sources 
and secure longer-term financing. Relatively 
favourable funding conditions also facilitated  
market-based financing by creditworthy issuers. 
Medium-term notes remained the predominant 
instrument, accounting for 96.9% of total 
corporate bond issuances. Activities were 
concentrated in finance, insurance, real estate 
and business services (65.6%); construction 
(13.5%); and electricity, gas and water (11.4%) 
sectors. 
During the first seven months of 2026,  
42 initial public offerings (IPOs) across 
diverse sectors raised RM5.8 billion, reflecting 
sustained interest in equity market financing 
as companies sought capital to scale up 
operations and pursue new investment 
opportunities. This momentum was further 
supported by ongoing efforts by Bursa 
Malaysia and the Securities Commission 
Malaysia (SC) to enhance market accessibility, 
streamline the listing process and facilitate 
equity fundraising.

taBLe 4.3. Funds Raised in the Capital Market,
January – July 2025 and 2026

economic outlook   2027
chapter 4   monetary and financial developments
150
Private sector fundraising was driven by 
continuous investment momentum, particularly 
in semiconductors, digital infrastructure, 
data centres and cloud computing, as well 
as advanced manufacturing and other high-
value activities. Government initiatives under 
the New Industrial Master Plan 2030 (NIMP 
2030) and National Energy Transition Roadmap 
(NETR), alongside developments associated 
with the Johor-Singapore Special Economic 
Zone (JS-SEZ) also provided further impetus. 
In addition, financing requirements associated 
with major infrastructure and development 
projects under Budget 2026 and the Thirteenth 
Malaysia Plan, 2026 – 2030 (Thirteenth Plan) 
are expected to support issuance activities as 
projects progress towards implementation.
Gross public sector fundraising through 
Malaysian Government Securities (MGS) and 
Malaysian Government Investment Issues 
(MGII) rose by 8% to RM110.6 billion, up from 
RM102.4 billion in the corresponding period of 
2025. MGS and MGII issuances amounted to 
RM55.4 billion and RM55.2 billion respectively, 
supporting the Federal Government’s deficit 
financing and refinancing requirements. 
However, higher redemptions reduced the net 
raise of public sector funds to RM56.7 billion. 
Demand for government securities remained 
healthy, backed by Malaysia’s deep domestic 
savings and a broad investor base. The 
Government’s borrowing programme continues 
to be guided by prudent debt management 
and fiscal consolidation objectives.
Moving forward, the domestic capital market 
is expected to continue supporting public 
and private sector financing. Corporate 
fundraising is anticipated to remain favourable, 
underpinned by financing for business 
expansion and investment, particularly across 
infrastructure, construction, utilities and 
technology-related activities. The Capital 
Market Masterplan 2026 – 2030 (CMP), 
launched by the SC in March 2026 will further 
catalyse capital formation by enhancing market 
vibrancy and elevating the value proposition 
of the domestic capital market. The ongoing 
execution of national development priorities 
and multi-year investment projects are set to 
generate further opportunities. In addition, 
the recently established regulatory cooperation 
framework between Malaysia and Hong Kong 
on dual IPO listings and the mutual 
recognition of selected investment funds is 
expected to enhance cross-border capital 
market connectivity, broaden investment 
opportunities and strengthen Malaysia’s 
attractiveness as a fundraising and investment 
destination. Meanwhile, net Government 
bond issuances are projected to moderate in 
tandem with continued fiscal consolidation 
efforts, reinforcing the capital market capacity 
to mobilise long-term funds for productive 
investment and sustainable economic growth.

seven months of 2026 amid heightened

end-2025 levels to 3.1%, 3.3%, 3.5% and

The upward movement in MGS yields 
largely reflected external uncertainties 
and developments in global bond markets. 
Higher energy prices stemming from the 
prolonged conflict in West Asia increased 
inflation expectations, while resilient economic 
conditions in advanced economies reinforced 
the expectations that interest rates would 
remain higher for longer. Collectively, these 
developments placed upward pressure on 
global bond yields, including MGS yields. 
Nevertheless, the rise in MGS yields was 
relatively contained compared with regional 
markets, supported by sustained demand 
from domestic and foreign investors. Foreign 
participation remained supportive, with net 
foreign inflows into the bond market reaching 
RM3.4 billion from January to July 2026, 
continuing the positive trend recorded in 2025 
underpinned by Malaysia’s stable interest 
rate environment and favourable domestic 
conditions. Looking ahead, foreign holdings of 
MGS are expected to remain broadly stable in 
2027, supported by Malaysia’s sound economic 
fundamentals, ongoing Government reforms 
and stable interest rate environment. However, 
changes in global financial conditions and 
broader external developments may continue 
to influence capital flows.

economic outlook   2027
chapter 4   monetary and financial developments
152
Corporate bond yields showed mixed 
movements across rating categories during  
the first seven months of 2026. The 5-year 
AAA- and AA-rated bond yields increased by  
approximately 11 and 7 bps, respectively. 
These increases remained relatively 
contained compared to the 5-year MGS yield, 
underscoring the resilience of the corporate 
bond market amid a rising interest rate 
environment. Meanwhile, the indicative 5-year 
A-rated bond yield fell by 11 bps, while the 
5-year BBB-rated bond yield declined by 59 bps 
respectively, amid strong demand for higher-
yielding credit instruments.
Performance of FBM 
KLCI
FBM KLCI remains resilient
The FBM KLCI recorded mixed performance 
during the first eight months of 2026. The 
index opened the year at 1,680.11 points and 
advanced to a seven-year high of  
1,771.25 points on 27 January, before 
retreating due to escalating geopolitical 
tensions. Notwithstanding, the domestic  
equity market regained momentum in July, 
with the FBM KLCI rising by 3.7% month-on-
month to 1,724.90 points at end-July from 
1,664.06 points at end-June, supported by 
renewed investor interest and improved 
regional sentiment. The index closed at 
1,725.88 points at end-August, with a growth 
of 2.7% year-to-date as the re-escalation of 
external volatilities tempered gains.  
Meanwhile, market capitalisation stood at 
RM2,191.73 billion with total trading volume 
and value during the first eight months 
reaching 567 billion units and RM540.9 billion, 
respectively. These developments signify the 
resilience of the domestic equity market.

taBLe 4.5. Bursa Malaysia: Selected Indicators, 
End-August 2025 and 2026
Moving forward, ongoing capital market 
initiatives, including measures under the 
CMP such as the MY Value Up Programme 
as well as enhancements to the FBM KLCI 
and FBM70 indices are set to strengthen 
market vibrancy and competitiveness. The 
expansion of the FBM KLCI from 30 to 50 
constituents to be implemented in phases 
from December 2026 to June 2027 will broaden 
coverage of Main Market capitalisation and 
improve sectoral representation. In addition, 
market segmentation review will strengthen 
public market clarity, enhance listing quality 
and provide a more structured pathway for 
companies to progress from the ACE Market to 
the Main Market. Collectively, these initiatives 
will widen investment opportunities, enhance 
market participation and facilitate more 
effective capital mobilisation for Malaysia’s 
sustainable economic development.

chapter 4   MONETARY AND FINANCIAL DEVELOPMENTS
154
INFOrMatION BOX 4.1
Key Capital Market Measures
Several key measures were undertaken by the Securities Commission Malaysia (SC) and Bursa Malaysia in the first eight months of 2026 to
 enhance the liquidity and efficiency of Malaysia’s capital market as follows:
The Bursa Malaysia Quality 50 Index (BMQ) and Bursa Malaysia Quality 50 Shariah Index (BMQ-S) are Bursa Malaysia's first financial 
performance-based indexes, designed to elevate the visibility of resilient local enterprises. The new benchmarks identify 50 financially resilient Main 
and ACE Market companies outside the FTSE Bursa Malaysia Kuala Lumpur Composite Index (FBM KLCI) based on profitability, capital structure and 
earnings quality, providing investors with a standardised tool to identify fundamentally strong companies. The indexes broaden access to quality 
investment opportunities, strengthen investor confidence and support a more vibrant Malaysian equity market.
1
Bursa Malaysia's First Financial Performance-based Indexes to 
Strengthen Quality-driven Investing
7
Equities Trading Data Reclassification to Enhance Investor 
Segmentation Transparency
6 APR
2
Malaysia’s first Social Exchange platform, Impakrintas, operational since 19 February 2026, provides a regulated and transparent avenue for non-profit 
organisations (NPOs) to raise donations for eligible social impact projects while giving donors greater visibility over their contributions. The Government 
allocated a RM2 million grant, administered by the SC, to partially defray eligible NPOs’ onboarding and fundraising costs, while donations through the 
platform qualify for tax treatment under the Income Tax Act 1967. The platform strengthens public-private collaboration in social impact financing. It will 
progressively expand to include social enterprises, community-based initiatives and other market-based avenues that support social and 
climate-resilient outcomes.
Malaysia's First Social Exchange to Strengthen Access to 
Social Impact Financing
The Capital Market Masterplan 2026 – 2030 (CMP) sets out Malaysia's five-year strategy to expand the capital market to between RM5.8 trillion – 
RM6.3 trillion by 2030 while strengthening the country's global leadership in Islamic finance through Maqasid al-Shariah-driven innovations. The 
Masterplan introduces 56 strategic initiatives to enhance market vibrancy, broaden access to capital, increase investment opportunities, accelerate 
sustainable and Islamic finance as well as strengthen regional market connectivity. Aligned with the Ekonomi MADANI framework, the CMP is projected 
to elevate the capital market at a compound annual growth rate (CAGR) of 6% – 8%, optimising market valuations and mobilising climate transition 
financing while broadening public access to wealth creation.
3
Capital Market Masterplan 2026 – 2030 to Drive Malaysia's 
Capital Market Transformation
4
The Malaysia Co-Investment Fund (MyCIF), administered by the SC, has launched two landmark funding initiatives allocating RM50 million to accelerate 
MSME growth. MyCIF introduced the Silver Economy Scheme to channel alternative financing focusing in care-tech, specialised healthcare and senior 
living solutions. Furthermore, under the VC/PE Profit-Sharing Incentive, MyCIF will share 50% of exit profits with venture capital (VC) and private equity 
(PE) firms that lead deals on regulated equity crowdfunding (ECF) platforms, bridging critical early-stage financing gaps while building a robust 
wealth-creation pipeline aligned with the Ekonomi MADANI framework.
MyCIF's Silver Economy Scheme and VC-led Profit-Sharing 
Incentive to Improve MSME Funding Access
16 MAR
5
ICM Innovation Lab to Advance Maqasid al-Shariah-Driven 
Islamic Capital Market Innovation
26 MAR
6
Bursa Malaysia and Hong Kong Exchanges and Clearing Limited (HKEX) signed an MoU to deepen regional market connectivity, with the HKEX Bursa 
Malaysia Large Cap Index introduced as the first outcome of the partnership. The index tracks 60 leading listed companies across both markets, 
comprising 30 constituents from each market with index weightings of approximately 40% for Malaysia and 60% for Hong Kong. It provides investors with 
a new cross-market benchmark and supports future exchange traded fund (ETF) products. The partnership opens opportunities for collaboration in dual 
listings, shariah-compliant products and carbon markets thus enhancing Malaysia's international market profile.
                     Launching of HKEX Bursa Malaysia Large Cap 
Index to Strengthen Regional Market Connectivity
27 MAR
12 JAN
12 FEB
9 MAR
The SC established the Islamic Capital Market (ICM) Innovation Lab (FIKRALab) under the CMP as a structured co-creation and applied R&D platform 
to develop new ICM products and instruments. Anchored on Maqasid al-Shariah as well as guided by halal and toyyib principles, FIKRALab expands 
beyond fintech-centric ICM innovation by enabling deeper collaboration between the SC and industry in ideation, research, product design and pilot 
testing. Key features include Maqasid al-Shariah clinics to assess and enhance existing ICM products and to reinforce real economy linkages, 
beginning with a pilot project to develop an innovative instrument to unlock shariah-derived income within mixed activity groups. The first FIKRALab 
cohort is expected to commence in Q4 2026, focusing on new generation ICM products and services, Islamic social finance and social capital as well 
as sustainability and transition finance.
Bursa Malaysia officially implemented an equities trading data reclassification on 6 April 2026 to provide greater transparency and highly granular insights 
into retail and institutional fund flows. Under this new reporting framework, nominee accounts are now accurately categorised at the trade level based on 
end-beneficiary information rather than lumped into institutional data. On the other hand, foreign-owned institutions incorporated in Malaysia are refined 
by fund origin to better reflect true market activity. The structural update does not alter total daily trading values. This reclassification will provide investors 
and market participants with highly precise data to support informed investment decisions.

ecONOMIc OUtLOOK   2027
chapter 4   MONETARY AND FINANCIAL DEVELOPMENTS
155
Bursa Malaysia and the SC launched the MY Value Up Programme under the CMP to strengthen long-term corporate value creation among Malaysian 
public-listed companies (PLCs).The programme initially targets the top 88 PLCs, representing approximately 80% of Bursa Malaysia's total market 
capitalisation, encouraging companies to articulate their growth strategies, enhance forward-looking disclosures and strengthen engagement with 
investors. The programme supports stronger corporate valuations, enhances Malaysia's market attractiveness and positions Malaysian companies as 
globally competitive investment propositions.
8
MY Value Up Programme to Enhance Corporate Valuation and 
Market Attractiveness of Malaysian Public-listed Companies
9
Khazanah Nasional Berhad in collaboration with the SC, successfully priced Malaysia’s first  tokenised sukuk valued at RM100 million under the Sukuk 
Danum Programme. The pilot uses Distributed Ledger Technology (DLT) to create a secure and immutable digital representation of the sukuk, enabling 
round-the-clock access to relevant information. This also provides a controlled and credible environment to test the benefits of tokenisation which include 
enhanced efficiency and transparency, broader market participation and support a more vibrant capital market. The tokenisation initiative establishes the 
regulatory and market foundations for future tokenised issuances therefore reinforcing Malaysia’s leadership in ICM innovation.
Tokenised Sukuk Pilot to Advance Digital Innovation in the 
Islamic Capital Market
28 APR
Under the CMP, the Equity Guidelines was revised to strengthen the public market framework by enhancing the distinct roles of the Main Market and ACE 
Market. The framework raises the Main Market's profit admission requirements, financial reporting standards, flexibility in assessing operating cash flow 
and broader eligibility for infrastructure-type listings. Meanwhile, the ACE Market is reinforced as a sponsor-driven platform for growth companies with 
enhanced listing requirements, including a minimum of two years post-listing track record prior to transfer to the Main Market. The reforms strengthen 
listing quality, improve investor protection and support companies at different stages of growth.
10
Market Segmentation Review to Strengthen Public Market 
Framework
28 MAY
11
MITI and SC have launched a new Smart Manufacturing scheme under the New Industrial Master Plan 2030 (NIMP 2030) Strategic Co-Investment  
Fund (NIMP CoSIF) to accelerate automation and Industry 4.0 adoption among SMEs and mid-tier companies (MTCs1). Featuring an enhanced 2:1 
government-to-private matching ratio until 2030, the Government will contribute RM2 for every RM1 raised via ECF and peer-to-peer (P2P) platforms for 
businesses investing in robotics, artificial intelligence and digital solutions. Additionally, the co-investment ratio for other sectors will be temporarily raised 
from 1:2 to 1:1 until end-2027, providing immediate financing support to strengthen industrial resilience and scale up local enterprises.
NIMP CoSIF Expands Digitalisation Support with New 
Smart Manufacturing Scheme
21 JUL
The SC and the Hong Kong Securities and Futures Commission (SFC) signed an MoU to strengthen regulatory cooperation and capital market 
connectivity between Malaysia and Hong Kong, supporting the CMP's aspiration to position Malaysia as a gateway to regional investment opportunities. 
The MoU expands mutual recognition and cross-listing to include ETFs and real estate investment trusts (REITs), while facilitating a simplified dual-IPO 
listing framework with a single set of submission documents and enabling eligible Malaysian PLCs to apply for secondary listings in Hong Kong. The 
simplified dual IPO framework takes effect in September 2026. The collaboration broadens investment opportunities, expands access to capital and 
deepens cross-border participation.
12
SC and Hong Kong SFC Sign Landmark MoU to Strengthen 
Collaboration and Capital Market Connectivity
23 JUL
13
Bursa Malaysia and FTSE Russell introduced enhancements to the FBM KLCI and FTSE Bursa Malaysia Mid 70 Index (FBM70) to ensure Malaysia’s 
flagship benchmark remains representative, investible and aligned with the needs of domestic and international investors. The FBM KLCI will expand 
from 30 to 50 constituents, increasing coverage of Main Market capitalisation from approximately 60% to 70% and broadening representation across 
sectors. Based on simulations by FTSE Russell, the expanded index would include Technology, Energy and REITs for the first time, while moderating 
concentration in Financial Services. The FBM70 will be reduced to 50 constituents and renamed the FTSE Bursa Malaysia Mid Cap Index. The 
enhancements will be implemented in phases, starting on 21 December 2026 and will be fully implemented by 21 June 2027.
                   Enhancements to FBM KLCI and FBM70 Indices to 
Reflect a Broader, More Diversified Equity Market
20 AUG
20 APR
1 Mid-tier companies (MTC) is defined as companies with annual revenues between RM50 million to RM500 million in the manufacturing sector, and 
between RM20 million to RM500 million in the services and other sectors.

Islamic Banking 
and Capital Market 
Performance
Islamic finance demonstrates resilience and 
sustained growth
Islamic finance serves as a key pillar of 
Malaysia’s financial sector development, 
contributing to inclusive and sustainable 
economic growth. The role extends beyond 
financial sector deepening, to include support 
for SMEs development, household resilience, 
halal sector competitiveness and sustainable 
finance to reaffirm Malaysia’s position as a 
global Islamic finance hub. As at July 2026,  
Islamic financing expanded by 7.9% to 
RM1,076.4 billion, accounting for 44.4%4  
of total banking sector loans and financing. 
The growth was primarily driven by the 
household sector, which constituted 63.1% 
of total Islamic financing and expanded by 
7.1% to RM678.9 billion, while financing to 
the business sector was supported by strong 
growth in the real estate segment, which 
surged by 33.5% to RM54.5 billion. With a total 
capital ratio of 18.5% and a LCR of 145.1%, the 
Islamic banking sector remains resilient and is 
well positioned to sustain economic growth.  
The Government through BNM champions 
value-based finance to deliver meaningful 
socioeconomic outcomes through greater 
innovation and wider industry adoption. 
Key domestic initiatives include the Risk 
Sharing Fund (i-CITA) to promote risk-sharing 
financing and the expansion of social finance 
initiative beyond iTEKAD Entrepreneurship, 
to encompass iTEKAD Protection and iTEKAD 
Employment. These expanded initiatives aim 
to enhance access to affordable social takaful 
coverage and support vulnerable communities 
through capacity building, employability 
support, and financial management guidance. 
Internationally, Malaysia strengthened its 
leadership through industry stewardship, 
4 Islamic Banking figures not including Development Financial Institutions (DFIs)

economic outlook   2027
chapter 4   monetary and financial developments
157
In July 2026, Malaysia successfully issued a 
USD1.5 billion Global Sukuk, attracting strong 
investor demand that resulted in a 4.7 times 
oversubscription. This exceptional market 
reception enabled the issuance to achieve 
record-breaking low spread for a Malaysian 
sovereign dollar offering, underscoring solid 
global investor confidence in Malaysia’s 
macroeconomic fundamentals, prudent fiscal 
discipline and a well-developed ICM. This 
issuance further reinforces Malaysia’s access to 
international capital markets, while establishing 
a pricing benchmark for Malaysian entities, 
including government-linked companies and 
corporate borrowers seeking access to offshore 
funding.
As at end-July 2026, 80.7% out of  
1,111 companies listed on Bursa Malaysia  
were shariah-compliant public listed 
companies, with market capitalisation stood 
at RM1,411.9 billion, representing 65% of 
the total market capitalisation. Shariah-
compliant equities were concentrated mainly 
in the industrial products and services (30%), 
consumer products and services (21%) as 
well as technology (11%) sectors, providing 
investors with broad exposure across key 
segments of the economy.
The ICM also made further progress in 
innovation and digitalisation, unlocking 
new opportunities for market development, 
including developing shariah-based solutions. 
The Maqasid Al-Shariah Guidance Note on 
sukuk encouraged issuers and investors to 
look beyond technical shariah compliance and 
the broader socioeconomic impact of financing. 
These include creating economic value, 
supporting social well-being and promoting 
responsible investment outcomes. Additionally, 
the launch of Malaysia’s first tokenised sukuk 
marked a significant milestone in leveraging 
digital technology in the sukuk market, 
offering the potential to improve operational 
efficiency, market transparency and investor 
accessibility.
capacity building, shariah collaboration 
and targeted partnerships. This is further 
supported by the establishment of the Malaysia 
International Islamic Financial Centre (MIFC) 
Advisory Panel in June 2026. In the Islamic 
financial market, Malaysia is transitioning 
from the KLIBOR to the Malaysia Overnight 
Rate (MYOR) and Malaysia Islamic Overnight 
Rate (MYOR-i), with KLIBOR to be discontinued 
effective 1 January 2029. In addition, MYOR-i 
will become the benchmark for new Islamic 
financial products from the second half 
of 2027, enhancing the transparency and 
reliability while fostering a robust shariah-
compliant financial ecosystem.
The Islamic capital market (ICM) is a major 
part of Malaysia’s financial system, supported 
by a well-established shariah governance 
framework and a broad range of investment 
instruments. At end-2025, total ICM size 
reached RM2.75 trillion, accounting for 63.7% 
of Malaysia’s capital market, with sukuk and 
shariah-compliant equities forming its main 
components. The ICM continued to evolve 
through product innovation, digitalisation 
and purpose-driven Islamic finance, thereby 
reinforcing its role in mobilising capital for 
sustainable economic development and positive 
social outcomes.
Malaysia maintained its leadership in 
the global sukuk market, accounting for 
approximately 29% of total global sukuk 
outstanding as at end-July 2026. Domestic 
sukuk outstanding stood at RM1,490.5 billion, 
representing 63.3% of Malaysia’s total bonds 
and sukuk outstanding. Total sukuk issuance 
increased by 22.4% to RM175.5 billion 
compared with the corresponding period 
in 2025, reflecting sustained market-based 
fundraising activities. The local sukuk market 
continues to serve as a vital conduit for capital 
mobilisation anchored by broad investor 
participation and a well-developed Islamic 
finance ecosystem.

ecONOMIc OUtLOOK   2027
chapter 4   MONETARY AND FINANCIAL DEVELOPMENTS
158
Moving forward, Malaysia’s ICM is expected 
to remain as an important source of financing 
and investment, driven by sustained demand 
for shariah-compliant and sustainable 
investment solutions. Ongoing efforts to 
broaden the investor base, strengthen 
regional financial linkages and expand access 
to international capital markets through 
upcoming new sustainable sukuk frameworks, 
will further catalyse market expansion. Coupled 
with strategic initiatives under the CMP, these 
developments are set to enhance Malaysia’s 
global competitiveness and reinforce its 
leadership as an international ICM hub.
Conclusion
Malaysia’s financial ecosystem is expected to 
remain resilient and supportive of economic 
activity, with the continuous role played by the 
banking system and capital market in meeting 
the financing needs. Financial intermediation 
will continue to support households and 
businesses with the upcoming Financial Sector 
Blueprint 2027 – 2030 and initiatives under 
the Capital Market Masterplan 2026 – 2030 
providing the overarching policy direction. 
Collectively, continued reforms, broader 
investor participation and diverse funding 
sources will further strengthen the financial 
system.
